8. **Kinds of nothing.** Five kinds of nothing render differently because the person's next move differs for each: _not loaded_ (a skeleton in the row's shape), _empty_ (a quiet line with the escape hatch), _error_ (a red-edged row carrying the reason in words with the daemon's own message under it), _not checked_ (a dotted badge), and _unknown, still computing_ (a badge with a clock glyph). A renderer that collapses two of these into one is wrong. **An empty surface names one next act.** The empty transcript reads `No messages yet. Say what you are after.` and offers the first act, naming what the session is bound to; a list with nothing in it says so and names the one control that fills it; a search that matched nothing says what it searched for and keeps its group headings drawn. **A subject that goes takes its surface with it, and says so.** A pane whose subject is gone closes itself, and a one-line note between the header and the surface says which pane closed and why — `Review closed — this session has nothing to review.`, `Terminal closed — this session has no shell.`, `Preview closed — this session has no page or dev server to show.` or `Sidekicks closed — this session dispatched nothing.`; on a project session the pane's chip stays where it stands, grayed with its reason, and opening any pane or leaving the session answers the note. A session whose remembered pane has lost its subject opens no pane and says nothing, because nothing closed under the reader. **A region that fails says so where it stands.** One line naming that region, `<Region> stopped rendering. Retry`, with `Retry` at its end, in the slot a pane uses to say why it closed, while the transcript and every other region keep updating; a press draws the region again. A read the daemon does not answer keeps the rows a list already had rather than emptying it, says in amber on the list's own header that it could not be refreshed, and counts no figure it could not read until the next answer lands. **Failure is the only red.** A failed call is marked; a call that succeeded carries no tick, no "done" and no green, because success is shown by absence. Failure is read from the run's own durable record, so a rebuild after a restart gives the same answer and a failed run still reads failed in the morning, and a refusal a person made is not drawn in the waiting color — it is a row whose reason says the person declined.
9. **The refusal grammar.** Controls are offered; refusals are rendered, in one of three shapes — **inline** on the control that was pressed (the reason in words, the daemon's own message under it, the next move when one exists), as a **card** in the transcript when the refusal changes history, or as a **banner** across the session screen when it changes what the whole session can do. A refusal never hides the control that produced it and never re-derives the daemon's rule. An inline refusal is visible words on the surface that asked, at that surface's own width, on one line under the control that was pressed; it stays until the next act and is never carried in a hover title alone. A whole dimmed list carries its reason once in its header, in amber, and again on every row, and a control grayed by one of the named exceptions §Rules every console surface obeys lists carries its reason in its own hover title. A banner sits directly under the session header, above the pane note and the surface, carries its own dismissal, blocks nothing and refuses nothing, and enters no attention count; a dismissal is for that occurrence alone, so the same condition arising again draws it again. One such banner is the provider that ignored part of its own configuration file: it names the provider, the file and the line or key it ignored, quoted from the provider's own report ([Spec-004 §Required Behavior](./004-provider-driver-contract-and-capabilities.md#required-behavior)), with `Open the file` and the dismiss as its two presses; the dismiss holds for that start of the provider, coming back to the session does not bring it back, and a later start still on defaults draws it again. **One Escape order covers the session screen.** Escape dismisses in priority order — the rewind menu or an open overlay first, the plan reader, the quick look, the command palette or the find box (which also clears its highlights), then typed text in Preview's address field when it has focus (discarded, the page address returns), then a pressed Preview tool, then full width, then the More menu, the fork popover, the worktree popover (stepping its sub-mode back to its list), the draft's command list, then an in-progress edit of a pending row (its text restored), then the editor on a sent message (closed at once when its words are unchanged, and otherwise asking first under the box), the Review base menu, and Review itself when focus is inside it. Escape pressed while the terminal body has focus is the shell's and this ladder does not run. When nothing on this ladder is open, the message box has focus and a turn is running, Escape does what Interrupt does; pressed again it interrupts again and reaches no child; with the draft empty and nothing running, a second Escape opens the rewind menu, and an Escape that interrupted a turn never counts as the first press of that pair; in a child's view with focus it acts on that child. A destructive act is confirmed in place, inline where it was started, saying in plain words what it removes and whether it can be undone before it offers the act; it is never a dialog and never switchable off. Replacing unsent words in the draft is one such act: an act that writes into the draft while it holds unsent text, starting a workflow from the palette for one, asks directly above the draft that it will replace them.

**Layout grammar.** The app frame is three columns: the icon rail at its own derived width, on macOS widened to clear the window buttons while they show, the sessions track, and the screen view taking the rest, which on the session screen holds one or more session views side by side (§The session screen). The middle track is nothing wide — a zero-width track and not a stub — while it is closed, and while it holds the all-sessions list or the notification list it takes the width the person has dragged it to, and until they have, the width a session row's own furniture needs at the current text size. The grid itself animates, so the screen view slides over rather than being covered, and the track pins its children to its full width while it animates so nothing reflows mid-motion. A desktop window with no kept place opens centered at a size taken from the display's own work area (§Main Process Responsibilities, window management) and never shrinks below its floor: across, the rail, the sessions track and one session view's floor — the transcript's own floor and one pane's floor — summed; down, the shortest window in which the header, the transcript and the composer still stack; both recomputed from those parts whenever the text size changes. The one width at which the grid changes shape is the fold to one column, and the content sets that point — the transcript's own floor against what the track and an open pane need at the current text size — never a device class, a build or a list of devices. Because a desktop window never shrinks below its floor, a desktop window never folds; the fold is reached where the same front end runs with no window floor, on Remote Control's phone and browser surfaces. Folded, every screen shows one level at a time: Sessions the list, then the session, then a pane; Sidekicks the list, then the editor; Skills the list, then the skill; Workflows the tab lists, then the canvas or the run page, then the step panel; Settings the page list, then the page. A detail level's header begins with one back control naming the level above it — `‹ Sessions`, `‹ Sidekicks`, `‹ Skills`, `‹ Workflows`, `‹ Settings`, and `‹ <session title>` over a pane. Both levels stay built and only one is shown, the other inert, so moving between them shows and hides rather than rebuilding: a streaming turn keeps streaming, the panes keep what they had open and nothing reloads. A window that folds lands on its deepest open level, a pane when one is open, and widening it again draws the levels side by side with nothing closed; on the session level a pane's header chip, while its pane is open but hidden, shows that pane's level and never closes it. The rail stays a rail: it is drawn beside each screen's list level and is absent on a detail level, where the back control leads to it, and it never becomes a bottom bar. The platform's own Back — a phone's back gesture or a browser's back — dismisses the topmost surface in the order Escape does and stops short of the work: it never interrupts a turn, clears a draft or opens the rewind menu. Where the pointer is coarse every press target grows to a fingertip, what hover reveals stands at rest, and every act a drag performs has a press that does the same. Every menu, list and card a folded screen opens keeps clear of the device's own top and bottom insets and caps its height at a share of the window rather than at a figure, so nothing opens under a home bar or past the top of the screen and every row of it can be reached; where the window has no inset the padding is nothing and the layout is unchanged. The chords belong to the screen, not to the layout: a keyboard attached to a narrow window reaches every control the same chord reaches side by side, and the one-column layout neither takes a chord away nor mints one of its own.

A session view's pane layout is the session column and a row of panes beside it. The row holds any number of main panes side by side — the inspector, the review surface, the agents pane, the Preview pane and the file view, each kind at most once, in any combination and with no cap on how many — and the terminal, which sits above or below the main panes and is as wide as they are together. The panes form one block beside the conversation, to its right by default and to its left once the person moves it there, and the side the block sits on is kept per session. Opening a pane adds it to the row beside the focused pane, scrolls it into view and replaces nothing. Panes reorder by dragging a pane's header and by the Keyboard page's `Move pane left` and `Move pane right` (`⌥⇧←`, `⌥⇧→`), the terminal moving above or below by `Move terminal up` and `Move terminal down` (`⌥⇧↑`, `⌥⇧↓`), each move announced in the live region: `Moved the <pane> pane to position N of M.`, or `The <pane> pane was not moved.` where it stays put, a drop back in the same place included. Every pane keeps its own floor and the session column keeps the conversation's floor; when the next pane does not fit, the row scrolls sideways — nothing is refused and nothing folds — and a pane just opened scrolls into view. A pane's own scroller keeps its vertical scroll to itself and hands a sideways swipe to the row, so a swipe over any pane moves the row. `Move pane left` or `Move pane right` pressed past the row's end that faces the conversation moves the whole block of panes to the other side of it, and pressed past the end at the window's edge moves nothing. A pane moved into its own window (§Detached panes) leaves the row and is drawn in that window instead. Inside the session track the column is the session header, then the surface, then the docked composer. Every pane's header names what the pane shows and carries its own close; the focused pane carries no mark of its own, and where the keyboard focus sits is drawn only by the one focus mark on the focused control, never by a hue. The inspector is a pane kind, not a fixed third column: `⌘I` opens it beside the focused pane and scrolls it into view, and closing it gives focus back to the pane focused before.

The command palette opens over the session column on a light scrim, with its search field focused and empty and the transcript and the composer still running under it; Enter runs the selected row and closes it — except `Copy link` and `Copy conversation`, whose row reads `Copied`, or `Could not copy`, for the one transient duration before the palette closes — 1,200 ms, the one fixed duration on which `Opening…`, `Copied` and the copy flash all clear — and Escape, a press on the scrim and the chord that opened it all close it without running anything. It has categories — each unbroken run of rows under a small heading reading that run's own category, the best-placed row's category leading and the categories not reshuffling between keystrokes, the headings labels the arrows walk straight past so the list stays one flat set of rows — recents, where an act run lately carries the `Recent` mark and an empty field lists those acts first, newest first and up to eight; the scope row reading `Acting on <label>` above the field; the footer legend `Enter to run · Escape to close` with the result count beside it; every match listed, with only the rows in view drawn; and one matcher shared with settings search, whose matched letters are marked inside a row's name by weight and brightness on the hue the name already wears rather than by a color of their own. That one matcher is the Settings screen's search as well: the field above the page list searches three strings per page — the page's own name, its entry's label, and the words that page declares itself findable by — the best of the three winning and ties falling in the list's own order, and it searches the controls inside every page, ranking a hit on the control's own label above the heading it sits under and that above the control's one-line hint, with ties again in page order. Each hit reads the control's label over the page and heading it lives on, the arrows move the highlight, Enter opens the page and lands on the control, and a term nothing matches draws one line naming what was searched for. The list holds every act the open session has, each under the name the screen already gives it — the pane chips, the session's own menu rows, and the composer's level, mode, model, effort and speed controls, a control's row opening that control's own list rather than standing in for it. Six acts live only here: `Copy conversation`, which puts the whole open session on the clipboard in the form the screen reads; `Clear the terminal`, which empties the active shell's own view and its scrollback on screen without sending anything to the shell, so it is safe while a command is running, reaches the active tab alone, is absent from the list while the session has no shell, and adds no control to the pane header or the tab strip; `Fold every run` and `Unfold every run`, which close and open every run group in the session at once; `Search all sessions`, which opens the box that searches every session (§The session screen); and `Run a workflow`, which puts `/workflow run ` in an empty message box and, over an unsent message, first asks `Replace the unsent message with /workflow run?` with `Cancel` and `Replace`. `Copy conversation` and a message's own copy are the only ways a transcript leaves this screen: nothing anywhere exports one to a file, prints one, or reads one aloud. An act this session cannot take is absent from the list rather than grayed; an act that exists but is closed right now stays listed with the one sentence its owner wrote for why, and refuses in place when it is pressed: `Open in new window` with no side pane open reads `Open a side pane first.`

Iconography is a single-stroke set — Tabler glyphs, and faces of our own drawn to the same geometry where Tabler has none that fits, in one collection compiled at build time — drawn on the 14 px glyph grid the icon token fixes, at the 2.25 px stroke weight the icon-stroke token fixes, with round caps and joins; the rail's glyphs are the one place they run larger. The robot is the one generic mark for an agent — drawn on the agent's hue it is the agent's swatch, the only avatar on the console — and an agent definition may carry its own glyph from the same set as that agent's picture; the mode control draws a hammer or a checklist. Tool glyphs carry a hue family of their own, apart from the agent wheel: each verb's glyph takes one hue aliased from the theme's own code and terminal colors (one `--meridian-` token per verb — read, edit and write, command, thinking, search and workflow), never a color minted for it, and a verb with no hue of its own takes the generic plug glyph at the muted hue ([Spec-011 §Flow Row Kinds](011-transcript-and-reasoning.md#flow-row-kinds)). The one user carries no mark, and a system message carries no glyph. Borders are the hairline token and there are no heavy rules; the radii are the radius scale's three steps, 3, 6 and 10 px, and nothing is drawn at another radius; elevation is the composer card's glass surface rather than a stack of shadows; each surface carries one filled control and no more; and hover is one derived color that reveals only affordances whose space is already reserved.

#### Rules every console surface obeys

- **Absent, not disabled.** A control whose capability the bound driver does not declare is not rendered — a disabled control would assert the capability exists and is momentarily unavailable, which would be false. A control the daemon may refuse is rendered and its refusal is shown; eligibility is never projected by the renderer — the `I-002-18` fail-closed-projection discipline, generalized. The rule reaches the menus, the composer's command list and its controls, the pickers, the session strip and the rail, and it has three named sets of exceptions, each grayed in place with its reason in its own hover title and each answering no press. **Three single controls** whose subject is a choice the person can change beside them: the review surface's approve verdict on a pull request the user authored, the composer's output-speed control on a session whose chosen model has no fast output, and the `browser` pane's back and forward, whose subject the person changes by following a link. **The session header's three pane chips**, which stand at every moment on either session shape and gray when they cannot apply, because the header is the one place a person's eye returns to and a chip that comes and goes cannot be learned. **And two rows that stand inside a list their neighbors explain**: the shell tab menu's close-others and close-to-the-right where there is no such tab, and the worktree create form's base list on a branch another worktree already has checked out.
- **Offer, then render the refusal.** The renderer never pre-denies; it calls, and renders the typed refusal code with the daemon's message text and the person's next move.
- **One accountant.** Every cost figure comes from the committed-spend read surface ([Spec-014 §Cost Figure Display Consistency](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#cost-figure-display-consistency)); the renderer never sums visible rows, never labels a figure a lower bound, never totals across sessions. A figure the daemon has not supplied is not counted in the renderer at all, and a gate whose input is missing refuses rather than guessing.
- **Write-only credentials.** A credential-bearing input appears on no reply and is never echoed ([ADR-026 §Decision](../decisions/026-provider-credential-custody-posture.md#decision); the Provider Accounts and MCP Servers sketches below).
- **Fail-closed projection.** An unknown enum member renders as the explicit unrecognized row or badge, never as a guess.
- **Wire figures are verbatim; wire quantities are formatted from the exact value.** Two classes, decided by the value's kind and never by the surface. **Byte-for-byte strings** — roots and paths, SHAs and digests, identifiers, versions, model names, commands, diffs, terminal output, provider-supplied labels and message text, and any cost or count the wire supplies as a string — render exactly as received, in mono, never re-parsed. A wire **name** is not a figure and is not in this class: an error code, a refusal reason, a status, an event kind, a tool name, a policy action or a node kind reaches the eye as words, under item 6 of §Meridian, the design language. **Formatted quantities** — a numeric duration, byte count, token count, percentage, rate, or timestamp, and a cost the wire supplies as a number beside its currency — render through `Intl` (`DurationFormat`, `NumberFormat` in unit, currency, and compact styles, `RelativeTimeFormat`), in mono, with every clock figure and date following the machine's own settings and never the app's language (§Meridian, the design language item 4), from the exact wire value with no client-side arithmetic beyond the format itself — the one exception being byte quantities, which the console's single shared formatter scales by powers of 1024 and labels from `B / KiB / MiB / GiB / TiB` (`Intl` has no binary unit), the scaled number still passing through `NumberFormat`, `apps/desktop/AGENTS.md` §Chokepoints holding that formatter as the only site that scales, with the `wire-figure-formatting` runtime tripwire firing on a figure rendered outside the two classes: a dollar figure as `$` and the amount in US dollars, two decimals above $0.50 and four at $0.50 or below, rounded half up from the stored amount and carrying no mark of an estimate, bytes in binary units, durations digital at one minute and above and `1.2 s`-style below, relative times always paired with the absolute timestamp on hover, and compact numbers only where the exact figure is one hover away — the exact wire value is exposed in the element's `title` and in the inspector, so no formatted figure hides the number the daemon sent. A model's context window is the figure its provider reports — Claude Code's at the session's creation and after every model change, Codex's from its model catalog corrected by the first turn's own report — and never an assumed one: it reads beside the model's name and as `of <window>`, and a row whose window has not arrived shows the model with no window, never a placeholder figure.
- **No interval polling.** Reads happen on subscribe, on window focus, on reconnect, and on the terminal events the owning spec names — through one refresh scheduler firing at `min(lastEvent + delay, firstEvent + maxWait)`, serialized, so a trailing debounce cannot starve under a stream.
- **Reserved, not stubbed.** A capability-gated feature the corpus reserves gets no surface, no placeholder, and no setting until the capability exists.
- **No control ships inert.** Every control the console draws is real and must work, and a fix lands on every surface, session and state that shares the part it repaired, confirmed on each rather than assumed.
- **One way in, one way out.** A menu or pop-over anchors to the control that opened it, flips to the other side when it would leave the window and clamps to the window's edge so every row of it stays reachable by the pointer, and closes on an outside press and on Escape and on nothing else, the draft's command and mention lists alone also closing when the draft loses focus or a card opens over the box. Closing a pop-up or a pane puts focus back on the control that opened it, however it closed — Escape, its own `×`, a press outside it or the chord that opened it — with three exceptions: a surface closed by a session switch leaves focus where the switch put it, a surface opened from a staged chip hands focus to the message box, and the composer's own surfaces — the model, effort, speed, mode, level and tool-server lists, the folded-chips menu, the file picker and the draft's command and mention lists — hand the caret back to the message box when they close. Every menu and list answers the same keys — the arrows move one cursor, home and end jump to the first and last row, Enter presses the row the cursor is on, Escape closes, rows that cannot act are skipped, the first row takes the cursor when the list opens, and no letter key moves the cursor, because the field keeps the caret. The cursor's row is scrolled into view on every move, and a list that shrinks as a query is typed keeps its cursor on a row that still exists. Transient statuses — an opening line, a copied line, the copy flash — all clear on one fixed duration. A surface waiting on a read says it is loading only after 800 ms and only while nothing of it is drawn, so a surface that was already open never flashes a loading line, and a session whose read fails reads `Could not load this session` with `Try again`.
- **An input method keeps its Enter.** On every field that commits on Enter — the message box, the worktree name, the reviewers field, a child's steer box, the command palette, the box that searches every session and the find box — an Enter pressed while an input method is still choosing a character is left to the field, so the character is confirmed and nothing is sent, created or run.
- **Every control has a name.** Every control carries an accessible name whether or not it draws a visible label: the message box's is `Message`, and a control whose face is only a glyph carries its tooltip's words as its name.
- **Nothing forces a state by hand.** No switch, flag or hidden control anywhere in the console stages an attachment, opens a picker, slows a list or forces a state. Fixture code and data are compiled only into a fixture-capable build and the console's own developer tools only into a development build, never into a release build (§Fixtures), so nothing in a shipped console can make scripted data look like real work.

#### The surface set

The app has no launch or splash screen: nothing — no picture, no logo, no window of its own — comes before the first console window, and the one thing drawn over that window at start is the cover this paragraph describes. Before any surface draws, opening the app draws one quiet cover over the window until the background service has answered, then fades it away and never draws it again while the app runs. The cover carries the working indicator and one line saying what is happening — `Starting the background service…` when the app started the service itself, `Connecting to the background service…` when it found one already running — and nothing behind it is drawn half-painted. A service that cannot be reached at app start draws one quiet card in the middle of the window in place of an empty console, `The background service is not answering.` with `Retry`, and nothing else; once the console has drawn, the same fact belongs to the working line, so the card never appears over a session a person was reading.

##### The icon rail

Five destinations and two controls. The destinations are Sessions, Sidekicks, Skills, Workflows and Settings, in that order ([ADR-029 §Decision](../decisions/029-five-rail-destinations.md#decision)), each a glyph with a text label on hover and in its accessible name, the Sidekicks glyph being the robot the composer's agents badge draws, the one generic mark that ever stands for an agent, the Skills glyph an open book, a stroked outline at the rail's weight in the rail's glyph box and never the robot, and the Workflows glyph the `workflow` pane glyph; the rail runs the first four, then a spacer, then the attention control and the color-scheme control, then Settings. It is a bare sunken column of square hit areas, each as wide as its glyph plus the press target a pointer or a fingertip needs around it and growing with the root text size, with no cards, no chips and no panels. The destination the person is on draws an accent spine at the spine token's own weight, in the rail's own padding and outside the button. Rail glyphs are the one place a glyph runs larger than the console's own size, and they come from the console's own collection. Every rail button carries a hover title naming its destination; an icon-only control carries one string that is both its hover title and its spoken name, with no second tooltip surface and no delay before it shows. The Sessions button's hint ends in the chord that opens the list, read from that act's own row on the Keyboard page each time it is drawn, so a rebinding changes the hint and the suffix is absent while the act holds no chord. The Sessions button toggles the sessions track and reads as expanded to assistive technology while that track holds the all-sessions list, the same way the attention control reads while the notification list is open.

**Each destination owns its own addresses**, so a deep link and a press on the rail are one act and the back control works. Sessions addresses the all-sessions list and one session's screen. Sidekicks addresses the definition library, one definition, a new definition, and `Browse plugins` at `#/sidekicks/plugins`; that screen is [Spec-026](./026-agent-definitions-and-peer-invocation.md)'s and this spec restates none of it. Skills addresses the list, the new-skill form, a folder opened at its entry file, and that folder with one other file open; that screen is [Spec-029 §The destination, the rail and the addresses](./029-skills.md#the-destination-the-rail-and-the-addresses)'s. Workflows addresses each of its three tabs, in the grammar Settings uses — the Workflows tab at `#/workflows`, the Runs tab at `#/workflows/runs` with one run being watched at `#/workflows/runs/<runId>`, and the Builder at `#/workflows/builder` on a new workflow and `#/workflows/builder/<definitionId>` on a saved one, with no draft in the address: the daemon holds one unsaved draft per saved workflow and one for a new workflow, so a reload reads it back by the address's own workflow — as in-page tabs of the destination [Spec-015 §Visual Workflow Builder](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md#visual-workflow-builder) owns; the Builder is a tab and never a pane beside a session, an unknown run id opens the run list with one line saying the run is not here, and an unknown or deleted definition id opens the Workflows tab with one line, `That workflow is not here.` Settings addresses the screen, one page, and a page with a selection; `Devices` is the last of its pages, after Runtime, and never a destination of its own. The command palette lists all five, with the color-scheme and notifications controls and the two searches, in one category of its own headed `Console`, so every destination is reachable from the keyboard as well as from the rail.

The attention control toggles the notification list and reads as expanded while that list is open. It carries the console's one badge: an amber pip, hidden from assistive technology with the count carried in the button's accessible name, absent rather than a zero when nothing waits, taken from the daemon's attention projection and never counted in the renderer. It counts the sessions and workflow runs waiting on a person right now. The color-scheme control cycles follow-the-system, dark, light. Settings carries one small quiet dot while an update is staged and waiting for the restart — no count, because there is only ever one update, and not the attention color, because nothing is needed from the person — hidden from assistive technology with the fact in the button's accessible name and gone when the update is applied; what the update is and the press that applies it live on the General settings page. That dot is the one exception to the one-badge rule.

Nothing else is in the rail: no session list inside it, no status text, no branding, no avatar, no second settings entry, and no mark on any other button. The Sessions button carries no mark of its own — a session row's status dot is that row's state, not a notification. What belongs to one session — its worktree and repository acts, Review, the approvals card and the agents pane — is reached inside that session, from its header, its pane layout and its composer; the rail names the top-level destinations and leaves no surface out. Assistive technology reads the rail as the console's sections.

##### The all-sessions list

One row per session in the sessions track, grouped by shape, and inside a project by the person's session groups, and by nothing else: chats under a `Chats` group header with its count above every project group, the projects under a `Projects` group header, each project one group under a foldable header carrying the daemon's running / waiting / done roll-up, and an archived group at the foot of the scroll under its own header with its count, `Archived (12)`, folded by default and opening the way a project header opens its list. Inside a project its session groups sit at the top of its list as foldable rows, above the project's sessions in no group, each reading its name and its count — `<name> · <count>` — and folding and unfolding on its own row as a project header does, a folded one carrying the same rolled-up status dot a project header has. A session sits in one session group at most and a session group belongs to one project; a pinned session stays pinned at the top of its session group; an archived session keeps its session group, so search still files it there, while its row goes to the archived group as any session's does; and a session group with no live session left hides from the list on its own and comes back when one of its sessions is unarchived. Dragging a session onto a session group's row moves it in and dragging it into the project's loose sessions takes it out; a session group's row renames in place on a double-click, and its actions list is `Rename group…`, `New session in group` and `Ungroup`, which sends its sessions back loose and archives or closes nothing. The list has no multi-select: many sessions move at once by asking an agent. Dragging a chat's row onto a project group opens the conversion to a project in the composer card with that project's path filled in — the third way in, beside `Attach a repo` on the composer's strip and `Convert to project…` in the row's actions ([Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)). Grouping is the only discriminator — no badge and no second row layout — and a row names neither the provider that runs it nor the machine it runs on; what a session is bound to is read in the inspector. A session the person typed in a terminal has no row: the agents reach it and the person talks to it in that terminal ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)). Each attached project is one group and its rows are that project's own sessions: no group stands for two checkouts and no tally is merged across them. The per-project tally, and the chats header's own count, are the daemon's and are never counted in the renderer. With two or more machines registered to the person, the list's head carries the machine in view, its name and a chevron, opening a list of every machine that marks `This machine` and `Not reachable`; picking one changes every screen to that machine, and the session this device last had in view there opens, or the list's first row on a machine it has never shown. A machine that is not reachable can still be picked: its list shows as it was last read, its head saying it could not be refreshed, and a row pressed there opens its session as last read. With one machine the switch is absent.

A row is a button of two stacked lines, the second padded to align under the title rather than under the dot: the status dot and the title on the first — with one bell-off mark after the title on a muted session, sized from the row's own text as the dot is, titled `Notifications muted`, the title truncating before the mark does and the row's spoken name ending `, notifications muted` — then the branch in mono on a project session or the document count on a chat, with the age right-aligned. Neither of those second-line facts sits behind a switch and neither costs a repository read at draw time. A third reading takes that same slot while the session is trading messages with another session and nobody is typing in either one: `talking to builder · 14` — the other session's name and how many messages the two have traded since the person last wrote in either — on **both** sessions' rows, with the age still at its right. It carries no cost figure, because money on a session's surface lives only in the inspector's cost section; it is never counted as something waiting on the person, so it never reaches the attention control or the bell; and it goes when the exchange ends, the row returning to its branch or its document count. An age reads `now` under a minute and then one count in one unit with no "ago", advancing on the console's one shared beat. A status dot has five states the daemon derives: running in the accent, waiting on the person in amber, done a hollow ring, failed a red mark, idle a faint gray circle. Its size derives from the row's own text and is a step larger on a project roll-up; each carries a hover title naming its state in one word — `Working`, `Waiting on you`, `Done`, `Failed`, `Idle` — which is also how the row's spoken name ends; and the running and waiting dots step through an opacity ramp rather than blinking. A session that finished while the person was away draws its done dot filled until they open it and hollow from the moment they do, read from the one seen-or-unseen fact the daemon keeps for the notification list, so the row and that list can never disagree. A running or waiting claim the daemon has stopped renewing stops reading as live once it is older than a bound derived from how often the daemon reports: the row falls back to idle and its timer stands still where it froze. A failed reading is never aged out, because it is a fact about the run rather than a claim about right now.

An untitled session's row reads `New chat` or `New session`, faint and italic, never an invented title and never a goal: a goal is a command sent inside a session, and nothing names, labels or groups a session by one. A session names itself from its first completed exchange — a short throwaway pass reads that exchange and writes a few words, falling back to the first words of the first message where the pass returns nothing — and keeps that name until it is renamed. The name is written only while the session is still unnamed, and a rename made while the pass is running is re-read before anything is written, so a name the person typed always wins; naming a session renames neither its branch nor its worktree. Renaming is an edit in place from the row or the header, saving on Enter and canceling on Escape, an empty name restoring the first-message title, and the row and the header updating together. A row the person minted keeps its place until they archive or close it: an emptied draft, an unnamed session or a session that never sent anything removes nothing, because the list loses a row only on an act of theirs.

There is one new-session control, dashed at rest and solid on hover or while open, and its picker is the whole of the choice a session is started from: `Just chat`, with the sub-line `no repo`, then a `Recent repos` heading over the recent repositories, then an `Elsewhere` heading over `Open folder…` and `Clone repository…`. The track's top row holds the search field with its `⌘K` chip, read from the live binding, and the close control, and assistive technology reads the track as `Sessions`. It offers no machine, because a new session runs on the machine in view, which the machine switch already chose. On the machine itself `Open folder…` opens the platform's own folder chooser and attaches the folder picked; on another device it lists the machine's own folders in place, starting at the home folder of the account the service runs as, so the device acts only on what the service listed, as the desktop acts only on what the platform's chooser returned. `Clone repository…` passes the address or path typed to git, started with an argument list and never through a shell, so git's own transport rules decide, and clones into the folder `Clone new repositories into` names, the clone running inside the composer card where a worktree's setup runs; a destination that exists and is not empty is refused in place before anything is fetched. It offers no agent, no provider and no model either: a session has one agent, no step anywhere attaches a second one to it, and a saved definition is named in the composer's command list or on a workflow node — the only two places one is chosen — rather than picked when the session is started. Minting a session prepends its row with a faint italic placeholder title and the right second line, an age of now, bumps its group's own count or forces that project's list open, closes the picker, switches the screen to the session and puts the caret in the draft. Opening the sessions track focuses the search field without scrolling the rows, and closing the track closes the picker with it and returns focus to the rail's Sessions button. The repository a session works in is chosen there and only there; a chat that later needs one gains it through the inspector's attach action. Search filters the list in place across every group and never hides a group header, matching a row on its title, its second line and its folder path, ranked so titles that start with the query come first, then titles that contain it, then second lines and paths, with a clear control at the field's right end that restores the whole list and returns focus to the field. A folded project holding a match unfolds while the field has text and folds again when it is cleared, and only the projects the search itself unfolded fold back. A group whose own name matches — a project or a session group — keeps every session under it. Searching the text inside sessions is a different surface — the box the palette opens — and the track's own field stays a search over the rows in the list.

A press on a row switches the focused view for real — title, chips, counts, transcript, composer state and panes — or, where the session is already on screen in a view, brings that view's window forward and focuses the view. The focused view's session is marked by one selected wash on its row and by that row reading as the current one to assistive technology; every other session on screen, in this window or another, carries a thin mark at its row's leading edge in the same accent, read as `On screen in another view`; nothing else marks either, and a hovered row takes the one derived hover color and no second mark. A right-click — or, where the pointer is coarse, a touch held still — raises that row's own actions at the pointer: the same list the session header's menu holds, built from one list so the two can never disagree, acting on the row it was raised on whichever session is on screen, and switching nothing, because switching stays the row's own press. Where the pointer is coarse the row draws its menu control at rest rather than on hover; editable text is left to the platform; and no gesture over a row archives, closes or deletes anything — a swipe across a row scrolls the list. While a row is hovered or holds focus its age is replaced in place by one word — `Archive`, or `Unarchive` on an archived row — in the slot the age holds, so no row changes height or width, and that is the row's only hover action. An archived row is the ordinary row at a dimmed ink; unarchiving returns it to its own project's list and drops the group's count, and the group and its count go when the last row leaves it. A closed session's row sits in the same group at the same dimmed ink and reads `Closed` in the age's slot, a state and not an action, with no action of its own; it stays readable and searchable until `Delete old data` removes it. A session can be pinned to the top of its own group from its row's actions and unpinned the same way: one pin and no second tier, pinned rows sitting above the daemon's order inside their group in the order they were pinned.

The list draws only the rows in view, plus six either side, once it holds 200 sessions or more, and stays flat below that; the threshold is read off the row count and never off a screen size. A group draws its first rows, as many as the list's visible height holds at the current text size, and puts the rest behind one row reading `Show N more`, carrying the roll-up dot of the rows it hides; no session on screen in any view is behind that cut or hidden by a fold — its row is still drawn under a folded project's header at that project's own indent. What a project header remembers is the folds and not the opens, so a project attached tomorrow arrives open and a fold survives a relaunch. When the order actually changes every surviving row glides from where it was to where it now is, a new row fades in and a removed row simply goes; a change of height alone starts no motion and the first fill never animates. Holding the platform's own modifier stamps a small key-cap chip on the first nine rows in the slot the age holds and those chords open them as a row press does, and a press on a row with that modifier held opens it beside the focused view; the chips are there only while the key is held, never at rest, and never while a menu or an overlay owns the keyboard.

Long names truncate and carry the full value in a hover title, and nothing in the list overflows the track's own width. The list fades at whichever end is hiding rows, each end gated on its own, over a ramp derived from a row's own height. Where two attached projects share a name each header grows a dim parent-folder prefix, enough of it to tell them apart, with the full path in its hover title; a prefix never truncates and wraps above the name where the pair will not fit. A newly attached project's header appears at once; a project the daemon cannot attach takes its header away again and says why in one line in the picker that asked — a folder that is not a git repository reads `Could not attach: not a git repository`, because a project is a repository ([Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)) — with nothing else in the list redrawn. A refresh the daemon does not answer keeps every row the list already had, says in amber on the list's own header that it could not be refreshed, and counts no figure it could not read until the next answer lands. With no sessions at all the list reads `No sessions yet. Press New session to start with a chat or a project.` and names nothing else; with a query that matches nothing it reads `No sessions match "<query>".` and keeps its group headers drawn. Before the list's first read has answered, the top row and `New session` draw at once and the list reads `Loading…` after the one short delay. The list carries a foot line naming the keys it answers, in words: `↑↓ to move · Enter to open · Esc to close`.

##### The session screen

**Session views.** Every window is one kind and none is the main one: the rail, the sessions list and one or more session views side by side, each view one session's header, transcript, composer and its own row of panes under the pane-layout rule below. The views share the window's width, each at least the conversation's floor plus one pane's floor, its own row of panes scrolling sideways inside it past that, and when the window cannot hold every view at its floor the row of views scrolls sideways and keeps the focused view in sight; a new window opens with one view. A session shows in one view on this device at a time, and each destination in one window; asking for either again, by any route, brings its place forward and focuses it, and nothing draws a second copy. A plain row press acts on the focused view (§The all-sessions list); a press on a row with the platform modifier held, and `Open beside` in the row's menu, add a view with that session beside the focused one and focus it; `Open in new window`, in the row's menu and the session's menu, puts the session in a window of its own, taken out of the view it was in. `Open beside` is absent from the menu of a session already on screen, and `Open in new window` from the menu of a session that is its window's only view. A view drags by its title, the session's name in its header: dragged along its own window's row it swaps places with the views it passes; dragged over another window it docks into that window's row of views at the place that window's indicator marks; and dragged past every window it tears off live, a window holding the view following the pointer, which a release over the desktop leaves there as a new window and a release over another window docks into that window's row. A window a drag leaves with no view closes. One view per window holds focus: a press anywhere inside a view focuses it, the Keyboard page's `Focus session on the left` and `Focus session on the right` move it, and every session chord, pane chord, the palette and find act on the focused view; moving focus between views changes nothing in either. While a window holds more than one view, each view's header carries a close control whose hover title and spoken name read `Close session view`, and the platform modifier with `W` closes the focused view, or the window when the view is its only one; closing a view or a window stops nothing, and its sessions keep running. `New window` — the platform modifier with shift and `N`, the File menu's row and the palette's — opens a window whose one view is empty, with the sessions flyout open and the new-session picker under it, and Escape there leaves the window on the sessions list. A session view that holds no session — the one a new window opens with, and the one beside the sessions list after Escape there, on a first run and at a safe start — draws the app's logo mark alone in the middle of its body: the app icon's outline, stroked in the hairline color `edge` at a size taken from the console's own scale, hidden from assistive technology, answering no press and carrying no words. It goes the moment a session opens in the view, so it is never drawn under or over anything to read; nothing else carries it — not an empty transcript, a list with nothing in it, a pane or the cover — and the one-column layout, which draws no empty view, draws no mark. Wherever this section speaks of the session on screen, the open session or the session in view, it means the session of the view the rule acts in. The renderer places every view and opens every window, main's window-open handler building each one (§Main Process Responsibilities), so no wire call and no bridge member carries a view: a view's drag between windows crosses the bridge only as screen points and window ids (`window.findWindowAt`, `window.startTearOff`, `window.moveTearOff`, `window.endTearOff`, `window.dockView`, [Preload Bridge Contract](../architecture/contracts/preload-bridge-contract.md)).

**A view's drag is the console's own pointer drag, between windows as within one** ([ADR-040](../decisions/040-drag-is-our-own-on-pointer-events.md)). A press on the title — the session's name in the view's header, never the header's empty space, which stays the window's own drag region — takes pointer capture once it lifts the view, and the capture keeps delivering the pointer's moves after it leaves the window, out to the screen's edge. Within the window the view lifts and follows the pointer and its neighbors glide aside. Outside it, each move's screen point goes to main through `window.findWindowAt`, which hit-tests the other windows' bounds and names the window under the pointer, and that window draws its drop indicator; past every window the renderer opens the tear-off window holding the view and main moves it with each move (`window.startTearOff`, `window.moveTearOff`), leaving it on the desktop at release (`window.endTearOff`), and a release over another window docks the view there (`window.dockView`). Escape puts the view back where it was and closes a torn-off window. Measured on Electron 44.5.1 on macOS with real mouse input, main's hit test took 0.2 ms and the target drew its indicator at its next frame, 8.8 ms median and 17.4 ms at the 95th percentile, and a window main moved on each move followed about 10 px behind the pointer at about 650 px/s, about one refresh. Outside Wayland, session views do not use the platform's native drag: a native drag released over the desktop reported its end about 569 ms late on macOS, after the platform slid the refused drop back, and another app under the release may accept the drop as its own. Files dragged in from Finder or another app still arrive through the platform's native drop (§The composer). On Linux under Wayland, Electron can neither read the pointer outside its windows nor move a window — `screen.getCursorScreenPoint()` is "Not supported on Wayland" ([Electron `screen`](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/screen)) and changing a window's global coordinates "is prohibited" there ([Electron `BaseWindow`](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/base-window)) — and it offers no call for `xdg_toplevel_drag`, the protocol Chrome carries a torn-off tab with ([`xdg-toplevel-drag-v1`](https://wayland.app/protocols/xdg-toplevel-drag-v1)). There a view's title drags with the platform's native drag from its start: a snapshot of the whole session view follows the pointer, a release over another of the app's windows docks the view there, a release in its own window swaps it, and a release outside every window opens the view in a window of its own where the compositor places it. The drag carries only the app's own data type, so another app's drop target does not read it.

**The session header** names the session and carries, in order: the title, the pull-request word where a request is open on the branch, the review chip with its added and deleted counts, the preview chip, the terminal control, the session's own menu, and, while the window holds more than one view, the view's close. It is as tall as its own controls and the type beside them, identical on every platform, and nothing about the title bar moves it. All three pane chips are drawn at every moment on either session shape, each carrying one fact and never growing a second; one that cannot act reads as unavailable to assistive technology, takes the faint ink, carries its reason in its own hover title, answers no press, and leaves its chord doing nothing. Each pane has exactly one chord, a row on the Keyboard page like every other: the inspector opens on the platform modifier with `I`, the review pane on that modifier and shift with `G`, and the agents pane, the terminal and the Preview pane on the same pair with `S`, `T` and `B`, the agents pane's chord toggling it only while the agents badge on the composer's strip is visible, so while the session has dispatched nothing the badge is hidden and `⌘⇧S` does nothing; while the review pane's diff holds focus two bare keys step its files, the next on `J` and the previous on `K`, answering neither inside a text field nor with a modifier held. Every one of them is rebindable, and what a chord reads anywhere in the console is the binding in force rather than a written string. On a project session the review chip grays with its counts at zero while the tree is clean, reading `Review · +0 −0` with `Nothing changed in this worktree yet.` in its hover title, and goes live from the first change, and the preview chip grays with `No page or dev server yet.` while the session has neither a page nor a discovered server and goes live again the moment it has one. The terminal chip is live on every project session whose working folder is there, because such a session can always open a shell: pressed when the session has none, it opens the pane and starts one; once that folder is gone it grays with `This session has no shell.`. On a chat session all three gray at every moment whatever the session has written, each naming in its own words what converting the session to a project would light, because a chat keeps files rather than changes and has no repository for a shell: Review's reads `Chats keep files, not changes. Convert to a project to review changes.`, Preview's `Chats have no page or dev server. Convert to a project to preview one.` and Terminal's `Chats have no repository for a shell. Convert to a project to open one.` The command palette lists each of those acts either way, carrying the chip's own sentence while it is grayed.

The pull-request word is a clickable word in the header's own color and font — no box, no border, no glyph, underlined on hover, answering focus and Enter as it answers a press — drawn only while a request is open on the branch and absent the moment none is, never grayed, because absence is the rule everywhere outside the pane chips. The word follows a change made on the site within a minute: while the header is on screen the daemon sends the site a conditional request every 60 s, which costs no quota when nothing changed, and it reads again at once after the person's own git and pull-request acts and the events that change the request. A press opens the review pane on its pull-request tab; where the branch carries more than one request the word reads the newest. Diff counts render with the minus sign and never a hyphen, and an untitled session's title reads `New chat` or `New session`, faint and italic.

The header carries no location chip, attach chip, status dot, goal item, model chip, run-status text, held-note count, port, agent chip or agents badge: the held notes live in the composer's attachment row and the agents badge in the composer's footer strip. It carries no sessions control either, except the one back control the one-column layout puts at the head of the line, which shows the sessions list and is absent side by side. The session's menu is the session's own actions, built from one list with the row's right-click menu and, where the pointer is coarse, the menu a touch held still on the row raises: `Rename session…`; `Open inspector` with its chord; `Open beside` and `Open in new window` where they apply; `Pin to top`, or `Unpin` on a pinned session; `Move to group` on a project session, opening the project's session groups, a rule and `New group…`, which puts a name field in place where the group's row will be, Enter making the session group with this session in it and Escape canceling, so no session group is ever empty and the project header carries no `New group`; `Mute notifications`, or `Unmute notifications` on a muted one; `Convert to project…` on a chat session alone; `Send to the cloud…` on a project session whose provider carries cloud tasks; `Copy link`; a rule; `Archive session` and `Close session…`. `Mute notifications` silences the session's `Finished` and `Failed` notifications on every device and every way they leave the machine, never its `Waiting on you` or a workflow's Notify step; the bell keeps every line and counts every waiting entry, and muting takes down the session's `Finished` and `Failed` banners still standing. Mute and unmute act in place through `session.mute` and `session.unmute` and switch nothing. `Copy link` puts the session's address, `sidekicks://session/<id>`, on the clipboard as one plain line — the one form the copy, the main process's link handler and `sidekicks open` share — acting on the session it was raised for and switching nothing, and its row reads `Copied`, or `Could not copy`, for the one transient duration before the menu closes. `Copy link` is the one control that copies a session's address: the inspector has no fact for it, because its `Address` fact is the session's inbox. The inspector item reads as opening the inspector and never as worktree details. `Send to the cloud…` is absent on a chat and a terminal session and on a provider or pinned build without cloud tasks; where the provider has them but this session cannot use them, it stays listed with its reason and refuses in place: `Cloud tasks need a ChatGPT sign-in on this account.` on Codex, `Cloud sessions need a claude.ai Pro, Max or Team sign-in on this account.` on Claude Code, and `Cloud tasks need a GitHub remote.` on both. Pressing it arms the composer for one message: a line at the composer's top, above any attached files and the draft, reads `Next message runs in <provider>'s cloud`, with `Cancel` at its end, and on Codex `Cloud environment` (the labels this account's tasks already name, or one typed, kept per project) and `Attempts` (1 to 4, Codex's own bound); Send then goes to the cloud and not to the agent, the message landing in the transcript as the person's own, the agent never receiving it, and the composer disarming, with nothing drawn over the transcript. Under that message sits the task's row, `Cloud task · <state>`, the state only what the provider reported and in sentence case on both providers: on Codex `Pending`, `Ready`, `Applied` or `Failed` (Codex's error, with its own message under it); on Claude Code `Submitted`, for the task's whole life, with `Open on claude.ai` and `Bring back` at every moment, because the app cannot know when the cloud session is done — it cuts a new worktree of the project, runs `claude --teleport <id>` there and imports the cloud session into it through the provider-session import, so the returned work opens as a new session beside this one, named after the task, while the task's own row stays `Cloud task · Submitted`; no row reads `Running`, `Finished` or a percentage the provider did not report. A ready Codex task's row carries `Show diff`, which opens the attempt's diff in the review pane as a read-only source, `Attempt <n> of <m>` where it ran more than one, and `Bring back`, which takes a file checkpoint through the daemon's checkpointer and then applies that attempt to the session's folder; the row then reads `Cloud task · Applied`, or on a partial apply `Brought back in part · <n> skipped · <m> in conflict` with every path listed, and `Undo bring back` on it puts the files back from that checkpoint through the same dry run and question as `Undo to here`.

**The pane layout** holds the session column and its row of panes. The row holds any number of main panes side by side — the inspector, the review surface, the agents pane, the Preview pane and the file view, each at most once — and the terminal above or below the main panes, spanning them; together they form one block beside the conversation, to its right by default and to its left once the person moves it there. Opening a pane adds it to the row beside the focused pane and replaces nothing, and closing one leaves every other pane where it was: the terminal opened with no main pane up fills the block alone, a main pane opened then takes the rest of the block while the terminal keeps its place above or below, and closing the last main pane leaves the terminal alone. The row carries no tab strip. Panes reorder by dragging a pane's header — the pane lifts and follows the pointer, the panes it passes glide aside, and the drop glides it into its place — and by `Move pane left` and `Move pane right` (`⌥⇧←`, `⌥⇧→`) with focus in the pane, which move it one place that way and, past the row's end, move the whole block of panes to the other side of the conversation, while `Move terminal up` and `Move terminal down` (`⌥⇧↑`, `⌥⇧↓`) put the terminal above or below; both are the console's own pointer drag and keyboard path ([ADR-040](../decisions/040-drag-is-our-own-on-pointer-events.md)), each move announced in the live region. Every pane keeps its own floor and the session column keeps the conversation's floor, so when the next pane does not fit the row scrolls sideways — nothing is refused and nothing folds — and a pane just opened scrolls into view. While the row moves under Preview — a sideways scroll, a pane's drag or a resize — Preview shows a still picture of its page in its place until the motion stops (§Pane kinds, `browser`). A pane moved into its own window (§Detached panes) leaves the row; while it is away its chip, its toggle and its chord bring its window forward, the chip's hover title reading `<Pane> · in its own window`, and a pane returned from its window lands back in the row, replacing nothing. That window is the pane's whole width, so it carries no full-width control. One entity opens one pane, structurally: a single mount point re-focuses the pane an address already has rather than opening a second. A pane opens by expanding its grid track in flow, never by overlaying, and panes are separated by a one-pixel inset line rather than a border. Opening the review pane, the terminal or the Preview pane by hand collapses the sessions track, and nothing restores it; a pane put back because the session had it open when last shown is not a press and never collapses the track.

Review, the terminal, the Preview pane and the file view can each take the full width, collapsing every other track; while one holds it its control reads as pressed and its hover title flips from `Full width` to `Back to the side (Esc)`, and a second press or Escape returns it without also closing the pane. Opening or closing another pane gives up full width first. Review, the terminal, the Preview pane and the file view each carry a drag edge of their own, and the sessions track carries the same seam; the inspector takes the width its widest label and value pair reads at and the agents pane the width its widest agent row reads at, and neither is resizable. A drag edge works one way everywhere: the pointer drags it, one arrow grows and the other shrinks it by one nudge of the current text size and by five with the shift key, and home or a double-click resets it; a separator reports its current size and its two limits on every set. The terminal above or below the main panes carries a second, horizontal edge with the same shape and the same keys turned on their side, and it opens on a third of the view's height, each half clamping where it stops being readable. The grab zone is wider than the line it draws — inside it the pointer becomes the resize cursor and a hairline brightens under it — while the drawn line itself never moves or changes width, so nothing on screen shifts. A drag suspends transitions and holds the transcript's follow until the pointer is released, and the width persists only on release. Every pane clamps at the narrowest its own content still reads at, and the session column keeps the floor its header must hold; the panes narrow themselves, each no further than its own floor, to keep that floor when the sessions track or the notification list opens, when a pane opens and when full width comes off, not only on a drag, and past every pane's floor the row scrolls sideways instead. The terminal is as wide as the main panes it sits above or below together, its own floor applying only while it is alone in the block.

Every pane clips its own overflow, scrolls in one named inner scroller and never overflows horizontally. Every scroller but the transcript draws its scrollbar over its content and takes no layout width for it in any state: the bar appears while the region is scrolled or hovered, fades when it stops, and its thumb drags. The transcript draws none, its right edge being the ask rail's. A wheel or a drag inside a box that scrolls scrolls that box, and only once the box is at its own end does the gesture reach the transcript behind it; the transcript keeps its own overscroll, never bouncing the window and never passing the scroll outward, with the platform's own scroll anchoring off so a picture typesetting three screens up cannot rock the column under the reader. Each pane loads its body the first time it is opened and stays loaded, so reopening rebuilds nothing, and a body that fails to load reads as one line naming that pane with a retry beside it. Every one-shot read a pane makes takes a cancel signal, and a pane cancels its own reads when it closes or when a newer read replaces them, so a diff nobody is looking at stops being decoded and drawn.

Every pane in the row reads every standing pane rule on its own. A shell that ends keeps its tab, clearing the last tab leaves the terminal open reading `No active terminal.` above its open control, and the main panes stay where they were; a main pane whose subject goes closes itself and leaves every other pane where it was. The terminal control is the one control that opens and closes the terminal, each pane's own close closes its own, and nothing joins the header for the row.

Nothing opens the Preview pane but a person's act: a page or a server appearing, or an agent's browser call, never opens it.

**The session's other work is read where it is acted on.** A goal shows only as the system messages that set, update, complete or clear it; a run is paused and interrupted from the working line; a dispatched agent is read in the agents pane, opened from the agents badge on the composer's strip; the worktree the session works in is the strip's worktree switcher and the inspector's facts; a waiting request is answered on the composer's card; and a finished plan or a file a chat wrote is a row in the inspector's artifacts section, a plan opening in its reader and a file in the file view.

**Banners sit under the session header**, above the pane note and the surface, in the shape §Meridian, the design language item 9 fixes: each carries its own dismissal, blocks nothing, and enters no attention count. Five conditions draw one. A session whose provider started without part of its settings names the file and the line or the key the provider itself reported — never a guess — and offers to open that file in the editor the General settings page names; it goes on its dismissal for this start of the provider, or the next time the file loads cleanly, and a later start still on defaults draws it again. A provider process that ended on its own under a running session is started again by the daemon, at once and then on waits that double from 1 second, each restart of a session's own process landing `Restarted · Claude Code is back` while the waiting messages stay waiting; an account's shared Codex service is restarted on the same waits, and only the sessions whose running turn the crash ended land `Turn ended` and then `Restarted · Codex is back`, its idle sessions showing nothing. The fifth crash within three minutes ends the restarts, and only then does the session read `Claude Code ended unexpectedly · exit code 137 · Restart ×` (`Codex ended unexpectedly …` on Codex, on every session of that service), the exit code the daemon's own and never composed; `Restart` starts the provider again by hand on the same session and clears the count, lands `Restarted · Claude Code is back` (`Restarted · Codex is back` on Codex) and lets the waiting messages go as the next turn. On a shared Codex service one `Restart`, pressed on any of its sessions, brings the service back for all of them: every session that showed the banner loses it and lands `Restarted · Codex is back`, while only the session where `Restart` was pressed sends its waiting messages as the next turn and every other session's keep waiting until the person sends them there. `×` puts the banner away for that crash loop alone, a later one drawing it again. An idle session whose provider sleeps has not ended and draws no banner and no row. A provider build that changed under a running session reads `<Provider> updated · <old version> → <new version> · Restart the session ×`, leaving the running turn untouched until the session is restarted; no build is ever called too old. A session whose folder is lost reads `<folder> can't be reached · new runs wait until it's back`, or `<folder> is a different repository now · new runs are stopped`, which carries `Re-attach` while the folder is still a git repository and `Open folder…` once it is not ([Spec-007 §Repo Mount Health (V1 Definition)](007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#repo-mount-health-v1-definition)), and that banner also goes on its own when the folder is back. When the window cannot save how its panes are arranged, the banner reads `Pane layout not saved · it will save again on the next change`; the arrangement stays on screen and is saved again on the next change, and the failure's code goes to the window's diagnostic capture, never onto the screen. One more line sits where the banners sit and is not one of them: while the window repairs a hole in what it received, or a read it depends on has failed, it reads `Catching up…`, appearing only once the repair has run 0.4 s and then staying at least 0.4 s, and it goes when the missing entries have arrived; nothing else on the screen repeats it. A repair read that fails turns it to `Couldn't catch up · Try again`, and `Try again`, the window's refresh on focus and a reconnect each read again. It shows no technical cause; the cause goes to the window's diagnostic capture.

**A message between two sessions is two rows, one in each transcript**, drawn in the console's own tool-row shape — a verb column, the target, and a trailing cell — and faint: the sending session's row stands open at rest, as every call with a body does, and the receiving session's row is collapsed. The sending session's row reads `SessionSend  to @builder · s-8f21`: the other session as `@name` with its id after it, and in the trailing cell the state, which says no more than happened — `sent` when the call returned, `delivered` once the message reached the other session, `queued, builder is paused`, `held by builder`, `refused: <reason>` in the provider's own words, and `not delivered` where the other session went idle without it. A send to a Codex session the person typed in a terminal, once the service it lives in has been away longer than that provider's own reconnect allows, reads `not delivered` with the reason on its second line, `Reopen it in a terminal: codex resume <thread id>`, and no `Retry`, since sending again cannot reach it. The receiving session's row reads `SessionSend  from @planner · s-4a10` with `replied` in the trailing cell once it has, and it sits where the message was folded into that turn rather than at the turn's end. Opened, each row carries the message, and the receiving one additionally carries `14th since you last wrote` as a fact and not a limit. The answer is a message of its own: it draws as its own `SessionSend  from @builder · s-8f21` row in the sending session where it arrives, carrying the other session's words, and nothing is drawn under the sending row — the same on either provider, whether the answer came inside a held wait or as a new turn. A message that carried files draws on **both** rows the file chips a sent turn's own attachments draw, one per file. Every other session tool draws one row of the same shape in the calling session — `SessionList` with the count, `3 sessions`; `SessionRead   @builder · s-8f21 · last answer`; `SessionStart   @tax-fix · s-91c3 · Codex · new   started`; `SessionAsk   @review-bot · s-77e0 · copy   answered`; `SessionStop   @builder · s-8f21   stopped`; `SessionGroup   auth work · 2 sessions added   done`. A call that waits — a send or a start with `wait: true`, or `session_wait` — reads `waiting` in its trailing cell while it waits, then `finished`, or `<name> is waiting on you` (`builder is waiting on you`) when the awaited session needs the person, the name opening that session; a `SessionWait` row carries the result under it, `SessionWait   @builder, @tax-fix   builder finished`, and a send's answer is its reply row. A row's target names the computer of a session on another of the person's computers: `to @review-bot · s-77e0 · desktop`. Each row's verb is the tool's name with each word capitalized and the underscores dropped, the rule every tool the daemon serves follows, the browser and workflow tools included (`BrowserClick`, `WorkflowResultsPost`); the session rows take the generic glyph at the muted hue rather than a mark of their own. The row is the same line on either provider, drawn from one daemon-side tool event, so nothing about it changes with which provider sits at either end ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)). A send is an ordinary tool call under the sending session's own permission level: at a level that asks the ordinary approval card comes first, at `Sandboxed` and `YOLO` nothing asks, and no gate of the app's own applies; what the message causes in the receiving session follows that session's own level. Two system messages belong to the same surface: an interrupt taken while an exchange is live reads `Turn interrupted · exchange with builder ended · 2 messages not delivered` in both transcripts, and a paused session carries one faint collapsed line, `3 messages waiting · paused`, which opens to the list of them and goes as the continue delivers them in the order they arrived.

**Finding inside the open session** is one chord and one box, drawn on whichever surface has focus — the transcript, or the shell where the shell holds it — on that surface's top-right corner over the content, with no scrim and nothing under it moving, while the transcript and the composer stay live. The box holds its field, focused on open with its text selected and kept for as long as the session stays; the count of matches, reading `N of M` or `No matches`; the previous and next controls; and its close control. There is no scope note, because the count speaks for the whole session. What is searched is the visible text of every row — the person's messages, the agent's replies, the tool rows and the system messages. Each match is marked where it sits, the one being read is marked as current and scrolled into view, and Enter or the step chord moves forward, shift-Enter back, all three wrapping at the ends. The count counts the whole session rather than the rows on screen, so stepping past the last loaded hit loads the history that hit sits in and moves the transcript to it, on the person's own press and never by itself; the count and the reach are the daemon's search over the session's own rows, never a renderer-side walk of what it does not hold. Stepping to a match opens the run group holding it first, so the marked line is never inside a fold. The chord pressed again while the box is already open re-focuses the field and selects what is in it rather than opening a second box. Escape closes the box, removes every mark and returns focus where it was.

**Searching every session** is a second surface, opened by the palette's `Search all sessions` and drawn the way the palette is: one field over the session column, and under it the hits grouped by session — the session's name, then its own matching messages, each hit carrying enough of its words to be recognized, with the matched letters marked the way the palette marks them. It reaches every session the list holds, the archived ones included, in the daemon's own ranked order and with no ceiling on how many come back. It answers its own chord as well as the palette row. Choosing a hit switches the screen to that session and lands on that message, loading the stretch of history it sits in on the way. This is the only surface that searches the text inside sessions; the sessions track's own field searches the rows in the list (§The all-sessions list). Both reads are the daemon's ([Spec-001 §Interfaces And Contracts](./001-session-core.md#interfaces-and-contracts)), and neither box walks rows the console does not already hold.

A region of the session screen that has nothing to show, has lost its subject or failed while drawing says so in the shapes §Meridian, the design language item 8 fixes.

##### The composer

The composer is the chrome every session view carries, sketched under §Signature Feature Composition Sketches.
