It is a floating card at the transcript's own content width, docked one gutter's gap off the bottom of the session column, and it holds — top to bottom — the hairline, the setup card while a clone or a worktree's setup runs, the working line, the approvals card while one waits, the composer proper with its command list, its attachment row, its draft field, its send control and its toolbar, and the session strip. The jump control sits above the card inside the dock while the follow is released. It addresses the session's own agent and carries no machine picker; the one other thing it can address is another session, which an `@` in the draft names (§Session Composer). Files reach the attachment row three ways, and the page code never holds a path in any of them: the attach control's picker, whose picked files come back from `native.showOpenDialog` as tokens; a file dropped on the card — what a drag over the card draws being [Spec-012 §Ingest Validation And Payload Bounds (V1)](./012-artifacts-files-and-attachments.md#ingest-validation-and-payload-bounds-v1)'s — which the page hands to `native.getDroppedFileRef`, the preload reading its path and handing it straight to main, which mints the token; and a picture pasted into the draft, whose bytes the page hands to `native.savePastedImage`, main writing them to a temporary file and minting its token. That file is deleted once `session.attachmentAdd` has copied the picture into the store. `session.attachmentAdd` takes the token and refuses a raw path string, and main's relay turns the token into the path the daemon needs. What its parts show, offer and refuse is in that sketch.

##### Pane kinds

Six kinds. The session's own flow is the `transcript` kind, scoped to its session. Beside it the session's row of panes holds any number of main panes side by side, or none — `inspector`; `diff`, which is both the review surface and the read-only file view; `agents`, the agents pane; and `browser`, the previewed page — with `terminal` above or below them, and filling the block alone when none is up. Opening a pane replaces none, and which panes a session had open, their order and widths, which side of the conversation the block sat on, and where the terminal sat, is remembered per session. A pane moved into a window of its own leaves the row (§Detached panes). Nothing else is a pane. Each pane's heading reads its title — `Transcript` for the `transcript` kind, `Review` for the review surface and `File` for the file view of `diff`, `Preview` for `browser` and `Sidekicks` for `agents` — and every pane announcement, the transcript feed's accessible name among them, speaks that title exactly as the heading shows it, never the kind id and never a second lowercase spelling. The Workflows destination is a page of its own with tabs, and one workflow run opens at its own address inside that page, so neither the builder nor a run is a pane kind ([Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md)). There is no approvals pane, no runs pane and no artifact pane: the one waiting approval, an agent's question and a plan waiting for a verdict are each the single card in the composer, one at a time; pausing and interrupting a run are the working line's own pressable words and a message sent mid-turn is the transcript's last row until the agent takes it; and a plan opens in its reader over the session column, a picture or a PDF in the quick look, and a text or code file — an attached `.md` among them — or a file a session wrote in the `diff` kind — scoped to that one file in the review surface on a project session, and as its own lines in the file view on a chat. The inspector is the session's own, and a repository's, workspace's or worktree's changes open the `diff` pane — no pane kind exists for those entities and the set is not widened for them. A pane-layout snapshot of an unknown version is discarded whole, an unknown pane kind is dropped and reported, and a pane reference that fails validation is rejected.

- **`transcript`** — the transcript surface, scoped to its session. One flow per session, all but the one on screen hidden, capped at the single width every row of it reads with a gutter either side, its rows one derived gap apart whatever they are. It is the one scrolling surface in the conversation: no run group, tool body, diff or output scrolls inside itself, nothing in it is cut at a height of its own beyond the cuts a long diff and a long output take, and nothing scrolls sideways — code wraps, a table wraps its cells and a diff line wraps. The flow fades into the header at its top edge over one line height and nowhere else. Older history comes in as the reader nears the top, one stretch per gesture so a fling cannot pull in a whole day, with a line at the top of what is loaded for the case the automatic one cannot see; newer rows page in as the reader nears the bottom without the reading position moving. Only the rows near the window are drawn, with a band above and below kept ready so a fast scroll never shows blank space, and old rows leave memory in cuts that never take a row on the screen or the row under the reader. A session long enough to reflow is held blank while its row heights settle and shown in one piece, with a failsafe so it can never stay blank; a session opened before mounts at the heights its rows really had last time. It has two position aids: the ask rail at its right edge, and the jump control above the composer. A run group is drawn open, as the transcript draws it, and stays open until a person closes it — by its own header, or by the palette's fold-every-run act; nothing chooses the state a run arrives in, and no setting bounds how many stand open at once. Every way of being sent to one row takes the same path — the row is loaded if it is not on the page, the run group holding it is opened first, the flow lands on it, and it washes faintly in the accent for one duration and then fades; a row that has gone says so in one line instead of moving the page. While the conversation holds a selection, `Send to draft` rises at the selection's end and goes when the selection is cleared; pressing it puts the selected lines into the draft inside a fenced code block, under whatever is already typed, moves focus there and sends nothing. There is no menu on a right-click anywhere in the conversation. The row kinds themselves, and their states, are [Spec-011](./011-transcript-and-reasoning.md)'s; §Transcript View composes them.
- **`inspector`** — the session's own details, as wide as its widest label-and-value pair reads at the current text size, with a header carrying a back control, the kind and the name, `Open in new window` and a close control, over its own scrolling body. The chord that toggles it, and the pane's own back and close controls, close it and leave every other pane where it was. Its kind word is `Worktree` on a project session and `Workspace` on a chat, and wherever it is open it re-keys to the session on screen. On a chat the repository fact reads none beside `Attach a repo`, which promotes the session in place, keeping its history and its managed workspace, and that session's inspector carries no branch, no base and no snapshots. Its sections, in order:
  - **Identity** — the facts `Repo`, `Path`, `Branch`, `Against base`, `Mount`, `Created`, `Provider`, `Account`, `Notifications` and `Address`, in that order. The account fact reads which account the session runs on and offers no switch, because which account a provider's work runs on is set on the Providers settings page and moves every session on that provider that is not pinned to an account ([Spec-025 §Moving a session to another account](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#moving-a-session-to-another-account)). The mount fact reads `Healthy`, `Can't be reached` or `A different repository now`, from the same `health` the lost-folder banner reads, so the two never disagree ([Spec-007 §Fallback Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#fallback-behavior)). The notifications fact reads `Muted` or `Not muted` and has no press of its own; the mute is changed from the session's actions list. The address fact carries `Copy address`, which puts the session's inbox on the clipboard as one plain line — the address a Claude Code session in the person's own terminal writes to when it messages this session ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)) — and is not the session's `sidekicks://` link.
  - **Related** — the sessions linked to this one, highest relevance first, each one line reading the relationship in words — `started by <name>`, `copy of <name>`, `<n> messages with <name>`, `related to <name>` — that presses through to that session as a sessions-list row does. `Link a session…` at the section's foot opens the session picker `@` opens and adds a `related` link, and a `related` row's actions list carries `Unlink`; the links the daemon writes from what happened carry no `Unlink`. With no links the section reads `No related sessions yet` with the one act `Link a session…`. The links are [Spec-001](./001-session-core.md)'s.
  - **Memory** — the `Auto memory` switch, reading `On` or `Off`, over one row per memory file or folder the provider reported for this session: on Claude Code the memory paths its first frame names and the project's `CLAUDE.md` and `CLAUDE.local.md` where they exist, on Codex the memory store under the account's home. Each row is the path in mono with `Open`. The section says which account home it reads, because an account's store is its own and never the person's `~/.claude` or `~/.codex`. The rows come from the daemon's `session.memoryRead` and the switch is `session.autoMemoryUpdate`: Codex applies it at once, and Claude Code when the session's process next starts, so a press mid-run reads `off when this run ends` or `on when this run ends`. `/memory` opens the inspector on this section and `/pause-memory` presses the switch.
  - **Hooks** — read-only, from the daemon's `session.hookList`: on Codex one row per hook the provider reports for the session's folder, naming the event, the handler in one word and the file it came from, with one dimmed row per error or warning in that report, in Codex's own words, so a hook skipped for want of trust or a file that failed to load is named rather than silently missing; on Claude Code, which reports no hooks, one row per file hooks live in — the project's settings files, the account home's settings file and each plugin's hook file — each with `Open`, under one line: `Claude Code does not report which hooks loaded; these are the files it reads.` The daemon's own hooks are not listed. `/hooks` opens the inspector on this section.
  - **Rules** — the approval rules in force for the session and its project, read from where the providers keep them, one row per rule reading what it allows or blocks and its scope — `Allow pnpm test · this session`, `Block api.example.com · this project` — each with a `Revoke` that confirms in place, and a session holding none reads `No rules yet`; it reads and revokes through the rule list and the rule revocation of [Spec-010 §Interfaces And Contracts](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#interfaces-and-contracts). A session rule's revoke applies from the next call on both providers; a project rule's revoke removes it from the provider's file. Every standing rule on the machine is the Providers settings page's list, read from the same files.
  - **Cost** — `Copy` on its header, which puts the section's rows and its total on the clipboard as plain lines; then one faint uppercase header per provider the session spent on, the session's own provider first, each over one row per account that provider spent on — the account named as the account fact names it, then tokens, then dollars — and, under a provider whose voice calls ran, one `Voice` row reading their minutes and dollars; a `Subtotal` row only under a header holding two or more rows; then `Total`. The figures are the daemon's cost receipt by account, each in the one money format and none marked as an estimate, with no input, output, cache, reasoning, per-turn or per-agent lines; a long account name keeps its line and its figures drop to the next line, right-aligned, and a name wider than the pane truncates with its hover title; and a request on a model the price list does not carry yet is held with its exact tokens and joins the rows and the totals when the next fetch prices it. The section ends in three rows the person can set, standing after `Total` and, on a session that has spent nothing, at the foot of the section under a `Total` reading `$0.00`: `Max steps · Unlimited`, that session's own bound on a turn; `Spend limit · Unlimited`, dollars for the whole session across every provider and account it uses; and `Tokens per run · Unlimited`, input and output together for one run. Typing a number sets it for that session and empty is `Unlimited`; they are the only figures in the section that are not a reading of what was spent, and the values every session starts from are the Runtime settings page's. When a turn reaches the step number it ends there and one flow row reads `Step limit reached · 40 · Continue`, and the run is interrupted; the press is a send into that run, returning the same run to `running` — as a message arriving from another session does too ([Spec-003 §The Step Bound On A Turn](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#the-step-bound-on-a-turn)). The two other limits are held by the service's own spend count, which feeds this section, and the service stops both providers itself at the first usage report past a limit, so one request can overshoot slightly. Nothing appears while a limit is unlimited or not reached; a reached limit lands as one system-message row, like `Goal set`, never a banner, a modal or a composer refusal. `Spend limit reached · $20.00 · Raise limit` stops every running turn in the session, the lead's and the helpers', as an interrupt does, with one row in the lead's transcript; a message sent while the limit stands still lands in the transcript, no turn starts, the same row lands under it, and the draft is never lost. `Token limit reached · 500K · Raise limit` ends that run, and the next message starts a new run with a fresh count; a helper's run is its own, so a helper that reaches it stops alone, its child row reading `stopped · token limit reached`, and the lead goes on. `Raise limit` opens this section's field, and saving a higher number carries the stopped turn or run on, as `Continue` does for the step limit. The app writes these rows itself, like `Step limit reached`.
  - **Context** — the tokens used against the model's window with the ring's own percentage, then one row per bucket, a deferred bucket drawn dimmed, and on a Codex session the line `Codex reports totals, not what is in them.`
  - **Changes** — the session's own `+A −D` on its header, one row per changed file with that file's own `+A −D`, and `Open in Review` under the rows, which scopes the review surface to that file, or `Nothing changed here yet.` where there are none. Nothing anywhere lists the working folder's untouched files: a file this session did not touch is named in the draft with `@` or opened from the person's own editor.
  - **Artifacts** — the count in its header, each finished plan a `Plan` row with its first heading, its time and its state word, and each file a chat session wrote a `File` row that opens the file view on it, or `Nothing made here yet.`
  - **Runs here**, on every session — the count in its header, then one row per workflow run in this folder reading its name and when it ran, `nightly-tests · 2 hours ago`, with `· running` after the time while it runs, or `No runs yet.` where there are none; **Snapshots**, on a project session only — a snapshot row carries `Restore`, whose press rolls the folder back and lands one flow row, `Restored to <snapshot name>`, and a rewind from a message lands `Restored to before <the message's first words>` instead; and **Actions** — `Attach a repo` on a chat, and `Open in editor`, `Open in terminal` and `Reveal in Finder` on a project, the first opening the session's whole working folder in the editor Settings names.

  Every `Open` on a path the daemon served — a memory path, a hook file — goes through the same open-in-the-editor member every other surface uses, `native.openInEditor`, with the file reference main's relay minted for that path, so the page never holds a raw path. The only controls in the inspector that change anything are the memory switch, a rule's revoke, a snapshot restore and the cost section's three limit rows, each one press or one number rather than a form; everything else it offers opens something elsewhere — a path in the editor, a file in the review pane, a plan in its reader — or, in the case of `Copy address` and `Copy`, puts lines on the clipboard. It offers no settings form, no model switcher, no token graph and no raw event dump, and nothing in it overflows its own width: every long value truncates and carries a hover title. **The console's own caches are bounded from this machine's memory, never by a count alone.** The files it has cached, the color spans the daemon computed and the pictures it has loaded each carry a share of the machine's physical memory, handed to the renderer once at start — pictures at their decoded size, each decoded straight to the size it is drawn at (`createImageBitmap` with its resize options) and at the screen's size when opened full size, 1/64 of it and at most 256 MiB, cached files 1/512 (16 to 64 MiB), color spans held packed 1/2048 (4 to 16 MiB), the prepared rows of sessions no window shows any more 1/256 (16 to 64 MiB) — the least recently used clean entry going first when one is reached, and a picture's decoded copy let go once nothing on screen draws it (the quick look closed, its session switched away) and decoded again from the file on return; the trim runs only while nothing is happening on screen and no turn is live, it never takes the rows a child's view or its parent is showing, and nothing about any of it is shown — no figure, no control, no notice. That run-time budget is a different thing from the fixed targets in §Budgets, which are the bars every console change is measured against.

- **`diff`** — the one pane kind both the review surface and the read-only file view use: a repository's, workspace's or worktree's changes open it from the inspector or from a row in the transcript, and a file a chat session wrote opens it read-only on that file's own lines as the file view. The file view's header reads, in order, `File`, the path in mono, `Open in editor`, `Copy path`, `Open in new window`, the full-width control and close, and under it the version stepper — previous, `Version <n> of <m>`, next — with `Compare`, which redraws the body as the changes between the version in view and the one before it. Nothing in the console edits a file: every file header, in the review surface and the file view alike, carries `Open in editor`, which hands that path to the editor the General settings page names, beside `Copy path`. What it shows is [Spec-009](./009-gitflow-pr-and-diff-attribution.md)'s and [Spec-012](./012-artifacts-files-and-attachments.md)'s; this spec fixes that both are the one pane kind, that the review surface is that pane with the ship strip and the pull-request tab composed on top rather than a modal, a route or a pane kind of its own, and that the review surface opens on no chat session, because a chat keeps files rather than changes. The ship strip is one state machine: a state line read off the daemon's facts, one next act — commit, push or open the pull request — and one alternate, a pull while the branch is behind standing in place of whatever the state would otherwise offer; with nothing to commit, push or open it reads `Nothing to ship from here.` The review surface's diff is one list at line granularity — every file header, every notice, every gap and every code line is a row of it — and only the rows on screen are built, a bounded number of them each frame however large the diff is; a folded file contributes no rows at all, so folding every file empties the list at once, and a line's coloring happens off the path that builds its row.
- **`browser`** — the session's own previewed page. Two page hosts, one path to them, and no debug port on either. On the machine that executes the session the page is real — a native `WebContentsView` in its own persistent partition inside the window, or the daemon's headless Chromium where no desktop is open there — and on another device it is a live picture of that page and nothing else, with the address line reading `Runs on <machine>`. One resolver in the daemon returns the session's browser context, and nothing above it branches on which host is live ([ADR-034](../decisions/034-embedded-browser-for-preview.md)). On the desktop, main attaches Electron's in-process debugger to each Preview view and to no other page, and carries its messages on the connection the app dials to the service (`preview.pageTargetReport`, `preview.pageDebuggerSend`, `preview.pageDebuggerReport`); the daemon's relay joins them into one browser whose target list is exactly the Preview pages, never a console window. The relay's page-session filter is a hard requirement: on a page's session only the few target commands that page needs for its own children pass, and every other target and browser command is refused, so no page reaches the console's own interface. The headless host is launched with Playwright's `chromium.launchPersistentContext` over Playwright's own pipe with no listening port, from an installed Chrome or Edge where there is one and otherwise from Playwright's Chromium, fetched once on first need, which the transcript shows as one flow row before the call goes on. A page's title, address, history and load state are read from whichever host is live, from the debug protocol's `Target.*` and `Page.*` events, and a page's reported title or address changes only when it really changed. Focus emulation is on for every page the service serves, on both hosts, while an agent's session is attached, so a page covered by another window, minimized or shown behind a sleeping display still takes the agent's clicks and keys. The live picture on another device is a screencast the service produces on whichever host runs the page, through a debugger session of its own, so such a page still gives frames and takes that device's clicks; it runs only while that device has the pane open.

  The pane's header carries `Open in new window`, the full-width control and close; under it an address line runs back, forward, reload, the address control, the size the agent holds as a chip — `390 × 844` — with `Fill pane` beside it, or `Offline` while the agent holds a network condition that stops the page's traffic, both together reading `390 × 844 · Offline`, the live width while the pane's edge is under the pointer, the machine line on another device, the marking tools with the stroke-hue slider under the pen, and last the hand-off that opens the same address in the system browser, drawn only while there is a page to hand over. A size the agent set is not locked: pressing `Fill pane` returns the page to the pane's size and the chip goes, and the agent learns its size was dropped the way it learns of any other page change. The person has no control that drops the agent's network condition; it lasts until the agent changes it or its session ends. Back and forward are always drawn and grayed in place from the page's real history rather than from a count the pane keeps. The address control, spoken as `Address` and with spell-checking off, edits in place, navigates on Enter, keeps typed text while the page updates under it, and takes an http or https address, a bare host, a loopback host and port, a bare port, an address literal or a path against the current page; anything else is refused in the pane's own one-line refusal naming the exact cause — words typed where an address goes read `Preview does not search. Type an address, not words.` — and it never searches. Where the service had to carry a dev server's port to the next free one because its own number was taken, the address reads the port it loads with a faint note naming the one it replaced, `127.0.0.1:5174 (5173 was in use)`. Its page tab strip appears only at two or more pages, one tab each with the page's icon, its title falling back to the host, its port on a loopback page and its own close control, drawn like the terminal's strip and activating the same way, reordering by drag — the tab lifts and follows the pointer and its neighbors glide apart to open the place it will land in, the place at the end of the strip included, and a canceled drag glides it back to the order as it was; the tab's own menu, opened by a right-click, the menu key or Shift+F10, moves it the same way with `Move left`, `Move right`, `Move to start` and `Move to end`, a row that cannot move disabled, and the strip takes no chord of its own; after a move, from the menu or a drag, the live region says `Moved <tab label> to position N of M.`; a canceled drag says nothing, and a refused move says the refusal's own sentence — and closing on a middle-click as on the control. While a page is loading a small turning mark takes the place of its icon on its tab, and with one page open, where no strip is drawn, the reload control turns instead; the mark goes when the page answers. A typed address navigates in place rather than minting a tab, and a page's own pop-up request opens as another tab rather than an operating-system window, and the pane opens on the page that was used last. A loopback address printed in a tool row's output or in a terminal shell opens this pane at that address on a plain press, while the same address written in the agent's own prose opens the machine's own browser on a plain press and opens here on the platform modifier; every other address opens in the system browser. The body holds exactly one of four states: the empty rectangle the desktop positions the real page over, the live picture on another device, the discovered development servers as rows naming each server, its framework where the owning process's command line names one, its loopback address, and `started here` on a server this session started, with exactly one server opening straight onto it rather than listing it and a page whose dev server is gone showing these rows rather than a dead page — or `No dev server is running in <project>`, with the address still live — and the load failure — `<host> did not answer.` with `Try again`, or, where the machine lacks the libraries the headless browser needs, `Preview's browser cannot start on <machine>: it needs system libraries that are not installed. Install them with: sudo npx playwright@<version> install-deps chromium`, the command shown as text to copy and never run by the console. The three tools are icon-only toggles with distinct glyphs and no word on the button — Comment a chat bubble, Box a dashed box, Pen a pen — no two tools sharing a glyph and no tool's glyph worn by anything else on the line; one is pressed at a time, a pressed tool drawn pressed in the accent on a wash of it — the tools are not value chips, so they are the one place on the session screen a pressed state is drawn rather than a value read as a word — and none is pressed while browsing, so there is no `Browse` or `Mark` control; a second press or Escape releases it. The tools' hover titles read `Comment: click the page to leave a note`, `Box: drag a box around what you mean` and `Pen: draw on the page`, the pen's slider is spoken as `Stroke color`, and a note's field shows `Say what is wrong` until it is typed in. With a tool pressed the body shows the page frozen into a picture under the marks layer, described as `The page, held still so it can be marked`: the capture is taken first and the view hidden second, never the other way round, the page is reported not visible for the whole frozen state so no rectangle report can bring the live page back over the picture, and the picture's scale is read from the returned image rather than from the platform's reported scale factors. Comments drop a numbered marker with a note, boxes are dragged with a note, the pen draws freehand in the color on the slider, which rests at the accent with its thumb wearing the next stroke's color, and a stroke keeps the color it was drawn in for life; comments and boxes take the accent and never the waiting color; a mark is removed from its note and a stroke from its own close control; `Clear marks` and `Send marks` exist only while a mark does; and releasing the tool returns the live page with the marks kept. A tool pressed with no page open does nothing, because there is nothing to freeze; a Box or Pen gesture that never moves leaves no mark, only Comment acting on a bare click; and switching pages or closing one while a tool is pressed deselects the tool. Sending them stages one attachment chip in the composer's attachment row, carrying the Comment tool's chat bubble and reading `<N> marks on <path>` (`1 mark on <path>` for one), and focuses the draft, the attachment carrying the picture with the marks drawn in, and per mark its number, note, element reference and box — a stroke its points and its color — with the address and the width; the sent picture carries no removal affordances and no unresolved color token. A mark's element reference is taken at the moment it is committed and is valid only for the snapshot that minted it. The pane reports its own screen rectangle, and the part of it still visible against everything that clips it, on every frame the pane moves or resizes. Main places the page from those reports, on a path apart from the frame the pane is drawn in, so a live page trails a moving pane: measured on Electron 44.5.1 on macOS, the page sat 10 to 15 pt behind its pane during a sideways scroll at about 600 px/s, where a control delayed by 50 ms read 30 pt ([Electron #32751](https://github.com/electron/electron/issues/32751), [Electron #39993](https://github.com/electron/electron/issues/39993)). So while the pane moves — the row of panes scrolling sideways, a pane's drag, a resize — Preview shows a still picture of its page, taken with `webContents.capturePage`, laid in its place with the live view hidden, and the live page returns when the motion stops (`scrollend`, the drop, the release of the edge). With the picture in, the gap measured 0 pt across 183 moving frames, and the swap took 26 ms. The cost is that a video playing in the page pauses while the row moves. An overlay that covers the visible part hides the page, because that is the one place an embedded page is visibly not a document element, and while a pane edge is under the pointer the page lets pointer events through so the drag never sticks on it. When a console window reloads or crashes, the app hides every page view in it until a fresh rectangle arrives, so a dead window never leaves a page painted. A Preview moved into a window of its own takes its page with it whole (§Detached panes). Zoom lives on the keys alone, per page, stepping through 50 · 67 · 75 · 80 · 90 · 100 · 110 · 125 · 150 · 175 · 200 % while focus is inside the pane, with no button, figure or menu for it, and a new page starting at 100 %; every chord the console has bound is still the console's while the page holds the keyboard, and everything else the person types reaches the page; while the page's developer tools hold it, they keep their own keys, and of the console's chords only `Inspect`'s act there, beside the application menu's own keys — on macOS `⌘W` is the File menu's `Close` and closes the focused session view from inside them too, while on Windows and Linux, where no menu row holds Ctrl+W, Ctrl+W does nothing there. The console's own page never zooms and never navigates: those keys and the platform's back-and-forward gestures are swallowed everywhere outside this pane, and the text size stays the Appearance page's, which scales the type and nothing else. A right-click on the page raises a small native menu that main builds from the page's own context-menu event — never the page engine's own, and on the machine never one drawn in the renderer, which would hide the page — whose rows are `Back`, `Forward` and `Reload`; `Cut`, `Copy`, `Paste` and `Select all`; `Copy link` where the press landed on a link; `Open in the default browser`; and `Inspect` last; a row whose act is not available where the press landed is absent rather than drawn dead. On another device, where the page is the live picture in the renderer, the device draws the same menu with the console's own menu component at the point pressed — and so for a long press on a touch screen, a touch the device never relays while it is held still and sends as a right press at its point once held long enough, the menu key and Shift+F10, wherever the browser engine turns them into a right-click menu, inside a frame of the page as on the page itself — while a page that draws its own menu shows it in the picture instead: `Back`, `Forward` and `Reload` go to the machine; `Copy`, `Cut` and `Copy link` write that device's clipboard with the selection or the link's address that came with the menu, `Cut` then removing the selection from the field while the field still holds it; `Paste` puts that device's clipboard into the field under the press, arriving as typing, so a page that takes text only from its own `paste` event does not see it as a paste; `Cut` and `Paste` appear only in a field that takes text, neither read-only nor disabled, and in a password field, where the browser allows no copy, `Copy` and `Cut` are absent and no selected text leaves the machine; `Select all` selects on the page; `Open in the default browser` opens on that device, an address off the machine's loopback in its own browser and a `localhost` one only through a port shared to it ([Spec-027 §Shared ports](./027-remote-control.md#shared-ports)), the row absent where the port is not shared or the device cannot open a shared port, and the address line's hand-off is drawn on the same terms; and `Inspect` is absent. A clipboard the device refuses says so on the pane's refusal line, `Could not copy` or `Could not paste`, and leaves the page as it was, and so does an edit the page took other input before, after the menu opened, `The page changed before that could run.`; while the menu is open the device relays nothing to the page. The machine's clipboard is never read or written for another device, and a right-click another device or an agent sends opens no menu on the machine's screen, except the one menu that opens for the person where they right-clicked the same point at that moment: main pops none for a press it relayed and hands that menu to the service for the device whose press it was, never one that another press at that moment, the person's at the machine, another device's or an agent's, could have raised: the contested press gets none, and a menu that only one of the two raised at the relayed press's point reaches neither, and where no desktop app runs, the service reads the menu in the page through a script of its own that runs before any of the page's. **The page's developer tools** open inside the app, for the previewed page alone, and never hand the page to another browser: `Inspect` opens them with the element under the pointer selected, and the `Inspect` row's keys — `⌥⌘I` on macOS, Ctrl+Alt+I on Windows and Linux, and F12 on every platform — open and close them while focus is in Preview, on its page, its chrome or the tools themselves, and do nothing anywhere else or on another device, where there are no tools; a rebind moves them in all three places, and a key the row gives up reaches the page. `Inspect` and the keys always open them docked at the bottom of the page, inside the pane, whichever side they were moved to last, because Electron keeps one dock state for every page's tools; their own dock control moves them to the page's left or right side or into a window of their own, and while they are docked a size the agent holds is fitted to the page's part of the pane, and where the app cannot learn where that part is, it opens that page's tools in a window of their own, where the page keeps the whole pane. They work beside the agent's debugger session on the same page, and closing the page closes them. While they are docked, every still picture of the page — the one shown while the pane moves and the one a pressed tool marks on — holds them too, each part laid where it sits, so they never vanish for that moment; the picture sent with the marks is the page's alone, and the tools' picture never leaves the pane. The pane's chrome draws no button for them. This follows VS Code's integrated browser, which binds F12 to its developer tools ([`browserDevToolsFeature.ts`](https://github.com/microsoft/vscode/blob/50f37bcc26c75b91937883a8974c969af300d4d4/src/vs/workbench/contrib/browserView/electron-browser/features/browserDevToolsFeature.ts)) and puts `Inspect` on the page's right-click menu, calling `inspectElement(params.x, params.y)` ([`browserViewMainService.ts`](https://github.com/microsoft/vscode/blob/50f37bcc26c75b91937883a8974c969af300d4d4/src/vs/platform/browserView/electron-main/browserViewMainService.ts)), over Electron's own calls on the page's `webContents`, `openDevTools({ mode })` and `inspectElement(x, y)` ([Electron: webContents](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/web-contents)). One saved site-data set per machine serves the pane, the agent's tools and a workflow's browser steps alike, so a login in the pane persists across restarts; the Browser settings page manages it — whether it is remembered, each site's `Sign in`, `Clear cookies` and `Forget`, and clearing all of it — and holds `Browser tools for sidekicks` and the browser in use. When one host takes over from the other, the daemon carries the saved cookies across and copies a site's local storage and IndexedDB by script; a login a site keeps in the platform's own authenticator does not cross, and the pane says so rather than dropping the login silently. A file a page saves opens the system save dialog on the machine's own desktop window, the Preview feature asking for it through the platform bridge and main owning the dialog and the file write, so the window gains no file access; on another device, on a headless machine and anywhere no person at that machine can choose, the download is refused with one line in the pane, `Downloads are off in Preview`: on the desktop main cancels it in `will-download` and reports the refusal to the service, the headless host is launched with downloads off, and on both hosts the service sends the line back. **Caps.** One browser per machine, one tool server per daemon, one renderer process per open page and one more for each page whose developer tools are open, gone when they close, and as many pages per session as the machine's own memory allows — no count stands between the person and another page. When available memory in main falls below 20 % of physical memory, and at least 1 GiB, the oldest page nobody is looking at is released, one every 1.5 s until the reading is back above it and never the page on screen, and the headless host does the same in the daemon; a page unseen for ten minutes is released and reloads on demand, the headless browser closes after ten idle minutes, the screencast runs only while another device has the pane open, and discovery ticks only while a project session is live. The service keeps each session's pages — address, title, icon, order, which is active and zoom — and `preview.pageList` marks a page `released`, on which main destroys its view: a released page reloads when shown, which is how headless pages, and every page after an app restart, come back, while after a service restart main's page views reconnect with nothing reloaded. The pane is never a web browser: no bookmarks, no download list, no developer-tools button, no search, no picture standing in for a real page at rest on the machine that executes the session — the still pictures are only the one a pressed tool marks on and the one shown while the pane moves — no second page shown beside the first inside the pane, and no dialog, overlay, backdrop or scrim anywhere in it. Preview is no source of attention either: a page loading, a mark drawn or an agent's browser call is none of the things that wait on a person. **The agent's browser tools** act on that same page through one tool server — Playwright's own, `tools.createConnection` from the pinned `playwright-core`, hosted inside the daemon, one connection per session handed that session's browser context — identically on both providers: the session's one tool-server entry reaches it on a loopback route guarded by a secret of that session's own, passed to Claude Code through `--mcp-config` with `--strict-mcp-config` and to Codex in the conversation's own configuration at `thread/start`. `Browser tools for sidekicks` decides whether the browser tools are in that entry's tool list, the entry staying for the daemon's other tools, and the entry never carries `browser_run_code_unsafe`, which would run any code in the daemon's own browser tool process outside the provider's sandbox and the command approvals. Each call is a row in the transcript in words with its screenshot inline, gated by the session's permission level like any other tool; a call driven from another device names the machine in its row.

- **`terminal`** — the session's own shells, lease-gated over [Spec-002](./002-machine-registration.md)'s terminal write lease. It holds the person's own shells and nothing else: it never mirrors an agent's own command-line screen, because what an agent is doing is read in the conversation, as its working line, its tool rows, its diffs and its approvals. The pane opens wide enough for a command line and its output to wrap rarely and never narrows past the point one still reads at, with its own drag edge and a full-width control, and it is drawn on no chat session. Under the pane header sits a tab strip: one tab per shell the session has open, each carrying the shell's own title — its basename until the shell sets one — and its own close control, with a control at the end of the strip that opens another shell and makes it active. A tab activates on a press and, while it has focus, on Enter and on Space; the strip takes no chord of its own and none is minted for it. A tab's shell is the person's own login shell started in the session's own tree, so their profile, aliases, prompt and version managers are all there; where their shell names nothing that can be started the daemon falls back to the platform's default and says so in one line above the prompt. While `Simplify for a screen reader` is on, every shell this pane starts carries `CLAUDE_AX_SCREEN_READER=1`, so a Claude Code the person runs there reads the same way. A tab's close control ends that tab's shell with it — refused in place while an agent's running command holds that shell, and asked once in place first when another of the person's devices holds it — and a middle-click closes a tab as its control does; tabs reorder by drag, and a right-click raises a small menu at the pointer with `Close`, `Close others` and `Close to the right`, the last two grayed with their reason in their own tooltip where there is no such tab — there is no close-everything row, because one press would end every shell in the session, a running server among them. The sub-line reads the active tab's project and tree, and the body — a fixed monospace grid one step of the type scale in size, with glyph shaping off and a line height that tiles so box-drawing characters meet with no seam — shows the active tab's shell, under the accessible name `Shell output`. It paints on the pane's own ground from the token every other surface reads, never on a dark box of its own; its ground, its text, its cursor and all sixteen colors a program can name read the theme's own tokens and change in place when the theme or the color scheme changes, with no reopen, the wider color cube a program can address is derived from those same tokens with its gray ramp mirrored in the light scheme so dim output stays readable on a light ground, and a color a program states outright is drawn as stated. Text in it selects the way text selects anywhere — a drag after a little travel selects a range, a double press a word, a third press whole lines, and Shift extends a selection — a selection keeps hold of the text it was made on as output scrolls under it, dragging into the top or bottom of the body scrolls the history while the selection grows, and the selection is washed with the accent rather than covered by it, stronger while the shell has focus and lighter when it does not, so colored output stays readable through it. Each shell keeps its own control lease, so this device can hold one shell while another of the person's devices, or an agent's running command, holds another. The lease is per device, not per window: the pane's output subscription to each shell belongs to the session's store in the one renderer that drives every window, so a terminal moved into a window of its own keeps that subscription and the lease this device holds through it, and takes no second one. The lease line sits between the strip and the body and speaks for the active tab's shell alone: where another of the person's devices holds it, the line reads `<device> holds the shell.` beside the button `Take the shell`, and the body is read-only with no cursor and says so to assistive technology — `Take the shell` asks once, in place on the line, `<device> holds the shell. Take it?` under `Cancel` and `Take it`, which takes focus, and Escape cancels; where an agent's running command holds it, the line reads `<agent>'s running command holds the shell.` with the text link `Stop the run`, which does what the command row's own `Stop` does, and there is nothing to take; while this device holds it, or nobody does, there is no line and the body is live, and the first keystroke into a shell nobody holds takes it. An agent's command may take the shell from this device while nothing is typed at its prompt and no other command is running in it, and when its hold ends, with the command or when the run leaves `running`, the shell goes back to the device it took it from while that device's hold is still bound to an open connection and pane, and otherwise to nobody. Until the holder and its name have been read there is no line, the body stays read-only and there is nothing to take. Switching tabs re-reads the line for the shell that tab shows. Closing the pane gives back this device's leases on its shells, unless another of its panes took them too, and ends none of them — a build started in a tab keeps running and the pane comes back to it — and quitting the console ends none either; a shell ends when its own tab closes, and a shell that ended keeps its tab, its last line saying it exited and with which code, until that tab is cleared. The code that draws the character grid loads the first time the terminal is opened, not at launch: while it loads, and until the active shell's first frame has arrived, the pane reads `Loading the terminal…`, and if that code cannot load it reads `Could not load the terminal` with `Retry`. A shell that does not start leaves its tab in place, its first line reading `The shell did not start · <cause>`, the cause the daemon's own, with `Try again` at its right in the one try-again shape; the tab reads as exited, takes no input, and its own `×` clears it; the session's working folder going is the one thing that closes the whole pane, with its one line, because every shell in it has lost its subject. The screen scrolls back at most 10,000 lines of the daemon's own window for that shell, and the scrollback starts at the first whole line. The view scrolls by the wheel and by the six terminal scrolls of §Settings — a page on `⌘PageUp` and `⌘PageDown`, the top and the bottom on `⌘Home` and `⌘End`, a line on `⌥⌘PageUp` and `⌥⌘PageDown`, and on Windows and Linux the same on Ctrl+⇧, a line on Ctrl+⇧+↑ and Ctrl+⇧+↓ — each rebindable; no other key scrolls it, so the paging keys, shifted or not, reach the program. Output a shell prints while its tab is not drawn is held for it under a bound and shown when the tab is drawn again; a tab whose command ended off screen carries a small quiet mark until the person looks at it, and nothing rings for it. A session opens as many shells as the machine allows, and the only refusal is the daemon's own, written in place under the strip in plain words with the open control left where it is. A selection in the body raises a control that lands those lines in the draft as a fenced code block and moves focus there without sending, which touches neither the scrollback nor the lease, so a shell someone else holds can still be read and quoted. While the body has focus every chord carrying ⌘ is still the console's — on Windows and Linux on the Ctrl+⇧ key §Settings gives it inside the terminal, five plain acts with none, taken before the terminal sees them — and every other key is written to the shell — printable characters as the person's own input method produces them, Enter, Tab and Shift-Tab, Backspace, Escape, the arrows and Home and End in either cursor mode, Insert, Delete, the paging keys, the function keys, control combinations, plain Ctrl+C, Ctrl+R and Ctrl+W on Windows and Linux among them, and Alt as an escape prefix, the chords the platform keeps for itself never among them; the keyboard way out of the body is a chord that moves focus, `⌘⇧T` or `⌘K`, Ctrl+⇧T or Ctrl+⇧K on Windows and Linux; a key pressed while the view is scrolled back snaps it to the live bottom first. An address the shell prints is a live link in the body's own text: a loopback address opens the `browser` pane at that address and any other opens the system browser through `native.openExternal`, drawn as the shell's own text with an underline on hover, with no glyph, no badge and no change to the shell's layout or its scrollback, and the press moves neither the shell's focus nor its lease. The pane carries no repaint control — a program whose frame has gone wrong is redrawn by moving an edge a step, which reaches it as a real resize — and no export: what a person wants out of it leaves as a selection. The pane is the same on either provider — the same tabs, the same keys, the same lease and the same controls — and nothing in it belongs to one provider and not the other. Opening it draws a tab for each shell the session really has, drops the tab of a shell that had already gone, and opens a fresh shell only where the session has none; switching session swaps the whole strip for the one that session owns, each shell keeping its screen and its scrollback rather than being redrawn. Focus follows the act: opening the pane puts the cursor in the active tab's shell, the open control puts it in the new tab's body, closing the active tab activates its neighbor, closing the last tab leaves the pane open, reading `No active terminal.` above the open control, and lands focus on that control, closing the pane returns focus where it was before it opened, and a shell opened from another of the person's devices lands as a tab without taking focus, becoming the active tab only when no tab was active. `Find in this session` pressed with focus in the shell — on Windows and Linux, where it has no key inside the terminal, chosen from the command palette — opens the same find box over the shell, searching that tab's output and its history, with the same count, the same chevrons and the same `×`, Escape closing it and clearing its marks. A paste of any size reaches the shell whole and in order, the daemon writing it in pieces under its own write bound, and where the program in the shell asked for paste marks the whole paste is wrapped in them before it is split, so the program reads one paste; the bound is the daemon's and no figure lives in the pane. A right-click inside the body does nothing, because copying and pasting there are the keys' own acts, `⌘C` and `⌘V` on macOS and Ctrl+⇧C and Ctrl+⇧V on Windows and Linux, where plain Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V reach the program; shift with Enter writes a line feed rather than submitting, which is how the provider command-line tools take a newline; and the cursor is a filled block while the shell has focus and an outline of that block when it does not, and never blinks.
- **`agents`** — the agents pane, its tree of every agent in the order the work happened and the child view inside it, composed in §Agents Pane View.

##### Detached panes

Any side pane — the inspector, Review, the agents pane, the terminal, Preview and the file view — can leave its session's view for a window of its own and come back. One act moves it: `⌘⇧A` on the side pane holding focus, and `Open in new window`, a pop-out control in every side pane's header beside full width and close, drawn the same way on each and carrying no text; pressed in the pane's own window, `⌘⇧A` returns it. There is one window per pane per session: the act on a pane already in its own window brings that window forward. The pane leaves its session's row of panes; while it is away, its chip, its toggle and its chord in the session's view bring its window forward instead of opening it a second time, and its chip's hover title reads `<Pane> · in its own window`. The window carries the pane's own header with `Return to session` where `Open in new window` stood, reading `Returning to session…` while the return is under way, and its close, which is the same return. In its own window the pane answers exactly as it does in the row, less full width, since the window is the pane's whole width already. The window is titled `<Pane> — <session title>`; the pane header is its drag region, with the macOS window controls in the header's inset; it opens at the pane's default width and the display's work-area height, and refuses to shrink below the pane's own floor.

A pane's own window belongs to its session and to no window: it stays when the window it came from closes or that view shows another session, and it ends only by its return, its close or a quit. A return puts the pane back in its session's row of panes, replacing nothing — a main pane beside the main panes there, the terminal above or below them — and where the session is on screen nowhere, the return opens the session in the focused view of the window used last, with the pane in its row. Its place and size are kept per pane kind, pulled back on screen when the display it sat on is gone, and a pane that was in its own window when the app quit comes back in its own window at its kept place, from the detached state the pane-layout snapshot keeps.

One renderer drives every window. The hidden console document opens the pane's window with `window.open`, and main's window-open handler (`setWindowOpenHandler`) hosts the child in its own `BaseWindow`, built from main's own options; the renderer copies its stylesheets into the child and renders the pane there, sharing its stores and daemon subscriptions. The child keeps the renderer's sandbox, isolation and content security policy, has no preload and no bridge of its own, and nothing crosses the bridge for a detached pane; main pairs the two closes, so closing the window closes the child and the child's end closes the window, and no detached window is ever in Preview's relay target list. A crash of the renderer takes every window with it: main closes them, and the reloaded renderer reopens every window, its views and each detached pane from the kept layout, as after a restart, unless the crashes repeat ([Desktop App Implementation Notes §Repeated renderer crashes](../architecture/desktop-implementation-notes.md#repeated-renderer-crashes)). Chords are when-scoped, so each window answers the chords of what it holds; an app-wide chord pressed in a pane's own window acts on its session's view where that view is on screen and brings its window forward, and otherwise acts in the window used last. The desktop's banner shows while no console window is focused, and appearance reaches every window. The terminal's lease is per device, so a detached terminal holds no second lease.

Preview's page moves with its pane, whole: main moves the page's view out of one window and into the other (`removeChildView` on the window it leaves, `addChildView` on the one it joins), with no reload — the same page, socket and debugger link, the agent's session and the screencast carrying on, a size the agent holds refitted to the new pane — and the pane reports its rectangle through `browser.publishPaneRect` against the window that holds it. The page's owner window becomes the new one, so focus aims there. Closing a window never closes the pages in it, so on every return main puts the page back in its session's view, and on a quit it closes it; a pane detached when the app quit comes back with its page `released`, its stored address, order and zoom kept.

A pane's own window is an arrangement of this desktop: another device and Remote Control see no change, and the session, what each pane shows and the terminal's lease are the same.

##### Settings

Settings is where the machine is configured — the accounts that pay for the work, the tool servers, the projects, the browser, the keys, the look, the notifications, the background service and the devices linked to reach the sessions — and nothing on it makes or runs work; a rail destination does that. Every page is one projection of what the daemon and the main process already hold: the screen recomputes nothing they report and keeps no second copy of a fact, so a page whose write is refused renders that refusal and keeps the value it had rather than pretending to save, and a reading the daemon cannot settle is drawn as unsettled — an account it cannot decide about reads as undecided and never as signed in. The console carries no identity of its own on this screen or any other — no sign-in gate in front of the first session, no organization or workspace step, no avatar, no security section, and nothing that offers to carry an earlier workspace across — and the only sign-in any page offers is a provider account's, on the Providers page. A passkey, the credential a browser or a phone uses to link itself, is listed and removed on the Devices page alone. Nothing on the screen authors an agent, sets how a diff reads, or holds a preference for an editor inside the console: an agent's instructions are written on the Sidekicks screen, the review surface keeps its own reading controls, and the editor preference on General names an outside editor.

Two panes, one page at a time. The left pane is the page list with its search field above it and the entries below in a fixed order; the right pane draws exactly one page. The search field finds controls on every page over the same matcher the palette uses: every word typed must appear in a control's own label, the heading it sits under or its one-line hint, hits rank on the label first, then the heading, then the hint, with ties in page order, each hit reads its page and heading, `Providers › Codex` — a control under no heading reads its page alone — and choosing one opens the page and lands on the control. The field's placeholder reads `Search settings`, and a term nothing matches draws one line, `No setting matches “<term>”.` A page that splits its own pane in two — the server list and the selected server's detail on MCP servers — is that page's own business and not a third pane of the screen. The pages are rendered always and in order with the open one marked as the current page to assistive technology, so a page whose service is unreachable keeps its entry and says on the page what is missing — a list that shrinks teaches a person the setting does not exist. While the background service is unreachable every page but Runtime reads `The background service is not answering, so nothing on this page can be saved right now. Every control below keeps the value it had, and the page says what it could not read.`; Runtime says so in its own words and keeps its controls drawn. The list is a roving-cursor list: while it stands beside the page its cursor opens the page it lands on, an arrow, home or end moving the cursor and drawing that page at once with no second press, and while the window shows one pane at a time the keys move the cursor without opening a page and enter opens the page under it, because drawing the page would take the list away; either way the cursor wraps from the last entry to the first and back, so the keyboard and the eye walk the same order. Which page is open lives in the route — the screen, a page, and a page with a selection — so a deep link and a click on the list are the same act and the back control works; the selection is handed to the page untouched and narrowed fail-closed against that page's own vocabulary, so an unrecognized selection opens the page for nothing rather than refusing to open. Three surfaces deep-link in: the worktree setup card's `Edit setup steps` link opens Projects on that project, the composer's tool-servers control opens that session's own server list, under which the single link `Manage in Settings` opens MCP servers, and a run refused for its account opens Providers on the account the refusal names. Arriving from any of them finishes the journey rather than landing at the top: the page opens, a folded section holding the named control is opened first, the control is scrolled into the middle of the view and lit briefly and then quiet again, and a control the page is not drawing leaves the person on the page rather than refusing the link. Where the window is too narrow to hold both panes at once — the break being the width the two panes' own content needs at the current text size, never a device class — they become one screen at a time, the list alone and a page with a back control above its title that returns to the list. Reopening Settings opens the page that was last open. The rail and its attention control are the same here as on any other screen, and the sessions track sits collapsed.

Every page opens with one line saying what it is and where its values are kept; on another device that line names where — a device's own pages say they are kept on that device, and the machine's pages name the machine. Every page's section heads take one style — uppercase, in the faint ink, at the heading letter-spacing step, at a size from the type scale and at weight 500 — and the screen keeps no second heading style; a page's facts read their labels in the muted ink and their values at weight 500 in the text ink, as General's install facts read `Platform macOS`. A control commits as it changes: the screen's only save-and-revert pair belongs to the Projects page's worktree draft, which is a real draft, so closing Settings commits the field being edited and never that draft, and the one other exception is a secret typed into a masked field — the web address and the mail password on Notifications — each submitted by its own `Save`. A control whose value differs from its default is visibly marked: a small round mark in the accent color after the control's name, read by assistive technology as `Changed from the default`, its tooltip naming the default — `On by default` or `Off by default` on a switch, and on a choice the default choice's own label, as `System by default`. A control kept in the machine's settings file draws only once the file has been read, so it never shows a value it does not hold and no changed mark shows before then: until the read answers its row reads `Reading settings…`, and a read that fails reads `The settings could not be read.` with `Try again`; every such reading line appears only after the console's one short delay and only while nothing it stands for is drawn, so a read that answers at once draws no flash of words. A refused write rolls the control back to the value it had and shows the daemon's own words in an inline strip on the page, with `Try again` as a faint clickable word at the right end of its line, no box and no icon, and an act that settles without changing what is drawn says so in one line on the page, in flow; there is no toast anywhere on this screen. Every switch carries its role, its accessible name and its state, answers Enter and Space, shows the one focus mark, and sits in a press target derived from the text size; a switch grayed by one of the named exceptions keeps all three and says why in its own hover title. A sign-in that does not finish — a provider account's or a tool server's — says so where it was started, leaves what it was signing in to exactly as it was, and keeps the control that starts it again. Credential material reaches this screen only as a write-only field. What this install looks like stays on this install: the theme, the scheme, the text size, the transcript width, the page last open and the keyboard map are kept for this machine and no page offers to carry a preference to another one, nothing exports or imports the screen's own values, and nothing resets the screen in one press — a reset is a page's own, and the Keyboard page's is the only one.

The ten pages, in that order:

- **General.** What this install is and how it updates itself, and what a new session starts from, under the page note `What this install is, how it updates itself, and what a new session starts from.` The page opens with the running version and, beside it, the platform, the architecture and the language, each as the app reports it. Then the update block, which draws the whole path as five numbered steps — `Checked`, `Downloading`, `Checking the signature…`, `Ready to install`, `Restart to finish` — with the step it is on marked and the ones behind it ticked: idle reads `The newest version seen is <version>.` with `Check for updates`; found reads `Update available — <version>, released <date>.` with `Download`; downloading reads `Downloading… <N>%` over a bar; ready reads `Ready to install — <version>. Restart to finish.` with `Restart to finish`, which restarts the app at once with no confirm and no line about work in flight, because a restart stops no session, run or shell: the background service keeps them running through it. Under the line naming the version found sits one fold, `What changed`, carrying that version's notes in plain sentences, shut until it is pressed and drawn in every arm that names a version and in none that does not. A verification failure reads `The update could not be verified and was not installed.`, with `Try again` as a faint clickable word at the right end of that line. Where the updater cannot be reached, the step that failed says so: `Could not check for updates.`, `Could not download the update.`, `Could not restart to install the update.` and `Could not read the update status.` where the block's status cannot be read. An update a previous run staged and never applied is reported in the same block — the update status's `staged` member, set beside whichever arm the block is in and filled at launch from the updater's cached pending update — naming the staged version beside the version this install is on, and it stays there until an update lands; while an update is staged or ready to install, the rail's Settings button carries one small dot. An install that cannot update itself draws no update button: it reads `This install cannot update itself. Run <command> to get the newest version.`, `<command>` being, on an install a package manager put there, that package manager's own update command (`sudo apt update && sudo apt upgrade`, `sudo dnf upgrade`), and a macOS copy running outside a writable folder reads `This install cannot update itself. Move the app to Applications, then check again.` Below the block: `Check for updates automatically`, on; `Keep crash reports`, on, under `Reports stay on this machine, with personal data stripped.`, the one control for it anywhere in the console — a report is built, stripped of personal data and kept on the machine that crashed and is sent nowhere, any of the person's linked devices reads it through Remote Control (`daemon.crashList`) and `sidekicks crash list` reads it from the command line, and with the switch off no report is kept; `Start a new session on the last model and effort used`, on, carrying those two values, kept in the machine's settings file beside the switch, written by the background service each time a pick moves them and read through `machineSettings.read()` and `machineSettings.subscribe()`, so the line under the switch and a new session read the same value, under `The permission level is never carried over. A new session always starts at YOLO.` and one line naming the model and effort that would be carried, `Last used: Claude Opus 5, at high effort.` — with the switch off, or on the first session ever, a new session starts on the first usable signed-in account's provider and that provider's default model, and the automatic-compaction bound is not remembered here either, staying the provider's own on Providers and the session's own behind its context ring; `Keep this Mac awake while a sidekick is working` on macOS and `Keep this computer awake while a sidekick is working` on Windows and Linux, off, under `Closing the lid still puts it to sleep.`, with nothing added to the rail for it — with it on, the background service holds the machine and its screen awake while any agent works and lets go when none does, so the hold lasts exactly as long as the work, with the app open or quit, and it stops only idle sleep, never a sleep the person asks for. The hold is the service's own, tied to its process, through each platform's own tool: `caffeinate -d -i -w <daemon pid>` on macOS; on Linux `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep:idle --mode=block` over a process that ends with the service, for sleep, and `org.freedesktop.ScreenSaver.Inhibit` held on the service's own session D-Bus connection, for the screen; on Windows, the service on Windows or in WSL 2 alike, the service's Windows half calling `SetThreadExecutionState(ES_CONTINUOUS | ES_SYSTEM_REQUIRED | ES_DISPLAY_REQUIRED)` on its own thread and clearing it with `ES_CONTINUOUS` alone; `Editor that opens files`, one preference every open-in-the-editor act in the console honors, listing every editor the console looks for — Visual Studio Code and its Insiders build, Cursor, Devin Desktop, VSCodium, Sublime Text and Zed, then Xcode, the JetBrains IDEs, Nova, BBEdit, Emacs and Notepad++ — each found through the operating system's own register of installed apps and never through the command path, because an app started from the Dock or the Start menu has no command path of the person's to search, on Linux the desktop entries in the system's application folders, backed by each editor's own launcher paths for an install that adds no entry — an entry matches an editor by its desktop id, a JetBrains entry by its `StartupWMClass`, or its `Exec=` program, the id match wins, and a `NoDisplay` or `Terminal` entry is skipped while another matches; detection on Windows is measured there before it ships; and Devin Desktop is one entry, found under its own identifiers and under the Windsurf ones an install that has not updated still carries, and reads `Devin Desktop` whichever it finds; main reads that list through `native.listEditors()`. `System default` leads the list and holds the value until someone changes it; an editor this machine does not have keeps its place dimmed and unpickable and reads its name with `(not installed)`, `Zed (not installed)`; and an editor chosen here that later goes missing reads `That editor could not be found. Pick another, or files open in the system default.` on its row, keeps its place, and lets the open land in the system default rather than failing. Last, `Default checkout for a new project session`, the two places the worktree switcher offers — `A new worktree`, the default, or `The checkout the project already has` — under `A choice made when a session is created always wins.`
- **Providers.** Signing in to Claude Code and Codex, which account new work runs on, and what each provider does on its own; the page's opening line ends `Every sign-in and pasted token stays on this machine.` on every platform. On a Windows computer with at least one WSL 2 distribution, one row above both provider sections, `Where Claude Code and Codex are installed`, reads the place the background service runs in, `Windows` or `Ubuntu (WSL)`, with each provider's version there, `Claude Code 2.1.271 · Codex 0.156.0`, under one line, `Sidekicks use the Claude Code and Codex installed here.`, and on every other computer the row is absent; Providers is the only page that names a side or a distribution, and only where the place is its subject. Where the row is drawn, every provider line that says this machine names the place instead, reading with Ubuntu in use: `Not installed in Ubuntu.` with `Install in Ubuntu`, which runs the provider's Linux installer inside the distribution and reads `Installing Codex in Ubuntu…`, and, where the other place has the provider, `Codex 0.156.0 is installed on Windows. Install it in Ubuntu, or pick Windows above.` beside the same button; Codex's shared-service line, `Runs one Codex service in Ubuntu while it is on, so sidekicks can message a Codex session you typed in an Ubuntu terminal.`, grayed `Codex is not installed in Ubuntu.`; the terminal plugin's line opening `Adds /sidekicks to Claude Code in an Ubuntu terminal.`, grayed `Claude Code is not installed in Ubuntu.`; `Command`'s `Where this provider's command lives in Ubuntu. It is checked again when the path changes.`; `Standing rules`' `one row per rule this provider holds in Ubuntu`; `Import sessions from Claude Code`'s `Reads the conversations Claude Code already keeps in Ubuntu and adds them to the sessions list. Sessions imported here are copies; import again to bring in what the tool added since.`; `Import memories from Claude Code in the Ubuntu terminal`; and `Remove`'s confirm, `Remove <email> from this list? Its folder in Ubuntu is left untouched. Sign-in history and spend go with it.` With Windows picked the same lines read `on Windows` and `a Windows terminal`. `Change…` lists every place with what was found in it, read when the list opens — `Windows · Claude Code 2.1.271 · Codex not installed`, `Ubuntu (WSL) · Claude Code 2.1.271 · Codex 0.156.0`, `Debian (WSL) · Checking…` — a WSL 1 distribution dimmed with `Needs WSL 2`; picking another place confirms in place and moves the service and what it keeps — sessions, agents and what they remember, workflows, settings and signed-in accounts, each account's sign-in file moved and never copied — while project folders stay where they are, and the computer stays one machine to every linked device. A move that fails puts everything back and offers `Try again`; where the place in use has gone from the computer, the row reads `Ubuntu (WSL) · No longer on this computer` under `Choose where Claude Code and Codex are installed.`, and a pick confirms under `Cancel` and `Start there` with `Use the Claude Code and Codex on Windows? The background service starts there with nothing in it; Restore… on Runtime brings back the latest backup.`, the last clause only where a backup exists. The move's progress, settled and failure lines are [Spec-006 §The service on a Windows computer](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#the-service-on-a-windows-computer)'s. Nothing is asked at first launch: the service starts where both providers are found, then where one is, then on Windows, and where that place has neither provider, the flow row a session that cannot start draws reads `Choose where Claude Code and Codex are installed` and opens this row. Then one section per provider, each in two halves: the settings that provider exposes at the provider level above the accounts registered against it. The provider's half reads whether its command is installed and signed in and which version, or `Cannot tell right now.`; a provider not installed reads `Not installed.` with `Install`, on every platform, which runs that provider's own documented installer once, as the person, where the service runs, with no administrator step, no terminal and no input, and `Cancel`, the installer's own timeouts bounding it, and a failure shows the installer's own reason with its command under it as text to run by hand. Under that: `Command`, one field that reads `Auto-detect` while empty — looking on the service's own path, then the login shell's, then where each provider's installer puts its command — and is checked again when it changes; `Available for new sessions`, which every provider can turn off; `Helpers at once`, empty by default and reading `Unlimited`, which is no limit, `0` running none and any other number that many, passed to Codex as its own limit and held on Claude Code by the daemon's hook on the Agent tool; `Standing rules`, one row per rule that provider holds on this machine, read from its own files, each reading what it allows or refuses with its scope (an account home or a project) and the file it came from, and `Revoke` confirming in place and removing the rule from the provider's own file, which the provider follows as it does on its own (`provider.standingRuleList`, `provider.standingRuleRevoke`) — the inspector's `Rules` reads the same files for one session, and the line under the section's rows reads `Applies from the next tool call.` under Claude Code and `Applies to conversations started after this.` under Codex; `Protected paths`, read-only, each entry naming where it came from; `Compact automatically at <N>% full` in both providers' sections, moving in steps of five between the lowest bottom stop and the highest top stop that provider's models report and mapped onto the provider's own knob; under Claude Code, `Output style`, whose choices and their one-line descriptions come from the installed build's own style listing and which the daemon applies to sessions started later as a flag setting that writes no file, `Advisor`, `Off` and then the advisor models Claude Code offers, under the line `Applies to sessions started later. A session already running changes its own advisor from its composer.`, kept in the machine's settings file and never in Claude Code's, which a session takes when it is created and keeps from then on, and `Use Sidekicks in terminal Claude Code`, off, which installs the app's plugin and one setting in the person's own Claude Code settings and, turned off, removes only those; under Codex, `Reach Codex sessions started in a terminal`, on, under `Runs one Codex service while it is on, so sidekicks can message a Codex session you typed in a terminal.`; and `Import sessions from <provider>`, under `Reads the conversations <provider> already keeps and adds them to the sessions list.
