Sessions imported here are copies; import again to bring in what the tool added since.`, reporting into one progress row on the page, whose settled line reads `Imported <n> of <total> sessions from <provider> · <m> already here · <k> failed · attached <projects>.`, each part drawn only where there is one and the projects the import attached last — for example `Imported 125 of 128 sessions from Claude Code · 34 already here · 3 failed · attached web, api.` The registry itself — what an account is, how it is signed in, how its billing mode and its per-limit quota are kept, which account a run starts on, and what pressing an account does to the sessions already running on that provider — is [Spec-025](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md)'s; §Provider Accounts And Cost View composes both halves and states what the page shows and refuses. The current-account mark also moves from any screen by the `Cycle accounts` chord the Keyboard page holds, as [Spec-025 §Moving a session to another account](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#moving-a-session-to-another-account) describes.
- **MCP servers.** The settings-level governance page for this machine's tool servers, and the only place in the console where one is added, removed or governed: a server list on the left of the page pane and the selected server's detail on the right. The governance rules it renders — the inventory, per-tool overrides, the authorization it never re-derives, and the events it reads — are [Spec-024](./024-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md)'s; §MCP Servers View composes the page and states what it shows, collects and refuses.
- **Projects.** Where cloned repositories go, every project attached to this machine, what runs after a worktree is made, and the environment rows every process starts with. `Clone new repositories into` sits first: one folder with `Change…`, which opens the platform's own folder chooser, reading until it is set the daemon's own answer (`repo.cloneFolderRead`) — the folder holding the most recently attached project, or the home folder when there is none — the same answer the session picker's clone line reads. An `Every project` section follows, holding the environment rows every process the console starts is given and `Branch names`, the pattern a new worktree's branch is named by, `sidekicks/{session}/{title}` until the person changes it, checked by git's own rule and against every branch in the repository and refused in place with nothing written. Then every project attached to this machine, one row each, opening in place; on a Windows computer with WSL, a project whose folder is on the other side's disk carries `This folder is on another disk, so sidekicks read it more slowly.` An opened row carries, first, `After creating a worktree`, the steps that run after a worktree is created — the files to copy, the commands to run in order, and the time limit they are given — behind the screen's one save-and-revert pair; they run as soon as the tree exists, at every level, with the repository's own git config, its hooks included, and no approval card, and the setup card is their record: each step in order with its status and the elapsed time, then `Worktree ready`. That project's own environment rows sit directly beneath those steps, a name set there winning over the row of the same name above it and saying so on itself, then `Branch names for this project`, empty unless set and overriding the `Every project` pattern when it is. Either environment list refuses at save, in place and writing nothing, a name that is not an environment variable name, a credential-shaped name — a provider key, or any name ending in `_TOKEN`, `_SECRET`, `_KEY` or `_PASSWORD` — and a name the console sets itself on the processes it starts; the rows are ordinary configuration and read back plain. Beside them sit four actions on the attachment: rename, one field on the row that Enter applies and Escape leaves, which edits the display name and moves nothing — not the folder on disk and not the project segment of any worktree path, so a renamed project's worktrees and the sessions standing in them are untouched; open in the editor General names; archive, which takes the row out of the sessions list's grouping and moves it to an archived group at the foot of the page, its sessions kept and one unarchive action on it; and delete, grayed while an agent is running anywhere in the project with its hover title naming the running session — the pattern a worktree's own removal control uses — and otherwise behind a confirm in place that says its sessions stay, the folder on disk stays, and the act cannot be undone. What the steps do, what an environment row reaches, and what attaching and detaching a project mean are [Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)'s and [Spec-008 §Required Behavior](./008-worktree-lifecycle-and-execution-modes.md#required-behavior)'s; this page is where they are written.
- **Browser.** What the browser inside the console remembers, and whether agents may drive it, under the page note `What the browser inside the app remembers, and whether sidekicks can drive it. One set of site data for this machine.` One saved site-data set serves this machine — the cookies and local storage, and the console's own visit list — shared by the `browser` pane, an agent's browser tools and a workflow's browser steps alike, with no per-session partition, so a login survives a restart. The page holds: `Remember site data`, on, under `Logins survive a restart. The Preview pane, a sidekick's browser tools and a workflow's browser steps all share this one set.`; `Sites with saved data`, one row per registrable domain reading the host, `Saved cookies` where it holds at least one unexpired cookie or `Saved site data` where it holds other data and no cookie, and when it was last used — never whether the person is signed in, because a saved cookie proves no login and a login kept in the site's own storage holds none — with the one act that row's state allows beside `Forget`, and nothing offered in a state where it could only refuse, what each act does being its own tooltip and no line on the page: `Sign in` on a `Saved site data` row, its tooltip `Signing in opens the site in a page of its own, and the login is kept here.`, which opens that site in a page of its own that belongs to no session, over the machine's one site-data set and showing the address line alone, so what the site saves is kept for every browser surface, and reads the list again when that page closes; and `Clear cookies` on a `Saved cookies` row, its tooltip `Clears this site's cookies, its parent domain's included. The site's other saved data stays.`, which clears the site's cookies across its registrable domain, parent-domain cookies included, and leaves its other data and claims nothing about a sign-out; `Forget`, per site and never per session, the site's full erase of its cookies, storage, cache and service workers; `Clear all site data`, confirming in place with `Clear site data for <N> sites? Every saved login goes with it.` under `Cancel` and `Clear`; `Browser tools for sidekicks`, on, which decides whether the browser tools are in the tool list of the one tool-server entry the daemon hands each session, the entry staying for the daemon's other tools, rather than whether one call is allowed, under `Off leaves the browser tools out of what every sidekick is handed; the app's other tools stay.` and `Every call a sidekick makes still goes through that session's own permission level. Nothing here loosens that.`; `Browser in use` — the app's own, an installed Chrome or Edge, or the one fetched on first need — reading `Uses the installed Google Chrome 141` or `Uses the browser fetched on Sep 12, 2026 · 141.0.7390.54`, with `Fetch it again`, which settles in place with `The browser is being fetched again.`; and `Self-hosted git hosts`, under `A self-hosted forge registered here lights the pull-request half of Review.`, one validated host per row with `Remove` beside the field `Add a host, such as gitlab.internal.lan` and its `Add host`, where adding one asks the installed, signed-in `gh` and `glab` which of them answers for it, so a self-hosted GitHub or GitLab lights the pull-request half of the review surface, and a host that does not parse is refused in place with `That is not a host name.`, one that neither answers for with `<host> didn't answer as GitHub or GitLab. Sign in to it with gh or glab, then add it again.`, nothing written either way. With no site data saved the page reads `No site data is saved.` rather than drawing an empty table. The page reads and acts through the daemon's `browser.siteDataList` (each site carrying `hasCookies`), `browser.siteSignIn {origin}`, `browser.siteCookiesClear {origin}`, `browser.siteDataForget`, `browser.siteDataClear`, `browser.chromiumRead` and `browser.chromiumFetch`. On the desktop the daemon clears through main, which owns the Preview partition — `preview.pageCookiesClear` for a site's cookies and `preview.pageSiteDataClear {origins?}` for `Forget` and, with no origins, for clearing everything — answered with `ses.clearData` per registrable domain, the one call that removes a login cookie set on the parent domain; on the headless host the daemon clears in its own browser context. The two switches are kept in the machine's settings file, which the daemon reads each time it launches the headless browser or a provider, and have no verb of their own.
- **Keyboard.** Every chord the console answers is a row on this page, under the page note `Every key the app answers to. Change any of them.`, and nothing on any other screen changes for it. The page carries its own search box, `Search shortcuts`, above the rows, filtering them as it is typed and reading `No shortcut matches that.` where nothing matches, and the rows sit in four groups in one order — Panes, Sessions, Find, App — one row to an act, fifty-three rows over fifty-five keys. Panes holds `Review` on `⌘⇧G`, `Preview` on `⌘⇧B`, `Inspect` on `⌥⌘I` or F12, `Terminal` on `⌘⇧T`, `Sidekicks` on `⌘⇧S`, `Inspector` on `⌘I`, `Open in new window` on `⌘⇧A`, `Move pane left` on `⌥⇧←`, `Move pane right` on `⌥⇧→`, `Move terminal up` on `⌥⇧↑`, `Move terminal down` on `⌥⇧↓`, `Scroll terminal up` on `⌘PageUp`, `Scroll terminal down` on `⌘PageDown`, `Scroll terminal to top` on `⌘Home`, `Scroll terminal to bottom` on `⌘End`, `Scroll terminal up one line` on `⌥⌘PageUp`, `Scroll terminal down one line` on `⌥⌘PageDown`, `Next file` on `J` and `Previous file` on `K`; Sessions holds `Sessions list` on `⌘B`, `Search sessions` on `⌘K`, `Search all sessions` on `⌘⇧F`, `New session` on `⌘N`, `Next session` on `⌥⌘↓`, `Previous session` on `⌥⌘↑`, `Focus session on the left` on `⌥⌘←`, `Focus session on the right` on `⌥⌘→`, `Open session 1` to `Open session 9` on `⌘1` to `⌘9`, `Previous ask` on `⌥⌘[`, `Next ask` on `⌥⌘]`, `Send message` on Enter, `Search sent messages` on `⌃R`, `Interrupt` on Escape and `Interrupt everything` on `⌃C`; Find holds `Find in this session` on `⌘F`, `Next match` on Enter or `⌘G`, `Previous match` on Shift-Enter and `Close find` on Escape; and App holds `Command palette` on `⌘⇧P`, `New window` on `⌘⇧N`, `Open Settings` on `⌘,`, `Open Workflows` on `⌘⇧W`, `Next waiting` on `⌥⌘N`, `Answer this run` on `⌥⌘↩` and `Cycle accounts` on `⌥⌘A`. Each of those is the default, and every row is rebindable. Each row's one line says what its act does, and these read: `Interrupt`, `Interrupts the sidekick in view when nothing on the screen is open to close.`; `Interrupt everything`, `Interrupts the lead and stops every sidekick, when nothing is selected and the focus is outside the terminal.`; `Open in new window`, `Moves the focused pane into a window of its own, and returns it to the session when pressed in that window.`; `Inspect`, `Opens or closes the previewed page's developer tools, inside Preview, while the focus is in Preview.`; `New window`, `Opens a new window with the new-session picker.`; `Focus session on the left`, `Moves the focus to the session on the left in this window.`; `Focus session on the right`, `Moves the focus to the session on the right in this window.`; `Move pane left`, `Moves the focused pane one place to the left, and past the end to the other side of the conversation.`; `Move pane right`, `Moves the focused pane one place to the right, and past the end to the other side of the conversation.`; `Move terminal up`, `Puts the terminal above the panes.`; `Move terminal down`, `Puts the terminal below the panes.`; `Scroll terminal up`, `Scrolls the terminal up one page.`; `Scroll terminal down`, `Scrolls the terminal down one page.`; `Scroll terminal to top`, `Scrolls the terminal to the oldest line it keeps.`; `Scroll terminal to bottom`, `Scrolls the terminal to its newest line.`; `Scroll terminal up one line`, `Scrolls the terminal up one line.`; `Scroll terminal down one line`, `Scrolls the terminal down one line.`; `Search all sessions`, `Searches titles and message text across every session.`; `Search sent messages`, `Searches the messages sent in the open session, from the message box.`; and `Cycle accounts`, `Moves the Default mark through a provider's saved accounts while the key is held, and settles on the one under the mark when it is released.` `Next session` and `Previous session` step the focused view through every session the list holds except those on screen in another view, in the list's own order with chats first, whether the sessions track is open or closed, whatever is folded and whatever the search field filters, wrapping at both ends and never opening the track. `New session` does what a press on the new-session control does — opens the sessions track if it is closed, opens the picker under it and puts focus on the picker's first row — and pressed again while the picker is open closes the picker and leaves the track as it found it. `Next file` and `Previous file` take the next or the previous file's header to the top of the review pane, only while focus is in the diff, never inside a text field and never with a modifier held. `Open in new window` moves the focused pane into a window of its own — with focus in no pane, the pane focused last — and, pressed in that window, returns it to its session (§Detached panes). `Search sessions` opens the sessions track and focuses its search, and never closes it; `Open Settings` opens Settings from any session and in any state, and pressed while Settings is open does nothing further, so it never toggles. `Interrupt everything` answers only with nothing selected and focus outside a terminal: with text selected the key is left to the platform's own copy, which is no row of this page, in the terminal body it is the shell's, and a second press never quits the app. `Move pane left` and `Move pane right` move the focused pane one place along its view's row of panes, and pressed past the row's end that faces the conversation move the whole block of panes to the other side of it, while pressed past the end at the window's edge they move nothing; `Move terminal up` and `Move terminal down` put the terminal above or below the main panes; all four answer only while focus is in a pane and not in a text field, where `⌥⇧` with an arrow selects by word on macOS, and never while the terminal's body has focus, where they reach the program as every key the console does not keep does. The six terminal scrolls move the terminal's view a page, to its top or bottom, or a line, on the keys macOS Terminal uses; on Windows and Linux they start on Ctrl+⇧ with Page Up, Page Down, Home and End, and on Ctrl+⇧+↑ and Ctrl+⇧+↓ for a line, as Windows Terminal ships them, so on every platform Page Up, Page Down, Home and End without those modifiers, with Shift or without it, reach the program and return the view to its newest line, as every key sent to the program does, and `⌘↑` and `⌘↓` are left free. **No chord carries two acts**, with one shape of exception: two rows may share one key where they are disjoint by state — Escape, where closing the find box answers only while that box is open and interrupting only when nothing is open to close, and Enter, where stepping to the next match answers only while that box is open and sending the message in the message box. A row carries the act's on-screen name, a one-line description of what it does — never a command identifier and never a raw when-clause — and the chord it holds now drawn as key caps; `Rebind` and `Reset` are drawn only while that row is under the pointer or holds focus, and the reset only while the row is overridden, so a page of fifty-three rows reads as names and chords rather than as a hundred controls. The page carries one whole-page reset naming how many chords are changed, `Reset all <N> changed shortcuts`, and `Reset all 1 changed shortcut` where one row is overridden. Rebind records the next press on the control itself while the rest of the keyboard is suspended, so a chord the console already uses can still be pressed; during a recording Escape leaves the chord alone and Backspace or Delete leaves that act with no chord at all, and one line under the search box says so: `Press Rebind on a row and then the chord to use. Escape leaves the chord alone, and Backspace or Delete leaves that act with no chord at all. The rest of the keyboard stops answering while a chord is being recorded.` A rebind that settles says so on the row in one line, `<Act> now answers to <chord>.`, or, where the act was left with none, `<Act> now answers to no chord at all.` A key another act already answers to is refused on the row, naming the act that holds it, as `⌘F already opens Find in this session.` does for `⌘F`; on Windows and Linux an act's key inside the terminal follows its chord by the rule above, and a rebind is checked against those keys too, so a chord another act holds inside the terminal is refused with the same line — `Open Workflows` rebound to Ctrl+⇧K, which `Search sessions` holds there, is refused. A key an application menu row holds on this platform is refused the same way, the line naming that row and its menu as the menu shows them, `⌘C is already Copy in the Edit menu.` With no modifier, or with Shift alone, only F1 to F24 may be recorded, and not Shift+F10: the keys that type, move the focus or the cursor, edit, dismiss, send, scroll or open the right-click menu already do that on the console's screens, Shift+F10 opening a right-click menu on every platform, a page tab's among them ([§Pane kinds](#pane-kinds)), and the few that do nothing alone, Insert among them, are refused with them. The row refuses such a chord with a line naming the key as the chord spells it on the platform and what the key does — a key that types names the character the press typed, `J already types j.`, `Space already types a space.`, on a Russian layout `J already types о.`, and `<chord> already types text.` for a dead key or an input method's composing, and the other keys their act, on Windows and Linux `Tab already moves the focus.` (`⇥` on macOS), `Page Down already scrolls.` or `Shift+F10 already opens the right-click menu.` (`⇧F10` on macOS) — while Escape, Backspace and Delete, alone or with AltGr held, which reads as the key alone, are the recorder's own keys, so only their Shift forms reach the refusal, and `Interrupt` and `Close find` go back to Escape only through their reset. Escape with any modifier is refused too, because menus and dialogs close on Escape whatever is held with it, as `⌥⎋ already closes menus and dialogs.` on macOS; on Linux GNOME's own Escape chords take GNOME's line first, each naming what GNOME does with it, since GNOME acts on them before the app sees them: Alt+Esc and Alt+Shift+Esc as `GNOME switches windows on this chord before any application sees it.`, Ctrl+Alt+Esc and Ctrl+Alt+Shift+Esc as `GNOME switches system controls on this chord before any application sees it.`, and Super+Esc as `GNOME restores its own shortcuts on this chord before any application sees it.` ([`org.gnome.desktop.wm.keybindings`](https://github.com/GNOME/gsettings-desktop-schemas/blob/51.0/schemas/org.gnome.desktop.wm.keybindings.gschema.xml.in), [`org.gnome.mutter.wayland.keybindings`](https://github.com/GNOME/mutter/blob/51.0/data/org.gnome.mutter.wayland.gschema.xml.in)). On Windows its own Escape chords take Windows' line first the same way: Alt+Esc as `Windows switches windows on this chord before any application sees it.`, Ctrl+Esc as `Windows opens the Start menu on this chord before any application sees it.`, and Ctrl+Shift+Esc as `Windows opens Task Manager on this chord before any application sees it.` ([Microsoft: Keyboard shortcuts in Windows](https://support.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/keyboard-shortcuts-in-windows-dcc61a57-8ff0-cffe-9796-cb9706c75eec)), and any chord with the Windows key is refused with `Windows keeps every chord with the Windows key for its own shortcuts.`, because Windows reserves that key for itself ([Microsoft: Keyboard](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/uxguide/inter-keyboard)). On macOS a chord of Option, or Option and Shift, on a key that types a character is typing too and is refused the same way, `⌥J already types ∆.`; and on Windows and Linux any press holding AltGr is refused with its key's line named with AltGr, since such a press types its character and fires nothing or else reads as the key alone: `AltGr+E already types €.`, `AltGr+↓ already moves the cursor.`, `AltGr+F5 needs Ctrl or Alt with it.`, and on Windows a Ctrl+Alt press that types a character is the same press and reads the same. Recording a row's own shipped chord is never refused and puts the row back as its reset does, so `Next file` and `Previous file` take back J and K, and on Windows and Linux `Interrupt everything` takes back Ctrl+C, which it shares with the Edit menu's Copy, split by whether text is selected. **A chord is modifiers plus exactly one key**, held in one spelling so two chords written differently are known to be the same and the refusal on a taken one can be trusted, and it answers only while the modifiers held are exactly its own — a finger too many defeats it rather than firing the shorter chord. `Cycle accounts`, `⌥⌘A` by default, is the one act read as a held chord: its modifiers stay down while its key repeats, it settles when they are released, and it is still one chord, recorded and refused exactly like the others; its row's one line is the one above. Where the platform reports a different character than the key that was pressed, the letter under the option modifier and the punctuation under the platform-and-shift pair are recovered from the physical key, so those chords still answer — on Windows and Linux the digits and punctuation under Ctrl+⇧ among them, Ctrl+⇧1 reporting `!` and Ctrl+⇧, reporting `<`. A letter chord answers on the key that types its letter, and on a layout whose keys type no Latin letter, such as Russian or Greek, on the key in that letter's place on a US keyboard, so on Windows Ctrl+F there, which reports `а`, still opens the find box. On Windows and Linux a chord holding Ctrl and Alt, with Shift or without it, fires only when the press types no character of its own: a press that AltGr — or, on Windows, Ctrl+Alt standing in for it — turns into a character on the current layout always types that character and fires nothing, inside the terminal or out, so AltGr+N still types `ń` and AltGr+⇧N `Ń` on a Polish keyboard, while Ctrl+Alt+⇧] on a US keyboard, which types nothing, is `Next ask` in the terminal. Sending the message is a row like any other, holding Enter by default: while it holds Enter, Enter sends and shift-Enter starts a new line; while it holds the platform modifier with Enter, Enter starts a new line and that pair sends. The message box gains no control for it either way. **The grammar is when-scoped.** Each act declares once whether its chord reaches into a text field: inside a field only the chords so marked fire and every other key falls through to the text editing, and while a previewed page holds the keyboard every platform-modifier chord and `Inspect`'s keys, F12 among them, are still the console's while everything else reaches the page, and while the page's developer tools hold it only `Inspect`'s keys and the application menu's own are, and while a terminal shell holds it the console keeps the chords carrying ⌘ and everything else reaches the shell. **On Windows and Linux**, where a ⌘ chord is written with Ctrl and a Ctrl combination is a key the program reads, the console's chords answer inside the terminal on Ctrl+⇧, taken before the terminal sees them as Windows Terminal and GNOME Terminal take their own, and a key there keeps the one meaning it has outside: a chord already on Ctrl+⇧ keeps its act — Ctrl+⇧B is `Preview` and Ctrl+⇧N `New window` there as everywhere, and the six terminal scrolls keep their keys; a plain chord moves up to Ctrl+⇧ only where that key is free — `Search sessions` to Ctrl+⇧K, `Inspector` to Ctrl+⇧I, `Open session 1` to `Open session 9` to Ctrl+⇧1 to Ctrl+⇧9 and `Open Settings` to Ctrl+⇧, — and a chord on Ctrl+Alt moves up to Ctrl+Alt+⇧ the same way, each onto a free key: `Previous ask`, `Next ask`, `Next waiting`, `Answer this run` and `Cycle accounts`. Four rows start on keys of their own on these systems, the same inside the terminal and out, because GNOME keeps Ctrl+Alt and Ctrl+Alt+⇧ with the arrows for its workspaces ([`org.gnome.desktop.wm.keybindings`](https://github.com/GNOME/gsettings-desktop-schemas/blob/51.0/schemas/org.gnome.desktop.wm.keybindings.gschema.xml.in)) and the platform's keys are never the console's: `Previous session` on Ctrl+⇧[, `Next session` on Ctrl+⇧], `Focus session on the left` on Ctrl+Alt+⇧Home and `Focus session on the right` on Ctrl+Alt+⇧End, none of them held by GNOME, KDE Plasma's KWin, Windows' own shortcuts or Windows Terminal ([`defaults.json`](https://github.com/microsoft/terminal/blob/v1.25.2733.0/src/cascadia/TerminalSettingsModel/defaults.json)). Five plain acts have no key inside the terminal, because the Ctrl+⇧ key they would move to belongs to another act: `Sessions list`, `Next match`, `Find in this session`, `New session` and closing the view; from the terminal they are reached after leaving it, by Ctrl+⇧K or Ctrl+⇧T, or through the command palette on Ctrl+⇧P, whose `Find in this session`, chosen while the shell held focus before the palette opened, opens the find box over the shell. An act that cannot act while the shell has focus has no key there either — `Interrupt everything`, `Search sent messages` and `Inspect` — so its key reaches the program. Copy and paste in the shell are Ctrl+⇧C and Ctrl+⇧V there, through the pane's own handler, and plain Ctrl+C, Ctrl+R, Ctrl+W and every other key reach the program, except the keys the platform keeps for itself, Alt+F4 among them, and a development build's Ctrl+⇧J ([§Fixtures](#fixtures)). The window closes on Alt+F4 on these systems, the platform's own close — Windows' own key ([Microsoft: Keyboard shortcuts in Windows](https://support.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/keyboard-shortcuts-in-windows-dcc61a57-8ff0-cffe-9796-cb9706c75eec)), GNOME's window manager's `close` ([`org.gnome.desktop.wm.keybindings`](https://github.com/GNOME/gsettings-desktop-schemas/blob/51.0/schemas/org.gnome.desktop.wm.keybindings.gschema.xml.in)) and KDE Plasma KWin's `Window Close` ([`useractions.cpp`](https://github.com/KDE/kwin/blob/v6.7.5/src/useractions.cpp)), and the key VS Code closes its window on ([VS Code: `windowActions.ts`](https://github.com/microsoft/vscode/blob/50f37bcc26c75b91937883a8974c969af300d4d4/src/vs/workbench/electron-browser/actions/windowActions.ts)) — so Ctrl+W keeps the one meaning it has, the focused view's close, and the Window menu's `Close` row holds Alt+F4, so the key closes the window under a window manager that leaves it to the app as well; inside the terminal Alt+F4 still closes the window, because the terminal leaves it to that row, and on GNOME and KDE Plasma the window manager takes it first. What the program loses is what the terminal would have sent for those keys: nothing for Ctrl+⇧ with a letter, a digit other than 2, `,`, `[` or `]`; NUL for Ctrl+⇧2 on a US layout, which the program still gets from Ctrl+Space; `ESC[1;6A`, `ESC[1;6B`, `ESC[1;6H` and `ESC[1;6F` for Ctrl+⇧ with ↑, ↓, Home and End, while Ctrl+⇧ with Page Up and Page Down only scrolled the terminal's own view; and for Ctrl+Alt+⇧ `ESC` and a carriage return with Enter, and `ESC[1;8H` and `ESC[1;8F` with Home and End, and on Linux `ESC {` and `ESC }` with the brackets and `ESC` and Ctrl+N or Ctrl+A with N or A, which on Windows the terminal reads as AltGr and sends nothing for (xterm.js's `_isThirdLevelShift` in [`CoreBrowserTerminal.ts`](https://github.com/xtermjs/xterm.js/blob/6.0.0/src/browser/CoreBrowserTerminal.ts)); and `ESC[1;3S` for Alt+F4 where the window manager leaves that key to the app. A chord answers only where its own act can act: a chord whose chip is grayed does nothing, and a chord whose surface is not open does nothing. The condition an act is live under is a typed condition tree built in code over a closed set of named contexts, never a parsed expression; conflict detection runs against those conditions, and the saved map holds only key combinations. Each window answers the chords of what it holds, and a chord's session view is the focused one. One line above the app's rows reads `In the terminal, keys with ⌘ act here; everything else goes to the program.` on macOS and `In the terminal, keys with Ctrl+Shift act here; everything else goes to the program.` on Windows and Linux, and one line under them reads `Claude Code and Codex keep their own keys. In the terminal, type /keybindings or /keymap to change them.` — the console neither reads nor writes either program's key file, because a session runs over the wire where no key exists and a command a person runs in the terminal pane reads its own file itself. The page lists once the one pointer gesture no rebind reaches: a `⌘`-click on a diff block in the flow opens Review on that file. The page closes by naming the keys it does not hold, under `Keys this page does not hold`: `⌘W closes the focused session view; when it is the only view, it closes the window.` on macOS, and `Ctrl+W closes the focused session view; when it is the only view, it closes the window. Alt+F4 closes the window.` on Windows and Linux. There are no two-stroke sequences, no keymap presets and no import or export of the map: every chord is one press, every row is changed on its own row, and the map arrives from nowhere but the person's own rebinds. Every chord drawn anywhere in the console reads the live binding rather than a fixed string. The map is local to this install and is never written to a wire. It is its own file, `userData/keyboard-map.json`, which main alone reads and writes through the bridge's `keyboardMap.read()` and `keyboardMap.write()`, the write returning the map as stored so the page redraws from what landed; it holds only the rows that differ from the chord they ship with, keyed by the act rather than by a position, so a row put back to its default drops out of it and a default improved in a later release reaches anyone who never touched that row, and an override for an act the console does not have is ignored when the file is read, draws nothing, and is left out the next time the file is written. It is written whole and atomically and readable and writable by this person alone, so a crash mid-save cannot leave the keyboard half-written; a missing or broken file reads as the chords the console ships with, main repairs it, and the page says both happened. It has no change feed: one renderer drives every window, so one reader holds the map. A development build's reload chords are compiled out of the shipped app and are no rows here, because in a shipped console there is nothing there to rebind.
- **Appearance.** How the console looks on this machine, kept for this install, under the page note `How the app looks.` Its first control is `Theme`, two swatches — `Meridian`, marked `Default`, and `Graphite` — each previewing live on press, under `A theme changes colors only. Every screen, every word and every control stays where it is.` Under it `Color scheme`'s three cards, `System`, `Light` and `Dark`, `System` the default, each as wide as a theme swatch and painting a live picture of the console at a swatch's height, corners and edge, so the two rows read at one scale and wrap as the swatches do, under `System is the default. It follows whatever this Mac is set to, and each theme ships both.`; then `Text size`'s four steps, `Small · Default · Large · Larger`, under `Text and the space around it grow. Icons and corners stay put.`, with `Simplify for a screen reader`, off, beside it; then `Transcript width`, a slider directly under `Text size` with the width it gives at the current text size read beside it, moving one root font size a step between the narrowest a transcript row still reads at and the widest a line of the agent's prose still reads comfortably, and returning to its default on a double-click of its knob, under `How wide the conversation reads; every session follows it.` The theme, the scheme, the text size and the transcript width are main's appearance record: a change goes to main through `window.setAppearance`, which writes the record, and a color scheme main could not save is not applied, the window's banner reading `Could not save the color scheme, so it did not change.` — `Transcript width` previews in every window while its knob is dragged and is written once, on release — and main stamps them on every console document it serves, so the first paint is right with no script and no read. The screen-reader switch draws the console flat — the panes lose their decorative borders and washes, no animation or transition runs, a tool call reads as one plain line of words, and the transcript reads as headings and paragraphs in order — and, because it reaches past the console, it is kept in the machine's settings file, where the background service reads it when it starts a terminal-pane shell and sets `CLAUDE_AX_SCREEN_READER=1` in those shells and in no other process. What each of them changes, and what each of them must not change, is described in §Meridian, the design language; this page is where they are chosen.
- **Notifications.** When the console tells the person something is waiting, and how a moment reaches them away from the screen. `Show a count on the app icon`, on. `Notify me outside the app`, on, which governs every operating-system notification on this machine, under one line saying the attention kinds are posted while the app is not in front and a workflow's Notify step is posted either way; beneath it four switches, each on and each with its one line — `Waiting on you`, `Finished`, `Failed` and `Notify steps`. A kind switched off stops that kind's banner on this machine and nothing else: the bell's list still holds every entry, the icon count still counts every waiting entry, and a banner already posted is still replaced and withdrawn by its moment. With `Notify me outside the app` off, the four keep their state and draw grayed, each reading `Turned off above.` Under the four, one line says a session muted from its menu stays quiet about finishing and failing and still says when it is waiting on the person. Where the operating system refuses the app's notifications the switches still draw, and the page says the system is refusing while the bell and the count inside the app keep working; on macOS main reads the permission through `native.getNotificationPermission()` (`notify-status`), where `granted` (authorized or provisional) is allowed, `denied` is the refusal this line draws, `notDetermined` means not asked yet, and `unsupported` — no bundle id, or no reader — means the platform cannot post one, which the page says in place exactly as it says a refusal. Then the two ways a moment leaves this machine, both off until the person sets them up. `Send to a web address` posts one signed message for each moment of the kinds picked under it to an address the person owns, naming the session and its state and never what was said, only while no app window is in front on any of the person's devices, and a Notify step either way; the address is masked and write-only behind its own `Save`, sealed in the keychain or the daemon's `secrets.json` file and shown back only as its scheme and host, with its signing secret shown once, a test send and one status line. `Email me what I have not seen` sends at most one email per chosen period from the background service through the person's own outgoing mail, listing what waits on them and what finished or failed that they have not opened, never what was said, over an encrypted connection only; its password is masked, write-only and sealed in the keychain or the daemon's `secrets.json` file, with a test send and one status line. Neither sends a muted session's `Finished` or `Failed`. Nothing on the page makes a sound, there is no per-session list, and the daemon's own stored records are never rendered as further switches: the daemon keeps no notification preference of its own. The switches are kept in the machine's settings file — the kinds as `notifications.kinds.waitingOnYou`, `.finished`, `.failed` and `.notifyStep`, a missing key reading on — and describe this machine's screen, while another device keeps its own; the background service reads them once, when it writes each entry, and marks an entry whose banner a switch or a mute withholds ([Spec-017 §Default Behavior](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md#default-behavior)). The mute itself is the session's: `Mute notifications` and `Unmute notifications` in the session's actions list (`session.mute`, `session.unmute`) silence that session's `Finished` and `Failed` on every device and every way out, never its `Waiting on you` or a Notify step.
- **Runtime.** The background service that runs the agents, the folders it can reach, what it keeps, and the port it listens on. The page shows what the supervisor reports about the service and edits none of it — whether it is running and since when, its version, and how much of the machine's processor and memory it is using, labeled `Processor` and `Memory`, each figure followed by the time it was taken, `<percent>% · as of <time>` and `<size> · as of <time>` (as in `212 MB · as of 1:16 PM`), and `Not read yet` for a figure not read yet, and, on Linux where no Secret Service answers, one line under the service's own, `Secrets are kept unencrypted in <path>, readable by this account alone, because no Secret Service is running.`, and on a Mac while its secrets are in `secrets.json`, from the approved logged-out service's takeover until `sidekicks daemon uninstall` moves them back, including after that service is turned off in Login Items & Extensions, and never while the service waits for approval, the same line with its own cause, `Secrets are kept unencrypted in <path>, readable by this account alone, because the service runs while you are logged out.` while that service runs, and `Secrets are kept unencrypted in <path>, readable by this account alone, because the service was set up to run while you are logged out.` once it is turned off in Login Items & Extensions, `<path>` read from the status read's `secretsFile`, absent on every other machine — read through `daemon.status.read` when the page opens and again on `Check again`, and nothing samples it on a timer. A service that is not answering reads `The background service is not answering.`, and the page keeps its controls drawn rather than emptying. On Windows, where the service has written down why it cannot start — WSL not responding, its restarts used up with the error it gave, the place it ran in gone from the computer, or its connection's name held by another program — the page reads that reason instead, in words naming what failed and what to do and never the side the service runs on, each line quoted in [Spec-006 §The service on a Windows computer](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#the-service-on-a-windows-computer), from the `cannotStart {reason}` member of the supervisor's `daemon.status` topic; and on Windows and macOS one line under the service says when it will not keep running while the person is signed out, from the same topic's `whileSignedOut` — on Windows, their Windows password changed since `sidekicks daemon install --while-signed-out` ran, or the computer allows no service with nobody signed in, each line quoted in the same Spec-006 section; on macOS, the system service that install registered waiting for an administrator's approval, `The background service will keep running while you are logged out once an administrator turns it on in System Settings › General › Login Items & Extensions, under App Background Activity.`, or turned off in Login Items & Extensions since, `The background service no longer runs while you are logged out, because it is turned off in Login Items & Extensions. Turn it on in System Settings › General › Login Items & Extensions, under App Background Activity.` ([Change Login Items & Extensions settings on Mac](https://support.apple.com/guide/mac-help/change-login-items-extensions-settings-mtusr003/mac); [Spec-006 §The service on a Mac while logged out](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#the-service-on-a-mac-while-logged-out)). Beside the facts sit `Restart` and `Stop`, each confirming in place that work in flight stops — and where Codex sessions typed in a terminal are inside the shared Codex service, `Stop`'s confirm counts them (`session.terminalProviderSessionList`), because stopping the service strands them until the person runs that provider's own resume ([Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)) — and `Update the background service`, which the app's own update on General does not touch: it draws `Checking`, `Downloading`, `Checking the checksum…`, `Waiting for running work to finish` and `Restarting the service` over a bar, with `Cancel` until the app asks the old service to stop, and it never stops work to update — while any runs it waits, naming on one line, its parts joined by `·`, up to three running sessions and runs by title, each opening its work, then `and <N> more`, then the count of terminal Codex sessions — and it settles naming the old and the new version, or both versions and the reason where it rolled back. Below them: the folders this machine can reach, each with what is using it and its own removal — a project's folder removed by the same act and under the same rule as Projects' delete, each worktree the app made listed beneath its project, marked `worktree`, and removed by the worktree switcher's rules, and on a Windows computer with WSL a worktree whose folder is on the other side's disk carrying `This folder is on another disk, so sidekicks read it more slowly.`, and each kept worktree a row with its name, when it was removed and its size, carrying `Put back` and `Delete now`; how long diagnostic logs (7 days by default) and finished sessions (90 days) are kept; `Package cache`, the packages workflow steps download, with `Cache limit` (`Unlimited` by default), what each cache holds, and its clears, which never break a step already installed; `Delete old data`, the one act that deletes finished sessions past their bound; `Backups` — `Back up automatically`, off; `Back up to`, with `Choose…`; the last backup's line; `Back up now`; and `Restore…`, which lists the backups in the folder and, confirmed in place naming the work that stops, has main stop the service, replace its data with the backup's and start it again (`daemon.requestRestore(backupId)`), `sidekicks db restore <backup>` doing the same on a machine with no app — a backup being a plain copy that holds no credential, so another computer reads it directly and the person then signs in to providers and links devices again; `Export all data`, which writes everything this machine keeps for the person to a folder they pick, leaving out settings, credentials and keys; `Erase all data`, which confirms in place and removes everything the app keeps on this machine, its credential-store items included, project folders staying; the port a workflow started by a web request listens on; `Ask me after one start leads to`, at `100 runs` by default; `Stop a run after`, `No limit` by default; `Max steps per turn`, `Spend limit` and `Tokens per run`, each reading `Unlimited`, what every new session starts from — no step limit being what both providers do on their own — under one line saying what each number bounds and that a session can set its own in its inspector; the memory cap for tool processes; and the two diagnostic switches, recording traces and recording raw provider messages, the second naming the folder it writes to on the side the service runs. What each of those controls does, what each refuses, and that each value is set in one place and no second one are [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s, and this page is the surface that shows them. No control on it turns a deletion on: the diagnostic logs are dropped past their bound, the package caches past a limit the person sets, and a finished session past its bound becomes eligible for deletion — eligible is not deleted — so `Delete old data` is the only thing on the page that removes a session. Diagnostics is not a page of its own — its three controls, the two switches and how long diagnostic logs are kept, are the whole of it — and nothing on this page reads the event log back: there is no recent-activity fold, no credential-attempt list and no audit view anywhere in the console. Nothing in the console inspects a stalled run, reads a failure's classification, requests a recovery action or reads the redaction policy, and no surface here claims those reads; [Spec-018](./018-observability-and-failure-recovery.md) owns the operations themselves.
- **Devices.** The person's computers and the devices linked to reach their sessions, the last page, at `#/settings/devices` and `#/settings/devices/<id>`, under `Computers and devices linked to reach sessions remotely.` Its body is Remote Control's, contributed to the page list through the settings page registry, and it is present on a machine with nothing linked yet, because it is where the first device is linked. Four sections in one column, each opening with its line and, while empty, showing its line and its act alone. `Machines` holds a card per computer that runs the person's sessions: its name, `This machine` on this one, its operating system and service version — a Windows computer whose service runs in WSL reads as Windows, one card whichever side runs the service — whether it is reachable or when it was last seen, `Rename` in place, `Keep this Mac awake for other devices` (`Keep this computer awake for other devices` on Windows and Linux), off, under a line saying it holds only while the machine is plugged in and closing the lid still puts it to sleep, and `Remove`, confirmed in place. `Devices` holds a card per linked phone or browser: its name, its platform and app version, `Connected now` or when it was last seen, when it was linked, `This device` on the one in hand, `Rename` in place, `Linked from <device>, which you revoked. Revoke it if that device was lost.` on a device that a since-revoked device linked, and `Revoke`, confirmed in place, after which the card moves to a `Revoked` group carrying `Forget` and that device never links again. `Link a device` draws in place a code to scan and the same link as text with `Copy link`, good once for 5 minutes, then six digits both screens must show before `It matches` links the device. `Passkeys` lists each passkey with the platform that holds it and when it was added, with `Remove` confirmed in place; a passkey lets a new phone or browser link itself with no other device at hand, and `Add a passkey` is present in the web client and the phone apps and absent in the desktop app. `Shared ports on <machine>` lists the ports that machine shares with the person's other devices, the one address besides the workflow port that the console sets. A name set by `Rename` is the one every device shows. Nothing here reads a log back: the statement chain's statements are kept for the machines to verify and never shown as an activity list, and no other page lists a device or a passkey. How linking, revoking and the machines' trust work is [Spec-027 §Device registration and revocation](./027-remote-control.md#device-registration-and-revocation)'s, and the methods the page calls are [Spec-027 §Interfaces And Contracts](./027-remote-control.md#interfaces-and-contracts)'s.

##### Notifications

The notification surface composes the daemon's attention projection — items grouped by session, actionable split from informational — under [Spec-017](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md). The list shares the sessions track at the same derived width and is shown in that track's place: the two lists are never open together, opening one closes the other, and only the open one's rail button reads as expanded. Its top row is its heading and its close control; its scroll holds a `Waiting on you` group under its own header with its count, then an `Earlier` group. A group with no entries is not drawn, and with no entries at all the list shows its heading and nothing under it, with no sentence. An entry is one line — a dot in the row's own state, the title, the state word and the relative time, newest first inside each group — and the whole line is the control: a session entry switches the screen for real, a workflow-run entry opens that run in Workflows, and neither closes the list. With two or more machines each line ends with its machine's name, and pressing a line on another machine switches to that machine and opens the session. Opening the list focuses its first entry and closing it returns focus to the rail's attention control; the chords that open the sessions track, the review pane and the terminal treat this list exactly as they treat that one. Only waiting entries are counted, and they are pinned above the rest: the count is the sum of what waits on every machine, an unreachable machine's as last read, so it always equals the lines the list holds, and the app icon's count is the same figure. Finished and failed entries are read and never counted, and an entry leaves the waiting group the moment its moment resolves — the daemon's projection, never the renderer's arithmetic. There is no dismiss (an item clears when the daemon resolves it), no re-filtering by preference in the renderer, no count on a finished or failed entry, no mark on an entry, no second badge, no overlay, and nothing said inside a session; a muted session keeps every line in the list and every waiting entry in the count. Outside the window, on this machine, the operating system's notification carries the moment. Whether an entry may raise a banner is decided once, by the background service, when it writes the entry: an entry of a kind switched off, one written while `Notify me outside the app` is off, and a muted session's `Finished` or `Failed` are withheld, and a withheld entry starts nothing. Main posts only the entries left pending, and only while no console window is in front, a workflow's Notify step either way; with the app not running, the service starts the app's own program with no window to post the banner, and that process exits. Each banner carries a stable id for its moment, so a later state replaces it in place rather than standing a second one beside it, and it is withdrawn when its moment resolves, a Notify step's never; no preference gates a withdrawal, and main honors the system's do-not-disturb (§Main Process Responsibilities). The web address and the email digest set on the Notifications settings page are the two ways a moment leaves the machine.

#### Fixtures

- **One app, two compositions.** At startup the app composes either its real boundaries or their fixtures, once, and nothing past startup asks which: no surface, service or store branches on a fixture flag, and a surface cannot tell which composition it is running on.
- **Where fixtures live.** The desktop app's `fixtures/` folder holds scenario definitions and scripted data only: one `scenarios/<scenario>.ts` per scenario, shared `data/`, and `index.ts`, the catalog. A fixture implementation sits beside the real boundary it substitutes — a service's fixture beside that service, a feature's fixtures inside that feature, a store's beside the store's owner — and answers the operations that boundary has and no others. There is no fake backend, no stand-in bridge answering by method name and no generic catalog of fixture methods, and no backend method is invented for a fixture. Fixture-only types stay in the desktop app and never enter `packages/contracts`.
- **The launch.** `--fixture <scenario>` starts the app on that scenario's scripted data with no daemon behind it and nothing running, and `--fixture <scenario> --session <session-id>` also opens that session; no argument is the normal app. Main reads the arguments and checks the name against the catalog before the hidden console window is built, then hands the checked launch to that window's document as renderer switches (`webPreferences.additionalArguments`). The preload reads the switches and, in a fixture-capable build only, exposes the launch as the page global `__fixtureLaunch__` beside `desktopBridge`; `services/platform/live-bridge.ts`'s `readFixtureLaunch` reads that global, and `app/fixture/composition.ts`'s `composeFixtureLaunch` turns it into the composition `App.tsx` hands to `PlatformBridgeProvider` as its `composition`, so the provider holds no fixture branch. A launch that names a session writes that session's address before the first render, so the window opens on it as on any other address. An unknown scenario, a missing value, or a session the scenario does not hold stops startup with an error that names it; nothing falls back to another scenario. The arguments are the same through the development command and straight against Electron (`electron . --fixture transcript-states`), and the test tiers launch with them too. The pane-harness address, `#/pane-harness/<paneKind>/<sessionId>`, parses in every window: a fixture launch's composition registers the harness screen, through which the test tiers mount one registered pane body for that session, and a window whose composition registered none renders the address as not found, as it renders any other unknown address.
- **The catalog is these scenarios**, each named for the behavior it exercises and never for an incidental count: `multi-session-streaming`, which holds several sessions across more than one project, each with its transcript, and one reply that streams word by word; and the focused scenarios `first-run`, `concurrent-streaming`, `transcript-states`, `empty-session`, `waiting-for-input`, `approval-request` and `terminal-lease`, which serve development and the test tiers. A lost connection to the background service is not a scenario: its states are exercised against the real boundary that reports the service's status.
- **Which builds carry fixtures.** Fixture code and data are compiled only into a fixture-capable build, marked by the compile-time `define`-substituted identifier `__FIXTURE_BUILD__` — true in the development and fixture builds, false in a release build. A release build carries no fixture code, no catalog and no loader, so `--fixture` there is invalid input and a startup error like any other, never a silent normal launch and never a hidden demo mode, and scripted data can never be mistaken for real work. A runtime `process.env` gate would leave the code in the bundle and is not used (§Pitfalls To Avoid). The fixture flag means only that fixtures are compiled in: code meant for ordinary development alone reads Vite's own `import.meta.env.DEV`, and the scenario itself is chosen only by the launch argument — three separate things, never one flag. Main's hidden-window controls, through which the Electron test tiers launch a window that stays hidden or takes no focus, read a main-process define of their own, `__TEST_TIER_BUILD__`, true only in the `smoke` and `fixtures` build modes and never the fixture flag, so a development build's window is never hidden.
- **The console's own developer tools exist in a development build only** and are compiled out of the shipped app; they are no row on the Keyboard page, because in a shipped console there is nothing there to rebind. In a development build they open from the View menu's `Toggle Developer Tools`, on `⌥⌘J` on macOS and Ctrl+⇧J on Windows and Linux, the keys Chrome opens its console on ([Chrome DevTools: Keyboard shortcuts](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/devtools/shortcuts)), and always open the tools of the window it was chosen in, from the console's own documents and from a Preview page or its developer tools wherever they leave the key unhandled, because the console's page-chord path never claims it — except, on Windows and Linux, from the page's tools in a window of their own, where no menu key answers so that Alt+F4 closes that window; F12 is `Inspect`'s alone, so it does nothing outside Preview, and in the terminal it reaches the program. The lock covers the console's own windows and never a Preview page, whose developer tools are in every build ([§Console Design (Meridian)](#console-design-meridian), the Preview pane). A development build also carries an on-demand memory report, read from developer tools and compiled out of the shipped app: each process's memory (`app.getAppMetrics()`), the renderer heap (`process.getHeapStatistics()`), Blink's image, script, style and font caches (`webFrame.getResourceUsage()`), the node count of each window's document, and each bounded cache's own size — so a budget failure is read in one call rather than bisected.

#### Budgets

Named targets. The renderer bundle budget is measured on every console PR; the endurance and accessibility tiers land once the macOS build is complete and gate every PR from then on, the endurance figures recorded in a dated endurance ledger. **Where they are measured.** The deterministic budgets — bundle size, heap at rest, steady heap, and first-transcript-row latency against the fixture's frozen clock — are measured on any runner: bundle size on every console PR, and heap at rest, steady heap and first-transcript-row latency on every console PR from the time the macOS build is complete. The hardware-dependent budgets — frame time and CPU — are measured under one protocol: the built bundle in the desktop app playing the `concurrent-streaming` fixture scenario with the frozen clock, ten warm-up seconds discarded, the metric taken as the median of three runs, on the **pinned CI runner class** the desktop workflow names by label (the reference machine for gating purposes), with thresholds calibrated on that class at first landing with 25 % headroom, re-baselined only when the label changes, and one automatic re-run before a failure counts. The figures in the table are the product targets on a laptop-class reference profile — eight cores, integrated GPU, one 60 Hz display — which the endurance ledger records beside the runner-calibrated thresholds, so a runner regression and a product regression stay distinguishable. A budget that cannot be measured on the pinned class at all is never silently skipped. **Every figure in the table is a gate on the harness and none of them bounds the running console.** What the running console keeps — the files it has cached, the color spans the daemon computed, the pictures it has loaded and the prepared rows of sessions no window shows — is bounded instead by the budget it works out from the machine's own memory, which the `inspector` bullet of §Pane kinds describes and which is never a figure written down here.

| Budget | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Renderer initial bundle | ≤ 450 kB gzip, excluding lazy chunks (terminal, node graph, math, diagrams, browser tools) |
| Frame time, four lanes streaming | p95 ≤ 16.7 ms on the reference machine |
| Scrolling, four lanes streaming | a trackpad fling with its momentum and a wheel scroll, on every scroller but the conversation, at 120 Hz on the reference machine: while a fling moves the content, p95 ≤ 8.4 ms (one refresh) between presented frames; and each scroll input that moves the content is drawn in the next frame the window submits after the input reaches it |
| Renderer heap, one session open at rest | ≤ 120 MB |
| Renderer heap, each further window showing one session at rest | the figure Plan-020 T-020r-5-3 measures when windows land, recorded in the endurance ledger; a later change that raises it fails |
| Windows opened and closed, fifty times over | the at-rest renderer heap returns to its level before the first opened, beyond what the recent-sessions cache holds within its share, and no closed window's document, listener or session store is retained |
| Steady heap, `concurrent-streaming` live stream | flat over thirty minutes; ≤ 250 MB renderer heap |
| Idle CPU, session open, nothing streaming | ≤ 0.5 % of one core averaged over sixty seconds; no timer fires except the refresh scheduler's deadline and the presence heartbeat |
| Streaming CPU, one lane | ≤ 15 % of one core on the reference machine; the reveal engine yields when the frame budget is spent |
| Terminal instance | ≤ 20 MiB at its scrollback of 10,000 lines |
| Time to first transcript row on launch | ≤ 800 ms from window show, fixture mode |

#### Persistence on the renderer scheme

Layout, scroll position, selection, pins, and expansion sets persist per install; **drafts do not** — composer text, form values, paths, and code a user has typed and not sent are user-authored content, and the only durable homes the corpus gives such content are the daemon's PII-mapped stores ([Spec-020](./020-data-retention-export-and-deletion.md)). A draft therefore lives in exactly one of those and never in window storage: it is daemon-held and session-scoped, saved a moment after typing pauses, restored on a session switch, on a restart and on the person's other devices, and cleared by sending it. The files staged beside it are copied to the daemon at staging time and held with the session outside the checkout, and a review's held notes live in the same store, so a half-written message or review reaches the person's other devices and sending needs no upload step.

What the renderer's own store holds is device-local convenience and nothing else: for every window, which session views it holds, their order and widths and which one had focus, and each pane that sits in a window of its own, one record per window keyed by the window id main's own place file shares; pane widths, the sessions track's own width, the terminal's height, which panes each session had open with their order, which side of the conversation the block sat on and where its terminal sat, and which child the agents pane was on, which marking tool the Preview pane had pressed, the review pane's scope and base branch per session, the reader's place in a comparison and in a session, the projects the person folded, which files in the review pane they folded, the expansion sets, and which settings page was last open. Two things a window needs before the renderer runs live instead in main's own files under `userData`, each written by main alone: where each window was — its place, size, display and maximized-or-fullscreen state, one entry per window, and a pane's own window per pane kind — and the appearance record, `userData/appearance.json`, which holds the theme, the color scheme, the text size, the transcript width and the current theme's light and dark grounds. Main paints each window and its console view from the kept ground before the first frame, and stamps the record on the root element of every console document it serves — the theme, the scheme, the scheme resolved to light or dark, and an inline style carrying the root font size and the transcript-width token, `--meridian-transcript-width` — so the first paint needs no script and no read. The renderer reads its starting values from that element, sends a change through `window.setAppearance`, which writes the record, and hears every change, the View menu's scheme pick included, through `window.subscribeAppearance`; the Appearance page writes through that member, the width as the one root token every session's transcript reads its own column width from (§Meridian, the design language item 4), and the renderer's store keeps no appearance value. Every read and write is wrapped defensively and the screen renders correctly when a store is empty or throws; a view whose saved state could not be read says `Could not read what you last set here, so this view starts from its defaults.` Everything open at quit comes back after a restart and nothing more: every window where it was, with its views, their order and widths and which had focus, and every pane's own window at its kept place, so a restart never multiplies windows; a session deleted or archived since drops its view, and a window left with none shows the sessions list. A crash of the renderer takes every window with it: main closes them, and the reloaded renderer reopens every window, its views and each pane's own window from the same kept layout, as after a restart, unless the crashes repeat ([Desktop App Implementation Notes §Repeated renderer crashes](../architecture/desktop-implementation-notes.md#repeated-renderer-crashes)). A remembered pane is put back only where its subject is still there — a shell to draw, changes to read, a page or a discovered server to open, the agents list and the inspector always having one — and the memory is held until the daemon has answered about that subject: an answer that has not arrived, or one that fails, reopens nothing and erases nothing and is tried again when the daemon reports itself reachable, and an answer landing after the person has opened a pane themselves is dropped rather than replacing what they opened. Nothing is announced when a pane does not come back; the session simply opens with an empty slot. A remembered base branch that the daemon's own branch list does not hold falls back to the default base, and a store that is empty or unreadable opens the review pane on its changes scope against that default with nothing said. The remembered reading place in a session is the bottom where the reader was following it and otherwise the row they were on and how far into it, with the history around that row loaded to reach it; a place that cannot be found opens the transcript at the bottom. A saved window rectangle that is off every attached display is pulled back on screen, and the window is restored without first appearing at another size. Projections never persist and are re-derived on reconnect (§Pitfalls To Avoid). The durable store therefore holds **UI state only** — a value-class enumeration the persistence layer's schema encodes, and a write outside it (message text, form values, paths, code, names, anything user- or machine-authored) is a tripwire failure at the store's write chokepoint. That persistence lives in IndexedDB under an own per-session partition with LRU trim and a quota gauge, which exists on the renderer's custom scheme because the scheme is registered privileged (`standard: true`) before `app.ready` ([Desktop App Implementation Notes §Renderer Bundle](../architecture/desktop-implementation-notes.md#renderer-bundle); Plan-020 I-020-6).

### Auto-Update Flow

1. Main checks for an update every four hours and at app start while `Check for updates automatically` is on, and whenever the person asks. It checks through `electron-updater` 6.8.10 with its stock GitHub provider, which reads the update files the release publishes, and refuses a release whose version is not above the last one this install has seen ([Desktop App Implementation Notes §Auto-Updater](../architecture/desktop-implementation-notes.md#auto-updater))
2. On an available update, General reads `Update available — <version>, released <date>.` with `Download`. The download starts on the person's press and never on its own, and it is a block-map delta where the platform has one — NSIS, the macOS zip once a previous update is cached, and AppImage — and the whole file otherwise
3. When the download lands, `electron-updater` checks its SHA-512 against the release's update file, and on Windows the installer's publisher (`Checking the signature…`)
4. The update is ready, `Ready to install — <version>. Restart to finish.`, carried to the renderer on `update.subscribe`. On macOS the platform's updater is handed the file only when the person presses `Restart to finish`, and never before the check has passed. An update a previous run staged and never applied stays reported in the update block, naming the staged version beside the running one, until an update lands
5. `Restart to finish` (bridge `update.requestRestart()`) restarts the app at once, with no confirm: a restart is a quit, and the background service keeps every session, run and terminal shell running through it
6. After relaunch main runs the handshake with the service (`daemon.hello`), as at every start. The app does not roll itself back; a bad release is fixed by the next one. A service outside the accepted version range is not a failed handshake: the console is read-only and the working line names the side that is behind (§Daemon Supervision Lifecycle)
7. Any failed check discards the file, and General reads `The update could not be verified and was not installed.` with `Try again`; the next check fetches it again

**The background service updates on its own path**, apart from the app: Settings › Runtime's `Update the background service` and `sidekicks self-update` take the same release feed and check the downloaded program against the SHA-256 the release's manifest lists for it, and nothing more. A service update never stops work: while any is running it waits on `Waiting for running work to finish`, naming that work on one line, its parts joined by `·` — the running sessions and runs by their titles, at most three, then `and N more`, then the terminal Codex sessions inside the service as a count — with `Cancel` offered until the app asks the old service to stop. What it does and refuses is [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s.

**On a Windows computer whose Claude Code and Codex live in WSL 2**, the Windows installers carry the Linux runtime for their architecture, for glibc and for musl, and the service's Windows half installs it into the distribution, keeping the current runtime folder and the one before it. The service's own update downloads the Linux release inside the distribution, checks it against the checksum the release publishes, installs it the same way and asks the Windows half to restart on the new folder. **The runtime has its own rollback**: if the new folder's daemon sends no ready line within 10 seconds, or its `daemon.hello` fails, the Windows half starts the previous folder again, and Runtime's settled line names both versions and the reason. The Windows half itself updates with the app: it runs from a per-version folder main fills at install and update, so an app update never replaces a running file, and the new copy takes over at the next service restart.

### Daemon Supervision Lifecycle

1. App start: main looks for the background service where it listens — its socket, or on Windows the per-user named pipe `\\.\pipe\ai-sidekicks-<user SID>`, on both kinds of Windows computer — and connects; the cover reads `Connecting to the background service…` while it does
2. If no service answers, main starts one and the cover reads `Starting the background service…`. On macOS and Linux main spawns the service detached (`child_process.spawn` with `detached`), never as a child of the desktop app, because a detached child survives a quit, SIGTERM and SIGKILL (measured on Electron 44.5.1 on macOS and Electron 44.1.0 on Linux); on Linux a logout ends it unless lingering is on. Where a detached child does not survive, the service runs through the per-user service `sidekicks daemon install` sets up. On a Windows computer whose Claude Code and Codex live in WSL 2, main runs the per-user logon task, which starts the service's Windows half (`sidekicks-windows-half.exe`), which starts the daemon in the distribution with `wsl.exe --exec`. In every case main reaches the daemon through its socket (see [Desktop App Implementation Notes §Detached Service Launch](../architecture/desktop-implementation-notes.md#detached-service-launch)), and waits up to 10 seconds for `daemon.hello`
3. Versions: each app accepts its own service version and the one before it. Outside that range the console is read-only — nothing mutating is sent and subscriptions still read — and the working line names in words the side that is behind, the app or the background service, with a press that opens its fix: the app's update on Settings › General, or `Update the background service` on Settings › Runtime
4. Live: main sends `daemon.ping` only after 5 seconds with no frame from the service, so a busy link carries none, and counts the link as dead after 20 seconds with no frame; a service that exits is noticed at once, when its socket closes. A service whose link died that way was never asked to stop, so it gets SIGTERM at once, which starts its drain, and SIGKILL only if it still runs once the drain bound and 2 seconds have passed (6 seconds), the same full drain bound and 2 seconds a stop gives it. The link's state reaches the renderer as the `daemon.status` topic on `daemon.subscribe`, and while the link is down the composer's working line is the connection line
5. Service lost: main starts it again with backoff (100 ms, 300 ms, 1 s, 3 s, 10 s); after five failed attempts it stops trying and the console shows the persistent state of §Fallback Behavior. On a WSL computer the Windows half restarts the daemon with the same backoff and, after the fifth failure, writes down why the service cannot start; main checks the Windows half's per-user mutex, and when no one holds it, runs the logon task at once rather than waiting for the task's own one-minute restart
6. Quit: main sends `daemon.flush`, exits when the flush answers or after ten seconds, and leaves the service, every run and every terminal shell running, and the app reconnects to them when it opens again. Only Settings › Runtime's `Stop` and `Restart` (`daemon.stop`, `daemon.restart`) end work in flight, and no surface explains that the work outlives the window. A service started from the command line is untouched either way
7. Stop and restart: after the flush and the request, main waits up to the service's drain bound (4 seconds: 2 for its terminals, 2 for the terminal host) for it to exit, and only a service still running after that gets SIGTERM and, 2 seconds later, SIGKILL
8. Restore: Runtime's `Restore…` (`daemon.requestRestore(backupId)`) goes through main, because the service cannot replace its own store while it runs. Main stops the service, its confirm naming the work that stops as `Stop`'s does, replaces the database, the settings file, the workspaces, the checkpoint copies, the conversation files and the agent memory folder with the backup's, and starts the service again. A backup is a plain copy that holds no credential: a restore on another computer reads it directly, and the person then signs in to providers and links devices again; a restore on the machine that wrote it needs nothing more. `sidekicks db restore <backup>` does the same on a machine with no app, and is refused while the service holds the data folder

The Runtime settings page is the one console surface that drives this lifecycle by hand, and the one that shows what the supervisor above reports. What its stop, its restart and its separate daemon update do, what each of them refuses, and how the daemon's machine-wide values are set — one place per value and no second one — are [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s and are not restated here; the page reads those facts and renders them, and edits none of them anywhere else.

## Default Behavior

- The desktop app connects to the background service at app start (starting it if none answers, per [Spec-006 §Default Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#default-behavior)) and exposes renderer-accessible capabilities via the preload bridge per §Trust Stance; the renderer is not a direct daemon client
- The window draws one quiet cover until the service has answered — the working indicator and one line saying what is happening, `Starting the background service…` when the app started it and `Connecting to the background service…` when it found one running, with nothing behind it drawn half-painted — then fades it away for the rest of the run and never draws it again
- Update checks are **on** by default (`Check for updates automatically`), and the person may turn them off on General; a download starts only on the person's press, and an update installs only at `Restart to finish`
- Crash reports are **kept** by default (`Keep crash reports`, on): a report is built on the machine that crashed, stripped of personal data there and kept there, and nothing sends it anywhere; the person's linked devices read it through Remote Control (`daemon.crashList`), and `sidekicks crash list` reads it from the command line; off, no report is kept
- Notifications are **on** by default: `Show a count on the app icon` and `Notify me outside the app`, and under the second its four kinds — `Waiting on you`, `Finished`, `Failed` and `Notify steps` — each on, and grayed with `Turned off above.` while `Notify me outside the app` is off; all of them are kept per device in the machine's settings file ([Spec-017 §Default Behavior](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md#default-behavior)). A session's own `Mute notifications` (`session.mute`, undone by `Unmute notifications`, `session.unmute`) silences that session's `Finished` and `Failed` on every channel and every device, and never `Waiting on you` or a Notify step
- Deep-link protocol handler registration is performed on first launch

## Fallback Behavior

- If the background service cannot be reached after five backoff attempts: at boot, one quiet card in the middle of the window in place of an empty console — `The background service is not answering.` and `Retry`, which asks main to start the service (`daemon.requestStart()`), and nothing else. Once the console has drawn, the same fact belongs to the working line — `Connection lost`, then `Reconnecting…` while the connection is being retried and `Not connected.` once it has stopped, with `Retry` at the line's right — so the card never appears over a session a person was reading. Everything under it stays as it was: the draft keeps its text, the panes stay open, no control is disabled, and a message sent meanwhile waits on its own row reading `Waiting to be delivered`; a message whose delivery has actually failed turns its waiting row red, reading `Not delivered · Retry`, and the press tries to send it again from that row. On Windows, where the service has written down why it cannot start, the card and Settings › Runtime say why instead
