- If a live stream that reopens on its own is down, whether it never opened or dropped after it had: the surface it feeds reads one line, `Live updates paused · Reconnecting…`, and the line goes the moment the stream is open again. That is the session's own streams, Providers' account stream, General's settings stream, MCP servers' status stream and the Runs tab's run subscription; a lost connection to the background service is not this line but the working line's `Connection lost`
- If the service is neither the app's own service version nor the one before it: the console is read-only, and the working line names the side that is behind with a press that opens its fix (§Daemon Supervision Lifecycle)
- If the auto-updater cannot reach the feed: skip this cycle; retry on schedule; do not block normal operation
- If a write from a settings page is refused: the control rolls back to the value it had, and the page renders the daemon's own refusal in place as an inline strip carrying `Try again` as a faint clickable word at the right end of its text line — no box, no icon, and no toast anywhere on that screen. Nothing ever draws as saved
- If the machine's settings file is malformed: the background service, its one writer, reads the defaults, repairs the file, and the page that reads it says both happened. A malformed keyboard map or appearance record reads as the defaults and is repaired by main, which writes both, and the Keyboard page says both happened of the map
- If this install cannot update itself: the General settings page draws no update button at all, says so in place, and names the command that gets the newest version instead
- If a chord a rebind records is already held by another act: the Keyboard page refuses it on the row and names the act that holds it; the row keeps the chord it had
- If the daemon is unreachable while Settings is open: every page keeps its entry in the list, every page but Runtime reads `The background service is not answering, so nothing on this page can be saved right now. Every control below keeps the value it had, and the page says what it could not read.`, and every control keeps the value it had rather than emptying or disappearing

## Interfaces And Contracts

### Renderer → Main Process → Daemon (via Preload Bridge)

- Wire format between renderer and main process: Electron IPC over `contextBridge`-exposed functions; serialization via structured clone
- Wire format between main process and daemon: JSON-RPC 2.0 with Content-Length framing per Spec-006 and ADR-009, over the service's socket — on Windows the per-user named pipe `\\.\pipe\ai-sidekicks-<user SID>`
- The main process is a transparent forwarder for the daemon contract — no method rewriting, only auth-header attachment, response-payload sanitization and the file-token swap below
- Main's own calls on the service: `daemon.hello`, the handshake at each connect; `daemon.ping`, sent only after 5 seconds with no frame from the service, the link counting as dead after 20 seconds with no frame; and `daemon.flush` before every quit. The link's state reaches the renderer as the `daemon.status` topic on `daemon.subscribe`, which carries the version range too: each app accepts its own service version and the one before it, and outside that range the console is read-only and the working line names the side that is behind. On Windows the topic also carries `cannotStart {reason: wslNotResponding | restartsExhausted {error} | placeGone | connectionNameTaken}`, which main reads, when the pipe is absent, from the status file the service's Windows half writes whenever its state changes (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\ai-sidekicks\run\service-status.json`), trusting it only while the Windows half holds its per-user mutex. On Windows and macOS it carries `whileSignedOut: on | off | passwordOutOfDate | notOffered | waitingForApproval | turnedOffInLoginItems`, for the service that keeps running while the person is signed out of Windows or logged out of the Mac, `waitingForApproval` and `turnedOffInLoginItems` being macOS's alone ([Spec-006 §Interfaces And Contracts](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#interfaces-and-contracts)).
- The machine-settings members carry the service's own verbs, because the service is the settings file's one writer: `machineSettings.read()` is `daemon.machineSettingsRead`, `machineSettings.write(change)` hands the change to `daemon.machineSettingsUpdate {change}`, answered with the file as written, and `machineSettings.subscribe(handler)` is `daemon.machineSettingsSubscribe`, whose first delivery is the current file (§State And Data Implications)
- Files cross the bridge as tokens, never as paths: `native.showOpenDialog` and `native.showSaveDialog` return tokens; a file dropped on the composer has its path read in the preload with `webUtils.getPathForFile`, and main mints its token; a pasted picture is written by main to a temporary file and handed back as a token; main's relay turns a token into the path the daemon needs, and a path a daemon reply offers reaches the renderer as a token main minted for it. No path string acts as a capability on the bridge
- The daemon calls main for what only main can do on the desktop, through the Preview page verbs (`preview.page*`): among them `preview.pageCookiesRead`; `preview.pageCookiesClear`, which answers `browser.siteCookiesClear {origin}` with `ses.clearData({dataTypes: ['cookies'], …})`; and `preview.pageSiteDataClear {origins?}`, which answers `Forget` (`browser.siteDataForget`) for one registrable domain, and `browser.siteDataClear` with no origins, through `ses.clearData`, because main owns the Preview partition and a per-origin clear leaves a parent-domain cookie. `browser.siteDataList` marks each site `hasCookies`, which Settings › Browser reads as `Saved cookies` where at least one unexpired cookie is kept and `Saved site data` otherwise
- Members main answers itself, with no daemon behind them: `window.setAppearance({theme, scheme, textSize, transcriptWidth}, grounds: {light, dark})` writes main's appearance record, which main stamps on every console document it serves, and `window.subscribeAppearance` carries the record back to every open console window (§Persistence on the renderer scheme); `keyboardMap.read()` and `keyboardMap.write()`, over the keyboard map's own file; and `native.getNotificationPermission()`, which on macOS reads the notification permission through `notify-status` (MIT), whose read resolves a promise and never blocks main — `granted` (authorized or provisional) reads as allowed, `denied` as refused, `notDetermined` as not asked yet, and `unsupported` (a process with no bundle id, or no reader) as unsupported. That library is used because Electron 44 has no read of the macOS notification permission — its `Notification` class offers `isSupported()` alone, and `systemPreferences.getMediaAccessStatus` covers only the microphone, the camera and the screen — and a native module of our own would be code to maintain forever. It was chosen over `node-mac-permissions`, whose newest release (2.5.0) has no notification read, compiles from source on every install and blocks its caller with no time limit, and over `macos-notification-state`, which reads Do Not Disturb and the screen lock but not the permission; `notify-status` ships prebuilt binaries with build provenance and gives up after 2 seconds

### Renderer → Main Process → Control Plane (via Preload Bridge)

- Path: main forwards each renderer control-plane call, unchanged, to the background service's own `controlPlane.call` over its own connection to the daemon, and the service sends it to the control plane — tRPC v11 over HTTPS for request/response (incl. relay negotiation), and the relay WSS connection speaking the relay's binary wire frames — per ADR-013 and [Spec-027](./027-remote-control.md). The service takes the call as `controlPlane.call {procedure, input}` on its local socket; `procedure` is one of `device.list`, `device.linkStart`, `device.linkRedeem`, `device.link`, `device.linkCancel`, `device.rename`, `device.revoke`, `device.forget`, `device.statementList`, `device.pushAddressSet`, `runtimenode.rename` and `runtimenode.remove`, and relay negotiation or any other procedure is refused.
- Main attaches no control-plane credential and holds none: the service presents this machine's access token under `Authorization: DPoP` with a fresh DPoP proof on each call, and the renderer never sees either. Relay negotiation (`negotiateRelay` → `RelayNegotiationResponse.connectionToken`) and the relay `connectionToken` are the service's too, so no control-plane token crosses the preload bridge to the untrusted renderer ([Preload Bridge Contract](../architecture/contracts/preload-bridge-contract.md) forbidden-surface list; renderer-untrusted §Trust Stance). The desktop app reaches the person's other machines as its machine, never as a linked device: the service, which holds this machine's keys and tokens, negotiates and authenticates each channel to another machine, and main forwards the renderer's calls to it and holds none of them. A procedure the control plane defines as anonymous is sent with no credential while the service holds none. Anonymous means authentication is not _required_, not that a held credential must be stripped: the relay counts its sign-in routes per source address, so a present credential changes neither admission nor throttling

### Main Process ↔ Auto-Update Feed

- Transport: HTTPS from the project's GitHub Releases, read through `electron-updater` 6.8.10 with its stock GitHub provider (§Auto-Update Flow; [Desktop App Implementation Notes §Auto-Updater](../architecture/desktop-implementation-notes.md#auto-updater))
- Artifact format: macOS `.dmg` to install and `.zip` for updates, arm64 and x64; NSIS per user on Windows x64 and arm64; AppImage on Linux x64 and arm64; and `.deb` and `.rpm`, which on install add the project's signed apt or dnf repository, hosted on Cloudsmith, and which the package manager updates and the app never does. There is no Snap, no Flatpak, no MSI and no staged rollout. Each release carries `electron-builder`'s update files, which list each update file's SHA-512, and a manifest listing every file's SHA-256
- Signature: `electron-updater`'s SHA-512 check of the download, and the operating system's code signature: Squirrel.Mac's designated requirement on macOS, and the installer's publisher on Windows (`verifyUpdateCodeSignature`); a release whose version is not above the last one seen is refused
- What the General settings page reads is this channel and nothing else: one subscription and one read over main's own update state, beside the facts the app reports about itself — its version, its platform, its architecture and its locale. The preferences on that page that carry no wire of their own — the editor that opens files, the default checkout for a new project session, the new-session switch, the keep-awake switch, `Keep crash reports` and the auto-update preference — live in the machine's settings file, which the background service writes (§State And Data Implications)

## State And Data Implications

- Where each window was, how big it was, which display it was on and whether it was maximized or fullscreen are remembered across restarts in main's own file under `userData`, one entry per window keyed by the window id the renderer's kept layout shares, and a pane's own window per pane kind, read before the window is built (§Console Design (Meridian) §Persistence on the renderer scheme); not sensitive
- The machine's own settings file, `<home>/.ai-sidekicks/machine-settings.json`, has the background service as its one writer: main and every other device write through the service's `daemon.machineSettingsUpdate`, and every console window reads through the service's live read. Main alone may read the file directly, and only before the service first answers, for the two values it needs at start, the auto-update preference and `Keep crash reports`; the renderer never reads it. It holds the auto-update preference, `Keep crash reports`, the editor preference, the default checkout, the new-session switch, the last model and effort the person used, which a new session opens on, Claude Code's default advisor (`advisorModel`), the two keep-awake switches (General's `keepAwakeWhileAgentWorks` and Devices' `keepAwakeForOtherDevices`), the notification preferences (the switches, the kinds, and the web address's and the email digest's settings other than their secrets), the browser's switches, the screen-reader switch, the environment rows for every project, the backup switch and the backup folder, the folder new clones go into, the `Branch names` pattern, and voice's two settings, the mode and the call voice. The folders this machine can reach are not in it; they are the service's. One schema in `packages/contracts` describes it for every reader. The service reads it each time it starts something a value governs — the headless browser, a provider process, a project session, a project's process, a Terminal pane's shell, a clone, a Codex voice call — and keeps no copy. Every write is an atomic rename over a schema-checked parse with fail-closed defaults, so a half-written file is never read back and a malformed one reads as the defaults, is repaired by the service, and is reported on the page that reads it. Everything else a settings page shows — the accounts, the tool servers, the projects, the browser's site data and the daemon's own facts — is the daemon's, read and written over its wire and never kept as a second copy in main or the renderer. Credential material is never in it
- The screen-reader switch is in that file because it reaches beyond the window: the service reads it when it starts a Terminal pane's shell and sets `CLAUDE_AX_SCREEN_READER=1` there alone
- The keyboard map is its own file, `userData/keyboard-map.json`, owned by main, written whole and atomically over a schema-checked parse through `keyboardMap.write()`, and readable and writable by this person alone; it holds only the rows that differ from the chords the app ships with, keyed by act, and is never written to a wire
- The appearance record is its own file, `userData/appearance.json`, under the same contract as the keyboard map: the theme, the color scheme, the text size, the transcript width and the current theme's light and dark grounds, every value an enum or a number, written by main through `window.setAppearance` and stamped on every console document main serves; a missing or broken file reads as the defaults and main repairs it, and an unreadable file or a failed repair reads as the defaults and is recorded in main's diagnostic log
- The updater's record is its own file, `userData/updater-state.json`, owned by main: the last release version seen, read and written whether or not the service is running, application state and never a setting, written by no wire; a missing or broken file reads as the installed version
- Console layout, scroll position, selection, pins, and expansion sets — UI state only, never user-authored content: a draft, its staged files and a review's held notes are daemon-held and session-scoped and never enter this store, so they are covered by the daemon's own [Spec-020](./020-data-retention-export-and-deletion.md) selectors rather than by a renderer one — persisted **per install** in IndexedDB on the privileged renderer scheme (§Console Design (Meridian) §Persistence on the renderer scheme) — never a projection, which is re-derived on reconnect; this is the one renderer-storage tier the app has, it holds no auth material, and it exists because the scheme is registered `standard: true` before `app.ready` (Plan-020 I-020-6)
- Main keeps no credential material and no keystore entry: this machine's DPoP key, the hosted account's tokens and every other secret are the daemon's. No credential is in the machine's settings file, main's own files or the renderer's storage
- The daemon session token is read from its file at every connect and held in main-process memory only; it is rotated on daemon restart per [Security Architecture §Local Daemon Authentication](../architecture/security-architecture.md#local-daemon-authentication)
- Crash reports are built and kept on the machine that crashed, stripped of personal data there, read through `daemon.crashList`, and deleted with the diagnostic logs; with `Keep crash reports` off, none is kept
- Update artifact cache: main-owned; cleared after a successful apply or after a configurable retention window

## Example Flows

- `Example: First run.` Main finds no background service answering and starts one detached, drawing `Starting the background service…` over the window until `daemon.hello` completes. There is no welcome wizard and no sign-in gate: the cover fades onto the sessions list, which reads `No sessions yet. Press New session to start with a chat or a project.`, and the only sign-in the console offers is a provider account's, on Settings › Providers.

- `Example: Auto-update applied.` Main checks the feed on schedule and finds a version above the last one seen; General reads `Update available — <version>, released <date>.` The person presses `Download`; the file lands and passes its hash check, and the block reads `Ready to install — <version>. Restart to finish.` The person presses `Restart to finish`; the app restarts at once while the background service and every session, run and terminal shell keep running; the new app's handshake with the service succeeds, and General shows the new version.

- `Example: Background service crash mid-session.` The service exits abnormally; its socket closes and main notices at once. The working line reads `Connection lost` and `Reconnecting…`, and a message the person sends meanwhile waits on its own row reading `Waiting to be delivered`. Main starts the service again with backoff; on recovery the service rebuilds from its event log, the renderer resubscribes and re-derives its projections, the working line goes back to what it showed before, the waiting message is delivered, and the transcript catches up.

## Signature Feature Composition Sketches

Each V1 Signature Feature view must compose daemon and control-plane state via the preload bridge. The owning plan listed in parentheses holds the feature's behavior; the renderer is a read-and-steer projection of that behavior, not the source of truth. The section also carries settings pages that compose under the identical rule without themselves being Signature Features; each such view says so in its own `Note`.

### Transcript View — the "everything happens here" surface (→ Plan-010 Transcript And Reasoning)

- Data sources: the daemon's transcript reads through the bridge, `transcript.read` for history and the session's one stream, `session.subscribe`, catching up from the last position the window kept and then following in the daemon's batched frames (per Spec-011). The transcript names no connected device: which devices are connected shows on the Devices page in Settings and nowhere in a session.
- Renders: chronological event stream (messages, tool calls, approvals, diffs, agent reasoning, interventions, state transitions) per Spec-011. A dispatch is one block whose header reads `dispatched 1 sidekick` or `dispatched <N> sidekicks`, then `, <M> more by them` where the tree is nested, then `· waiting on <N>` while the agent that dispatched them waits on its children, dropping those words in place when the wait ends; each child is one line on the tool rail reading `<name> · <model> · <effort>`, the effort one word from the one ladder, named whether the dispatch chose them or the child inherited them, and, for a peer call, `via <agent>` after them as plain muted text, never a pill
- Interactions: scroll-to-tail, the ask rail at the transcript's right edge (§Non-Goals), and the actions on the person's own messages — Copy, Fork, Edit and `Undo to here` (bullet below); no jump by event id, no filter by actor or type, and no replay from a row, since the transcript is the replay and a person scrolls it
- Fork on the person's own message asks once, directly under that message, naming how many turns the new session carries, the new worktree — named from the message, off the current one at its current commit — its base commit, and that the session's level and tools carry over; a chat's fork stays a chat and names no worktree. Confirming calls `session.fork` and opens the new session
- Edit and undo on the person's own message: every message the person sent carries, on hover or on focus inside it, Copy, Fork, Edit and `Undo to here`, on both providers, through a running turn, and never absent. The pencil puts the message, with everything it carried, back into the draft, and its row holds its place reading `Editing… · 4 turns after this will be superseded`, followed, when the undo's dry run finds files to put back, by its count (`Put back 3 files · 41 lines · 1 skipped`); files another session also changed since that point are listed and left as they are unless the person presses the include line; Enter puts the conversation and the files back to the moment before that message exactly as `Undo to here` does, then sends the edited message, and Escape closes the editor at once on a message whose words are unchanged; on one whose words changed, `Discard changes?` appears under the box with `Keep editing` and `Discard`, and the edit stays until `Discard` is pressed, with no dialog. The composer holds one edit at a time: a second pencil press puts the first row back and takes the second, and a session switch or the message's own disappearance puts the row back to its words and leaves what was typed in that session's daemon-held draft. While a resend is in flight the turns it will supersede dim together and stay readable and pressable, and once it lands they stay in the flow marked superseded. A resend that fails puts the edited message back in the composer with everything it carried, leaves the conversation as it was, and lands one system message naming the cause, `Resend failed · Connection lost`; where the cut had already landed, that row says the conversation went back and the resend did not. `Undo to here` rolls the conversation and the files back to the moment before that message, together, through the daemon's own checkpointer: it shows the dry run's count first — `Put back 3 files · 41 lines · 1 skipped`, the skipped file and its reason in the count's hover title — and asks in place, showing no count when the dry run finds no file to put back, and afterwards the flow carries `Restored to before <the message's first words>` with the turns after that point marked superseded; an undo that applied only in part names the part that did in those words and the part that did not with its cause, `Restored to before <the message's first words> · files not restored · <cause>`. The undo and the pencil's resend ask first when agents or commands started after that point are still running, naming each count from the daemon's own tree: `Undo to here? The 2 sidekicks and 1 command started after this will stop.` Esc Esc with an empty draft and no turn running opens the same undo as a rewind menu in place above the composer, one row per message the person sent, offering the conversation and the files together, the conversation alone, or the files alone. The dry run is `session.restorePreview` and the undo is `session.restore`, both the daemon's ([Spec-003](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md)); this view renders their result and never re-derives it
- Question card: an agent's question — Claude Code's held user-question request or Codex's user-input request, recorded as `question.asked` — is not a row of this surface. It is the question card in the composer, in the place the approval card takes, so exactly one card ever waits on a person at a time: the eyebrow reads question with the agent's short header beside it, the ask is the title, its structured options are rows carrying a mark, a label, a description and a number key on the first nine, the row the agent marked recommended stays where the agent put it and carries a recommended mark with the provider's own suffix stripped from its label, and a dashed something-else row with a text field stands last, so the free-text answer arm is **unconditional** ([Spec-011 §Transcript Entry Types](011-transcript-and-reasoning.md#transcript-entry-types)). Several asks page one at a time with the count beside the eyebrow, picks and typed text surviving the paging both ways and every answer going back together on the last page. A permission request is not drawn as a question at all — it is an approval and takes the same place under §Approvals View; a question and an approval are different records, so exactly one card renders any request. Answers go back through `question.resolve`, carrying per question the picked labels, the typed text, the secret (never stored) or a skip, and the card closes when its attention entry on `attention.projectionRead` resolves. The daemon holds the request open with no timer, so the card carries no countdown, no expiry and no expired state: it waits until it is answered, is rebuilt on reload and on every linked device with the first answer settling it everywhere, and is canceled with an interrupted turn. Answering lands one turn of the person's own carrying, per ask, the question as a faint quoted line with the answer under it, and that turn is the record — it cannot be answered again. Plan-010 owns the card and its states; this view renders that projection
- Restart question card: a run halted after a restart on [Spec-013 §Fallback Behavior](013-persistence-and-recovery.md#fallback-behavior)'s question is drawn as a waiting card where the run stopped, in the place the approval card takes, carrying that question and its two choices in Spec-013's words and answered through `run.recoveryResolve`; the run takes an entry in the attention list, in amber because a person is needed.
- The surface the rows sit on is the `transcript` pane kind (§Pane kinds), which fixes the flow's single width, its derived row gap — one token, `--row-gap`, half the reply's own reading line (`--sm` over 1.65), between every two consecutive rows whatever they are, with no row kind keeping a margin of its own and the stamp under a reply sitting at half the gap — its history paging, its drawn window, and the one path every jump to a row takes. Four surfaces may cover it: the plan reader, the command palette and the quick look over a picture or a PDF, each on a light scrim with the transcript and the composer still running underneath, and the find box, drawn on the surface's own corner with no scrim and nothing beneath it moving. Nothing else is drawn over the transcript.
- Copying out: the palette's copy-conversation act puts the whole open session on the clipboard in the form the screen reads — the same markdown a reply copies and the same plain text a message copies, in the order they are drawn — and the per-message and per-reply copy controls stay. Copy on a reply writes two flavors in one clipboard write, the markdown and a formatted flavor beside it, taken from the words the agent wrote and nothing else: the authored text blocks joined by a blank line, with the tool rows, their output, the thinking and every transport detail left out. Copying part of a reply puts the markdown that made it on the clipboard, rebuilt from what was selected, with a formatted flavor beside it; Copy on the person's own message writes plain text and nothing else. `⌘C` with nothing selected in the message box copies what is selected in the conversation, joined in the order it is read; with something selected in the box it copies that. Nothing exports, prints, publishes or reads a transcript aloud; those two are the only ways a transcript leaves the screen.
- State handling: one surface that tails live and pages history through the `transcript` pane kind, with no second mode; local projection cache invalidation on daemon reconnect
- Owning plan: Plan-010

### Session Composer — the chrome every session view carries (→ Plan-020 Desktop App And Renderer)

- Data sources: the driver capability read and the provider command-and-skill enumeration through `bridge.daemon.call(…)`, both node-local and both scoped to the session's own provider, account and model binding ([Spec-004 §The provider command and skill surface](004-provider-driver-contract-and-capabilities.md#the-provider-command-and-skill-surface)); the context figure the Plan-010 surfaces this spec mounts already carry; and the draft, its staged files and the review notes being held, all three daemon-held and session-scoped rather than kept in window storage.
- **One agent, one composer — and one way to write to another session.** The card addresses the session's own agent, and the only other thing it can address is another session, which the person names with an `@` at the head of the draft (§Commands below). There is no target picker, no machine picker and no posture control anywhere on it, and nothing on it attaches an agent, a provider or a model to a running session: another agent takes part only when the session's own agent delegates to it, or when the person names one from the Sidekicks group of the command list, which inserts the provider's own form for addressing it. A change to the provider, the model, the reasoning effort or the output speed is one configuration mutation on that agent (`agent.configUpdate`); a spawn-bound one applies when the run ends, so it is acknowledged as pending rather than drawn as already in effect, and its `pending` / `applied` / `degraded` / `failed` disposition is rendered from the daemon's answer ([Spec-014 §Same-Agent Provider Switch](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#same-agent-provider-switch)). The account a session runs on is not on this card; the inspector's account fact reads it and switches nothing, and it moves on the Providers settings page, or by `/account <name>` typed in the session, which presses that same switch: the named account becomes the provider's current one and every unpinned session on that provider moves to it, this one included ([Spec-025 §Moving a session to another account](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#moving-a-session-to-another-account)). What a run may do on its own is the permission level on the toolbar below, which applies from the next turn, and the composer carries no second reading of it.
- **Every capability gate is the daemon's own report.** The composer derives none of them. A control the session's provider, account or model cannot run is absent rather than grayed — the levels the level control lists, the rungs the effort control offers, the compaction popover behind the context ring — on the fail-closed-projection discipline `I-002-18` states, since a disabled control would assert the capability exists and is momentarily unavailable, which would be false. The one named exception is the output-speed control, which is drawn on every session and grays in place where the chosen model has no fast output (§Rules every console surface obeys), its reason both in its own hover title and in one small faint line beside the composer's chips naming that model — the line following the model control, appearing and going as the chip grays and lights, and folding away with the chips on a card too narrow to hold them. Where the provider says fast output is not on for a turn that asked for it, one flow row reads `Fast output is not available on this model · running at standard speed` and the control reads `Standard` until fast output is on, its value alone: no suffix, bracket or mark is ever added to a chip.
- State handling: the command-and-skill enumeration is a **live read held for the session's current binding and nothing longer** — not persisted, not cached across sessions, and re-read rather than patched when the provider announces that its own set changed, which is what keeps the list from offering a definition the running provider no longer has. The compaction popover's completed state renders only on the daemon's own compaction row rather than on the call returning.
- **The card.** A floating card at the transcript's own content width, read from the same root token the transcript column reads so the two move together at every width and every text size, docked one gutter's gap off the bottom with the gutters beside it still clickable. Its children run top to bottom: a hairline that takes the strong edge color while the session is stopped; the worktree setup card while one runs, its `Edit setup steps` link opening Settings › Projects on that project; the working line; the approvals card while a request waits; the composer proper — the command list, the attachment row, the draft line with send at its end, and the toolbar; and the session strip, which carries the project chip and the worktree switcher on a project session — on a chat session the worktree chip is absent rather than disabled and the strip reads `Session workspace` beside its `Attach a repo` control — what the worktree switcher shows, offers and refuses being [Spec-008 §Required Behavior](./008-worktree-lifecycle-and-execution-modes.md#required-behavior)'s — and, right-aligned, the agents badge with the robot glyph and its count — hidden while nothing was dispatched, the live count while any agent runs, the total dimmed once none is live, and in the accent while any child waits on an approval. The empty draft's placeholder reads `Ask, think out loud, or paste something… Shift+Enter for a new line` on a chat session and `Message the sidekick… Shift+Enter for a new line, @ to mention a file` on a project session, voice's own placeholders standing in its place while voice is on. A press on the card's own empty space puts the caret in the message box, unless one of the card's menus is open, in which case the press closes that menu and the closing hands the caret back. Entering a session puts the caret in the message box, once for that session: never taken from a shell that already has it, never from a session that is not the one in view, and not again on a session the person comes back to. On a settled session a bare printable key that nothing else has claimed puts the caret at the end of the draft and lands there, so typing is enough to start a message; it does nothing while a modifier is held, while a menu, list or overlay is open, while the shell has focus, and while the question card is waiting on its number keys. Focusing the draft strengthens the card's border; the message box has no focus ring of its own. The draft grows from one line to the smaller of about eight lines of the current text size and about a third of the transcript's visible height, and scrolls inside itself past that; the transcript's bottom padding is republished to the card's measured height plus its gap whenever the card resizes, so the newest row stays readable above it, and a growing approvals strip, command list or setup card pushes the transcript up rather than covering it.
- **The working line** reads, left to right: the activity indicator; the action words; then, from the moment the turn starts, the elapsed clock and the token figure, each behind its own separator; then, each with its own separator and only while it has something to say, the state slot, the tasks word, the count of unanswered questions, the warnings word — `⚠ 1 warning` or `⚠ N warnings`, pressable, present only while the session's provider has reported a warning — a Codex warning or deprecation, or Claude Code's one informational warning — and, only while a Codex turn is held for a safety check and Codex names a faster model, `Retry with a faster model`, which asks before it acts; then the pause word and the interrupt word. There is no "working" word — the indicator and the action words carry it. The line sits at the head of the card above the draft and is drawn whenever it has something to say — the connection line and the warnings word on an idle session too — and is absent on an idle session that has nothing to say and while a plan waits; the card grows upward for it, so the draft never moves when a run starts or ends, and it is never an empty reserved strip. Nothing in the row wraps, and the action words are the only part that truncates, with their full text in their own hover title. The action words say what the agent is doing now, hold the last step's words while reasoning streams between steps, read as thinking before the turn's first step has landed, stay faint after a pause or an interrupt, and read as sending from the press of send until the turn starts, with no clock and no token figure beside them then. They read as compacting while a compaction runs, in place of the verb, and on a session with no live turn the line appears for that duration and hides again; and they read as retrying while the provider is retrying a call of its own, the clock still running and the token figure holding where it stands. While any command runs they are pressable and open the running-commands list in place above the line: one entry per command in the order they started, each with a live timer, its own stop word — the same word that command's transcript row carries — and a live tail of its output that follows the bottom and pauses while it is scrolled up, its height and its fold [Spec-011 §Required Behavior](./011-transcript-and-reasoning.md#required-behavior)'s, with a last row, `Stop all commands`, that stops every running command in the session and touches no agent. The list never runs under the session header: it stops short of it and scrolls inside itself when its entries and their tails need more room. The tail is a window onto the command's own transcript row and never a second copy of its output, and the line itself never counts commands. The token figure reads a down arrow and the tokens received this turn, starting at zero when the turn starts, stepping up once per model round rather than per word, holding across a pause, muting with the clock after a stop, and hidden while the line is the connection line; no other live token figure stands anywhere on the screen. The state slot holds one reading at a time and is absent with its separator when there is none — thinking while reasoning streams, pausing, paused, or interrupted with what is still running after it. Because the slot sits after the clock, nothing before the clock changes on a pause or an interrupt: the indicator freezes, the clock and the token figure mute where they stand, the action words fade, and the clock never moves again. The tasks word is drawn only while the turn has a task list and opens that list in place above the line, one list open at a time with the running-commands list. A lost connection turns the indicator amber, swaps the action words for the connection state, puts the reconnecting or not-connected wording after the clock, hides the token figure, the state slot, the tasks word and the controls, and puts the retry control at the right — all at the row's own height and type, so nothing grows.
- **Commands.** A leading slash or currency character opens the command list, which assistive technology reads as a list of choices and which stays open only while the draft holds no newline and a row still matches what is typed; a word no row matches closes the list rather than refusing the word, because that word is going to the provider as typed. While it is open Tab completes the highlighted row, the arrows move the selection and wrap, Enter runs or completes it and Escape closes it; with it closed Tab and Shift+Tab traverse the screen as they do everywhere else, so the box never swallows the key. It, and the mention list, also close when the draft loses focus, when a press lands outside them and when a card opens over the box, completing and sending nothing and leaving the draft's text as it was. Its order is the console's own words first, each with one line of help and its caught spellings matched but never drawn as rows, then on a Claude Code session every other word that provider's frame offers, marked as the provider's and sent as typed, then every skill this session's provider can run, with its origin mark, then a Sidekicks group under its own heading whose row inserts the provider's own form for addressing one, then one group per working tool server under the server's own name holding the prompts it publishes. `/agents` is a caught spelling of `/sidekicks`: it is never drawn as a row, it presses the Sidekicks pane, and the typed word is replaced, not sent. The console's own words are intercepted in the client and never forwarded as text: most press a console control, and the five bridges — `/goal`, `/review`, `/reload`, `/btw` and `/voice` — do their work through the daemon on the provider's own verb. `/output-style` and `/advisor` are Claude Code's own words, which the daemon answers for that session alone with `apply_flag_settings`, changing that session's output style or advisor and leaving the Providers page's default and every settings file untouched; typed with no style or no model, each names the session's own choice and the choices beside it on the one line beside the message box, and after `/output-style <style>` or `/advisor <model>` the provider's own answer shows on that same line word for word, with no row added to the conversation. That line, cleared by the next keystroke, carries the console's own answers and nothing else: these, and a compaction the person asked for that did not run. Three cases are sent as typed instead, pressing nothing, and the provider's own answer is what shows, with no line of the console's: a console word whose control has nothing to act on in this session — `/effort` on a model with no effort levels, `/fast` on a model with no fast output, `/fork` before anything is said, `/diff` with nothing changed, `/background` with no command waiting — and a console word given an argument its control cannot take — `/effort turbo`, a model not on the chip, `/workflow` with an unknown verb — and a console word that cannot run right now — `/compact` while a turn runs. A word on no list is sent as typed and the provider answers it as it would in its own terminal; there is no escape for a literal slash and no unknown-command row. A word that does work of its own lands one small quiet system message in the transcript; a word that presses a control lands exactly what that control's own press lands, which for most is no row at all. On a Codex session, which never asks a tool server for its prompts, the daemon lists and reads each working server's prompts through its own client, a server behind a sign-in on the service's own sign-in, so the tool-server groups in the list work the same on both providers. The arrows move and wrap, tab completes, Enter runs or completes, and Escape closes the list for the word it was open on and leaves the draft as it is. The draft draws its text plainly — no word in it is tinted, underlined or marked — because the open list is where a recognized word is confirmed. A file mention opens a ranked search of the working folder's files, run on the machine that holds them rather than in this window, waiting a beat after the last keystroke instead of searching per keystroke, retrying once when that machine could not be reached, dropping an answer a newer search has overtaken, and keeping the rows already shown while the next answer lands; it says which state it is in rather than letting an empty list stand in for a failure. An accepted mention is plain text in the draft and renders as a mono chip on the sent turn where it matched a real file; where two files in one message share a name each is drawn with the shortest tail of its path that tells them apart, in the search list and on the sent turn alike, and a row in that list inserts its mention and does nothing else — it opens no file and carries no second control. **The same `@` offers the live sessions beside the files**, each row the session's name and what it is doing, and picking one puts that session's `@name` in the draft: a draft that **begins** with a session's `@name` is addressed to that session, so sending it delivers the message there and it arrives as that session's own row rather than starting a turn on this one, while a session's `@name` anywhere later in a message to this session's own agent stays a mention and draws its chip on the code wash exactly as a file path does. Agent targeting stays a row in the command list and never an `@`.

  **The console's thirty-four words**, each meaning the same on both providers. Twenty-nine are shortcuts, where the typed word presses a control the console has — the same act as the click — and the text never reaches the provider; five are bridges, where no control exists and the daemon does the work on the provider's own verb. A caught spelling is matched and never drawn as a row.

  | Word | Caught spellings | What it does | Argument it takes |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `/help` |  | opens this list in place above the draft |  |
  | `/new` | `/clear`, `/reset` | a new session, never the provider's in-place reset |  |
  | `/rename` | `/name` | renames the session | the new name |
  | `/model` |  | the model control | a model the control offers |
  | `/effort` |  | the effort control | a rung the model offers |
  | `/fast` |  | the output-speed control |  |
  | `/plan` |  | the mode control |  |
  | `/level` | `/permissions`, `/allowed-tools` | the permission-level control | a level the control offers |
  | `/compact` |  | `Compact now` |  |
  | `/autocompact` |  | the compaction slider | `500k` moves it to the nearest percent of the model's window; `auto` returns it to the Settings default |
  | `/context` |  | the inspector's `Context` section |  |
  | `/usage` | `/cost`, `/stats` | the inspector's `Cost` section |  |
  | `/status` | `/pwd`, `/cwd` | the inspector's facts |  |
  | `/diff` |  | Review |  |
  | `/fork` | `/branch` | Fork | a name for the new session |
  | `/copy` |  | Copy on the latest reply | `2` copies the second-latest |
  | `/sidekicks` | `/agents`, `/subagents` | the agents pane |  |
  | `/skills` |  | opens this list in place above the draft on its skills section: the skills this session's provider can run, each with its origin mark, and none that only the other provider can run; a skill this session's provider failed to load stays grayed with its error; picking one inserts its call form ([Spec-029 §The call form](029-skills.md#the-call-form)) |  |
  | `/mcp` |  | the tool-servers control | `on <server>` or `off <server>` presses that row's switch, `enable` and `disable` caught as the same two verbs |
  | `/rewind` | `/checkpoint`, `/undo` | the rewind menu |  |
  | `/worktree` | `/cd` | the worktree switcher | a worktree's name; `/cd <path>` inside one of the project's worktrees presses that worktree, the repository root presses root |
  | `/account` |  | `Switch account for all sessions` | an account's name |
  | `/workflow` |  | starts a workflow run here, or pulls a run's results into this session | a workflow verb and its argument, as [Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md) lists them |
  | `/memory` | `/memories` | the inspector's `Memory` section |  |
  | `/pause-memory` | `/memory-pause`, `/toggle-memory` | the `Auto memory` switch, a toggle for the session |  |
  | `/hooks` |  | the inspector's `Hooks` section |  |
  | `/tasks` | `/bashes`, `/ps` | the tasks word and its list |  |
  | `/stop` | `/clean` | opens the running-commands list; `/stop all` presses its row that stops every command | `all` |
  | `/background` | `/bg` | `Move to background` on the row that carries it |  |
  | `/goal` |  | bridge: sets or clears the session's goal | a condition, or `clear` |
  | `/review` |  | bridge: the provider's own review | a target: the working tree, the staged set, or the branch against its base |
  | `/reload` | `/reload-skills`, `/reload-plugins` | bridge: reloads the provider's skills and plugins |  |
  | `/btw` | `/side` | bridge: a side question | the question |
  | `/voice` |  | bridge: voice in this session | `off`, `hold` or `tap`; `settings` on Codex picks the call's voice |

  A word that takes a name completes that name too: once the word and a space are typed, the list shows a second level offering exactly the names that word's own control offers, read from the same place the control reads them — the models, the levels, the saved workflows, the accounts, the worktrees, the tool servers, and `/review`'s three targets — filtering as it is typed, Tab and Enter completing one, and a word whose control offers nothing showing that control's own empty line rather than an empty list.

- **Interrupt and pause.** Interrupt is the working line's last word and Escape is its key once nothing else is open to close; the composer carries no stop control, send never becomes anything else, and there is no cancel-turn anywhere. It interrupts the lead and nothing under it: the turn ends, the command it was running is ended, its running agents are spared, every unfinished live timer in the transcript settles, and one system message lands. The one thing it reaches beyond this session is the session on the other end of a live exchange, whose turn ends with this one and whose queue is emptied, because a press on one side would otherwise leave the other side free to wake it again; that system message names what it reached, in both transcripts ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)). Anything pending goes at once as the next turn, and with nothing pending the state slot says the lead is interrupted and waiting. After the interrupt, while agents the session dispatched are still live, the word gives way to interrupt-everything, which stops every one of them at every depth from the daemon's own tree, one stop per identity with partial failure carried on the row it failed for and nothing atomic, and returns any still-pending text to the draft one line per message in send order. Pause sits before interrupt while a turn runs, on the session and in every child's view, and is a toggle with no key: the word turns amber, the state slot reads pausing while the step in flight finishes and paused once nothing runs, the clock freezes where it stands, and pressing the word again or sending anything continues from exactly there with nothing repeated. There is no pause-now, no resume word standing in pause's place and no pause row in the transcript; pending rows stay pending while paused and go as the continue; and interrupt stays available throughout.
- **The attachment row and send.** The attachment row is drawn only while something is staged, and holds one chip per staged thing with its own removal control: one chip per file the attach control picked, one chip for the marks a `browser` pane sent, and one chip for the review notes the person is holding. Attach opens the platform's own picker through `native.showOpenDialog` — files only, several at once, no type filter, never a folder — and staging writes nothing into the draft: the chip is the whole record of what will go with the message, and a folder reaches an agent only as a path typed or dropped in. Staging is bounded by what the daemon and the provider accept, and those figures are read from them rather than written on the screen: a file the session cannot take at all — its provider takes no attachments, or a card above the box is waiting — is refused in the same one-line refusal naming that cause, whichever way it was offered, the picker, a paste or a drop, so a chip never simply fails to appear; a file that does not fit is refused in the row's own one-line refusal naming that file and the limit it hit, and where several were picked at once the line says how many were taken — a file is never dropped in silence. A file still being copied says so on its chip and send waits for the copy, so a drop followed straight by Enter never loses it: the wait is invisible on a small file, past a short delay the chip's size slot reads `Copying…`, and a copy that fails takes its chip away and the row's one-line refusal reads `<name> was not attached · <cause>`, the cause the daemon's own, with `Try again` at its right, which copies that file again. Send is the one filled control on the card, at the right end of the draft line, sharing one diameter and one center line with the context ring below it; it is dimmed only while the message carries no text, no staged file and no held notes, and it carries no other state — a message that is only a file goes as it is, with no sentence written for it. The send key is the `Send message` row on Settings › Keyboard: while it holds Enter, its default, Enter sends and shift-Enter inserts a newline, and while it holds `⌘Enter`, Enter inserts a newline and `⌘Enter` sends. A message lands in the transcript the moment send is pressed, whether or not a turn is running, drawn as an outline with the time it was sent and settling in place the moment the daemon has it, so no row re-mounts under the reader; several stack in send order as the transcript's last rows while the agent's new rows are inserted above them. A send that fails takes its row back out of the transcript and puts everything it carried back into the composer — the words, the staged files and the held notes — with the caret where it was, and lands one system message naming the cause, `Send failed · Not connected`; where putting it back into the draft itself fails, that message says so too rather than reporting a hand-back that did not happen. From an empty draft the up and down arrows walk back through every message this session has sent, which the transcript already holds, and forward again, returning whatever was typed before the walk; they recall only from an empty draft, so they never collide with moving the caret through text, and they yield to a question card while one is open. `⌃R` in the message box searches the same messages: what is typed after it narrows to the newest sent message containing it, `⌃R` again steps to the next older match, Enter or `→` puts the match in the draft, and Escape gives the draft back as it was; recall and search fill the draft and send nothing. `⌃R` is the `Search sent messages` row on Settings › Keyboard, whose line reads `Searches the messages sent in the open session, from the message box.`, and in the terminal the key stays the shell's.
- **The toolbar's controls.** Six value controls, drawn on every session: the model control, reading the model with its context window on its own rows — the chip itself naming a window only where the larger-window row of a model is chosen, `<model> · <window>`, and the name alone otherwise — and opening **both providers' models under two headings**, `Claude Code` and `Codex`, so picking a model under the other provider's heading is how a session changes provider — the control keeps reading the model, the working line's state slot reads `switching to Codex` or `switching to Claude` and nothing longer, picking again replaces the pick and picking the current provider's model back cancels it, and the switch itself applies when the run ends, landing two faint collapsed rows in the transcript at that point, `Hand-over brief · Claude Code` and `Switched to Codex · continued from a brief · 3 things did not carry over` — and the plain transcript file the daemon writes beside the brief for the new provider to read on demand is not drawn at all ([Spec-014 §Same-Agent Provider Switch](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#same-agent-provider-switch)); the effort control, reading the reasoning effort as one word and opening the one ladder — `Low`, `Medium`, `High`, `Extra high`, `Max`, `Ultra` — with the rungs the model lacks absent, so a model offers exactly the rungs it publishes, each rung mapped one to one onto the provider's own name (`low`, `medium`, `high`, `xhigh`, `max`, `ultra`) and applied from the next turn; the output-speed control, reading fast or standard; the mode control, which swaps its glyph with its word between build and plan; the permission-level control, reading the session's level and opening the five levels in one fixed order from the most careful to the least — read-only, which reads on its own and asks before any change; ask, which edits files on its own and asks before running commands; reviewed, where the provider's own reviewer approves routine work, blocks the rest and tells the agent why, and a block can be allowed once; sandboxed, which never asks, writes only inside the worktree and the repository's git folder, whose hooks and config stay closed; and YOLO, which never asks and has no limits at all — with a one-line note on each; and the tool-servers control, reading how many of the session's tool servers are switched on and opening them as a grouped list with a session-level switch on every row under one link into the MCP servers settings page; this list is the only place in a session that lists its tool servers and their states, and the command list names a server only as the heading over the prompts it publishes — grouped rather than marked, the working servers first under their names alone, then a faint rule, then the ones that are not working, dimmed, each carrying one short faint word — `Needs sign-in` or `Failed`, from the same inventory that settings page reads — with its whole reason in that word's hover title and the failed ones last, a server still starting sitting with the working ones until it says otherwise. A row's one other act, `Attach a resource…`, lists what that server publishes and stages the chosen one in the attachment row; no row carries a reconnect or a sign-in control. On Codex a resource read rides Codex's own connection, and the service fronts each server for Codex's long calls, so prompts, resources and long calls work the same on both providers. A switch turned off stops the session offering that server's tools at all, so none of its definitions reach the model, it applies at the next turn and one flipped mid-turn says so in one faint line on its own row rather than changing anything under the run, and settings, every other session and every session started later are untouched. A session with no tool servers still draws the control, its list holding one line saying it has none above that same link. Every one of them reads its value as a word and never carries a pressed tint, no value chip ever looks selected beside its neighbors, and each opens its list in place above the chip, hung off the dock rather than inside the card, which clips its overflow — a list that is present but half clipped is a defect. A control whose value differs from what the session started on carries one small mark saying so and loses it the moment the value matches again; that mark is the only sign of a departure. The model control's list says which of four states it is in: skeleton rows while the provider's catalog is being read, the rows once it is, one line naming the failure with `Try again` in the one try-again shape when the read failed, `Try again` reading the catalog afresh rather than serving what failed, and, where the provider offers nothing, one line saying so above `Manage in Settings`, which opens Settings › Providers. A session already running on a model the catalog no longer offers keeps that model: the control reads its name with `no longer offered` after it, its rows list the current catalog, and the turn still sends. The two headings follow the provider list live ([Spec-025 §Interfaces And Contracts](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#interfaces-and-contracts), `provider.list`), so a provider whose `Available for new sessions` switch flips in Settings › Providers changes the rows every open composer offers at once, with no restart. A new session opens the model and effort controls on the last model and effort the person used, and the level control at `YOLO` however the last session was left; with remembering off, or on the first session ever, it starts on the first usable signed-in account's provider and that provider's default model. Nothing else carries over — not the mode, the speed or the compaction bound. The level list never shows a provider's own mode name and never shows plan, which is the mode control's, and a level the session's provider, account or model cannot run is absent rather than grayed. Beside the six, the right-hand cluster holds the attach control and then the context ring at the far right: one figure and one meter, gray on gray, never colored, with a faint notch on its arc at the compaction bound, opening the compaction popover in place above it: `Compact now`, then the slider reading `Compact automatically at <B>% · of <window>`, its range derived for this session from the provider's live figures — the top stop at the provider's own compaction point on this window (Codex's at 90 %), the bottom stop at the session's fixed start plus a tenth of the window, rounded up — and a Settings default outside that range reading at the nearer stop, unmarked. The figures no request reports are read from the installed provider, never typed in, and read again when the installed version changes; none is called provider-reported. Before a Codex session's first usage report the slider is inactive under `The range opens after the first reply, once Codex reports what this session loads.`, its bottom stop then counted from that turn's input, and `/autocompact` refuses a value outside the stops. The `Context compacted` row opens to the summary the provider kept, in the provider's own words, in a capped panel that scrolls inside itself; a compaction whose provider kept no summary stays a plain row with nothing to open. A compaction the provider attempts and fails lands no row, and one the person asked for that did not run says why on the one line beside the message box, cleared by the next keystroke, while the working line goes back to its verb; a turn too long even after compaction comes back as a failed send reading `Send failed · Too long for this session's context window`. Nothing on this screen estimates what a draft will cost — the ring's figure is the one the provider reported, and no local count of the text being typed is drawn. Once the card is measured too narrow to hold them — the chips' own width at the current text size against what is left beside the ring and send, measured and never a figure — every value chip gives way to one overflow chip whose menu carries a row per control reading that control's name and its current value, and picking a row opens that control's own list exactly as its chip would; the right-hand cluster never folds, nothing else in the card reflows, and the chips are back the moment they fit again. The composer carries no standing attachment tray, no emoji picker, no formatting toolbar, no clear control, no colored context ring, no abort control, no second send variant, no send-later or schedule, and no count of the draft; it proposes no text of its own, and the draft is the platform's own text field, so the operating system's dictation, spelling, substitutions and input methods work in it. The console's own voice carries no button on the card: it is the `/voice` command and the Space key (the voice bullet below).
- **The ask rail** rides the transcript's own right edge inside its scroller and is pointer-only: invisible at rest, fading in once the pointer has rested inside the rail's own edge zone and out again a moment after it leaves, hidden from assistive technology, carrying no focusable control, not drawn while the session holds fewer than two asks, and not drawn at all while the transcript column is at its floor, so a narrow window or a pane taking the width leaves the transcript its whole measure. It carries one hairline tick per message the person sent and none for an agent turn or a tool row; the tick of the ask nearest the middle of the visible band is filled in the accent; hovering a tick shows a one-line label with the first words of that ask, and pressing it scrolls the transcript there. There is no preview card, no widening and no jump arrow, and two chords, `Previous ask` and `Next ask` on Settings › Keyboard, give the keyboard the same two jumps without adding anything to the screen, each landing the reader on that message and announcing it.
- **The jump control** sits above the card inside the dock, riding the card's height, and is drawn only while the transcript's follow is released; nothing is drawn over the transcript for it and nothing else moves. One press lands on the newest row and pins the follow again, scrolling to the tail pins it too, and a session opens pinned with nothing drawn.
- **Voice.** `/voice` turns voice on in the session it is typed in, and `/voice` again or `/voice off` turns it off; `/voice hold` and `/voice tap` pick the mode, one mode for both providers kept in the machine's settings file as `voice.mode` (`hold` or `tap`, default `hold`), beside the call voice, `voice.callVoice` (the voice a spoken call answers in; only Codex's call speaks today, and `voice.voiceList` gives the choices; missing reads as Codex's `defaultV1`, picked with `/voice settings`, which lists in the command list the voices `voice.voiceList` returns), both read and written through `machineSettings.read()` and `machineSettings.write(change)`, and voice is on in one session at a time on this computer, the service holding which, so every window and device showing that session shows the same. While it is on the session strip reads `Voice on`, and `Listening…`, beside a mark that swells with the microphone's level, takes its place while the microphone is open; the empty draft's placeholder shows the mode, `Hold Space to talk` or `Tap Space to talk`, and reads `Tap Space to stop` while a tap has the microphone open. Only that session's Space talks, and nothing is recorded until Space is held or tapped. Voice waits for the person's answer to the system microphone prompt however long it takes. On Claude Code a recording is dictation: the window captures the microphone with echo cancellation, noise suppression and gain control, cuts it to 16 kHz 16-bit mono, draws its level and hands the frames to the daemon, whose own socket to the speech service returns the words at the caret, dimmed until they settle (`voice.dictationStart`, `voice.dictationWrite`, `voice.dictationStop`, `voice.dictationSubscribe`). On Codex it is Codex's own spoken call with the window as its WebRTC peer: the capture runs through an AudioWorklet into a media-stream destination whose track the call carries, which holds what is said while the call connects, and the call's offer carries the `oai-events` data channel the window reads `turn.created` from, so a new answer silences the rest of an old one (`voice.callStart`, `voice.callStop`, `voice.callSubscribe`, `voice.voiceList`). A hold's request plays into the call at 1.5× with its pitch kept ([Desktop App Implementation Notes §Console Libraries](../architecture/desktop-implementation-notes.md#console-libraries), the voice playback row). A Codex hold keeps at most two minutes of speech and then passes the request in. The mark beside `Listening…` swells with one level read every 50 milliseconds from the samples' root mean square in decibels, `x = clamp((dB + 70) / 60, 0, 1)`, drawn at `min(1, 1.4 · (max(0, x − 0.2) / 0.8)^0.7)`, the same on both providers. Escape while recording drops the recording, puts the draft back as it was before the recording began and interrupts nothing, and voice's refusals are the strip's own one-line refusal. The rows voice lands, the tap that sends, when a recording stops and the six refusals with their triggers are [Spec-004 §Voice](./004-provider-driver-contract-and-capabilities.md#voice)'s.
- Owning plan: Plan-020
- Note: chrome, not a V1 Signature Feature — it is the composition point the Signature Feature views mount their composer-area affordances into.

### Approvals View (→ Plan-009 Approvals, Permissions, Trust Boundaries)

- Data sources: the daemon's approval subscription and its approval projection, both read through the bridge; the rules in force on the session, read from where the providers keep them. What an approval is, when one is raised, and how a rule is scoped and kept are [Spec-010 §Required Behavior](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#required-behavior)'s and [Spec-010 §Default Behavior](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#default-behavior)'s; this view is the one surface that draws them, and it re-derives no rule of its own.
- **One card, in the composer.** A waiting request renders inside the composer above the draft, so a person answers without leaving the transcript. It is never a modal, a toast, a free-standing transcript card or a pane of its own: there is no approvals pane and no approval history. The one list of approval state is the inspector's `Rules` section, one row per approval rule in force on the session, the session's own and the project's alike, each reading what it does, its subject and its scope — `Allow pnpm test · this session`, `Block api.example.com · this project` — with `Revoke` confirming in place (`approval.ruleList`, `approval.ruleRevoke`); a session with none reads `No rules yet`. Every standing rule on the machine, in every account home and attached project, is Settings › Providers' list (`provider.standingRuleList`, `provider.standingRuleRevoke`), read from the same files. Exactly one is shown at a time and a queue is worked one item at a time: with more than one waiting the card reads its place in the queue beside its eyebrow — `1 of 3` — and with one waiting it carries no count at all.
- **What the card holds.** A two-pixel accent inset top rule rather than a fill; an eyebrow reading approval; the title over its summary line; one reason line under the summary in the provider's own words saying why this particular act stopped, stripped of control characters and terminal escapes before it is drawn and absent rather than empty where the provider sent none; and, in a fixed-width block, the exact thing it is asking about, whole — the command for a command, the path for a read or a write, the text for an input — wrapping rather than cutting and scrolling inside itself past a height derived from the card, which pushes the transcript up rather than covering it and keeps its actions in reach. That one line is editable in place before the card is answered, in its own type, and the changed text is what goes back with the answer and what the row then records as having run; there is no raw form of the call, and the card offers only the three actions below.
- **Three actions, in one fixed order:** decline, then a standing allow for this session on the subject the daemon derived from the ask — a command's program and first subcommand, a network request's host, a written file's name — then approve once, the hero last. The row is compact: the eyebrow and the command share one line, the context sits a quarter-line under them, and the three buttons sit beside them at the app's button height. The middle action reads `Always allow <subject> this session` and, on a project session, carries an arrow offering `Always in this project` (a chat has no project and no arrow); either scope hands the rule to the provider, which keeps it — a project rule in the provider's own project file, Claude Code's `.claude/settings.local.json` or Codex's `.codex/rules/sidekicks.rules` kept out of commits by `.git/info/exclude` — and the daemon keeps no rule store. A later ask on that subject runs with no card at every level that asks. The middle action is absent rather than grayed on an ask the provider marks as one that must not carry a standing allow, and nothing takes its place. An ask to send input to a command that is already running reads `Send input to <the running command>`, naming the command as its row does, with `Terminal input in the <worktree> worktree` under it and the input whole in the fixed-width block, labeled `Terminal input`; it takes no standing allow, so the card draws `Decline` and `Approve once` alone, a decline telling the agent so while the command keeps running, and on Codex that input is not editable, because Codex takes no changed input back. The person's own typing into a terminal never raises this card. On a network ask decline carries an arrow offering `Block <host> this session` and, as the project scope, `Block <host> in this project`, so the rule set is allow and block rather than allow alone, and an allow and a block on one subject cannot both stand. Pressing decline opens one optional line under the actions for why not, which goes back to the agent through the provider's own refusal field and sends a bare decline when it is left empty. The two plain answers are also command palette rows, `Approve once · <subject>` and `Decline · <subject>`, `<subject>` being the words the always-allow answer names; a row never shows a request's id, whether one ask waits or several, and answers exactly as the card's own button does, with no rule.
- **The card exists only at the three levels that ask.** The two that answer for themselves never raise one: moving the level to one of them answers an open card and its blocked row runs, a later ask raises nothing, and moving back to a level that asks asks again.
- **It waits until it is answered.** The daemon holds the request open with no timer, so the card carries no countdown, no expiry and no expired state. It is rebuilt on reload and on every device the person has linked, the first answer settles it everywhere, and a device that was showing it closes it with a line saying it was answered elsewhere rather than with anything that reads as an error. The closers are the answer itself, an interrupt of the run — which cancels the open request and records the cancellation — a move to a level that never asks, and the provider process ending. An agent's own question and a plan waiting for a verdict take the same place on the same terms.
- **Answering.** Approving resolves the call in place: the card hides, the blocked timer stops waiting and keeps running, the working line becomes the running verb, the result lands when it settles, and the session's review data is marked stale — there is no approved banner and no extra row. Declining resolves in place the same way, the blocked call's row reading declined where its result would sit, and the session leaves the attention list. An approval a child raised is the parent's approval, answered in the parent's composer through one approval pipeline and one trust boundary; the child's own inline card answers by pressing the composer's matching action by index, so there is still exactly one answer surface, and walking to an approval from a child scrolls it into view and tints it faintly in the accent for a moment while the pane renders no second approval control. The card takes focus when it arrives while the draft has focus, landing on approve once, and that focus move never scrolls the transcript; its actions are walked with the left and right arrows and pressed with Enter, the walk stopping at each end. No single key answers it: there is no per-action keyboard accelerator, no approve-all, no per-approval notification and no cancel-turn action.
- Owning plan: Plan-009

### Runs View (→ Plan-002 Queue, Steer, Pause, Resume)

- Data sources: the daemon's run-state and queue subscriptions ([Spec-003 §Interfaces And Contracts](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#interfaces-and-contracts)). A run's state, its queue and its intervention history are the daemon's facts; the renderer derives none of them.
- **There is no runs pane.** A run is controlled where it is watched. Pause and interrupt are the last two pressable words of the working line at the head of the composer card — pause a toggle with no key, interrupt answering Escape once nothing else is open to close — and after the interrupt, while agents the session dispatched are still live, interrupt gives way to interrupt-everything. The composer carries no stop control, send never becomes anything else, and there is no cancel-turn anywhere in the console.
- **What is waiting is the transcript's own last rows.** A message sent while a turn runs lands at once as the person's own row, drawn as an outline with the time it was sent, and stays there until the agent takes it at its next step, when it settles in place without re-mounting; several stack in send order as the transcript's last rows while the agent's new rows are inserted above them. On hover or keyboard focus such a row offers four acts, the same on both providers: a grip that drags it, or the arrows with the row focused, to move it within that stack (`run.queueReorder`, the full new order, refused unless it names exactly the messages still waiting); `Edit`, which puts its text back in the draft and leaves `Editing…` in the row so its place is held, sends back into that place on Enter as one call that replaces the waiting message, its old entry becoming superseded (`run.queueCreate` with `replacesQueueItemId`, refused once the agent has taken the message), and restores the old text on Escape; `Remove`, after which nothing reaches the agent (`run.queueCancel`); and `Send now`, which interrupts the turn, delivers that row first and lets the rest follow in order as the next turn's messages. Moving a row here changes the send order of messages the person has already sent; it is not a priority override on the daemon's admission queue, whose only removal path stays cancel-before-admission.
- Interactions: pause and its continue on an active run (`run.pause`, `run.resume`) and the interrupt (`run.intervene`); a steer is a message sent while the turn runs; a child's own controls are `run.childSteer`, `run.childInterrupt` and `run.childPauseSet {paused}`, and `run.childrenStop` stops every child at every depth; enqueue and cancel-before-admission on the queue. All are forwarded to the daemon's run engine per the run-state domain model ([Spec-003 §Interfaces And Contracts](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#interfaces-and-contracts)), and the pencil's resend and `Undo to here` on a sent message are the daemon's undo (`session.restore`), not a run control of their own (§Transcript View).
- Owning plan: Plan-002

### Agents Pane View (→ Plan-013 Multi-Agent Orchestration)

- Data sources: the session's agents and their child-run links through `bridge.daemon.call(…)` — `agent.list`, `orchestration.childRunLinkRead`, and `orchestration.budgetRead` per agent, drawn only for a budget the person has set, so with none set no budget row is drawn — and each child's own flow from the same transcript reads the Transcript View uses, per the Plan-013 method-string registry (orchestration-payloads.md §Plan-013). A child run is started by the daemon's own paths (`orchestration.runCreate`) and never from a control here; run-level stop-condition outcomes render in the transcript, not here
- Controls: a child's pause and interrupt are `run.childPauseSet {paused}` and `run.childInterrupt`, its steer box sends through `run.childSteer`, and the list's stop-all is `run.childrenStop`, which stops every child at every depth from the daemon's own tree, one stop per identity; each is one wire mutation per explicit act
